# Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 

Tejas Pradeep Shirodkar ∗

IIIT, Hyderabad 

Abstract 

Singular learning theory and information geometry have studied the same parameter spaces in mostly separate vocabularies: the former computes Bayesian invariants in resolved coordinates, the latter works in original coordinates under a non-degeneracy assumption that overparameterised models routinely violate. We bridge them through one primitive, the dead direction : a unit vector along which the Fisher metric degenerates, equivalently a tangent to the analytic singular set with a definite KL order, set by how fast the KL divergence vanishes. The two readings name the same vector; our central move shows its KL order is recoverable as the decay rate of the directional Fisher curvature approaching the singularity, in original parameter coordinates and without a Hironaka resolution. A selection rule on smooth fibres translates this rate into Watanabe’s single-direction contribution to the real log canonical threshold, and we extend the recovery to multi-component crossings, multiplicity 𝑚 , the singular fluctuation 𝜈 (universal in the KL order for 1D directions), prior-RLCT shifts, and tempered posteriors. We then lift this rate to a deep network: a multi-layer K-FAC factorisation writes each Fisher block as a product of activation- and gradient-side rates with a duality between them, instantiated at modern-network primitives (residual streams, layer normalisation, attention). A quotient theorem carries the rate to the gauge quotient Θ/𝐺 under gradient flow on a 𝐺 -invariant metric; SGD qualifies, standard Adam does not, and we construct a 𝐺 -equivariant Adam-family preconditioner ( DDCAdam ) that does. The bridge yields a parameter-coordinate handle on singular geometry, closed-form per-architecture predictions, and a trajectory-rate readout of Watanabe’s triple (𝜆, 𝑚, 𝜈 ) from one checkpoint’s forward and backward passes, without posterior sampling.

# Part I Foundations and the bridging primitive 

# 1 Introduction 

A trained neural network is a single point in a high-dimensional parameter space : one coordinate per weight, with training tracing a path to a setting that fits the data. In small classical models this endpoint is isolated, and perturbing the weights in any direction degrades the fit. Overparameterised networks behave differently. With far more parameters than the data constrains, the settings that fit equally well form continuous families rather than isolated points. Along some directions the loss does not move at all; along others it changes only at high order. The local geometry at the solution is degenerate , a singularity of the parameter space, and that degeneracy is informative: it reflects how much of the network’s capacity the task uses and which directions the learnt function ignores. The natural instrument for this geometry is the Fisher information metric 

𝐹 (𝜃 ) = E𝑥 ∼𝑝 ∗ [𝜕 𝜃 log 𝑝 𝜃 (𝑥 ) 𝜕 𝜃 log 𝑝 𝜃 (𝑥 )⊤], which measures how sharply a model’s pre-dictions respond as the parameters move. A direction in which 𝐹 is large is tightly constrained by the data; a direction in which 𝐹 degenerates to zero is left free. The exactly-fitting parameters Σ𝑇 = {𝜃 : 𝑝 𝜃 = 𝑝 ∗} form the singular set , and the Fisher metric loses rank along it. Two communities have spent two decades studying this parameter space, in mostly separate vocabularies. Information geometry, following Amari (2016), treats a parametric family 

{ 𝑝 𝜃 : 𝜃 ∈ Θ} as a Riemannian manifold under the Fisher metric. Natural gradient, the dual (∇ , ∇∗) connection structure, and the exponential / mixture flatness duality are its central constructions, and all require the metric to be non-singular. Singular learning theory, following Watanabe (2009), addresses the opposite case: a non-identifiable model whose parameter space contains an analytic singular set rather than an isolated optimum. Watanabe’s framework computes invariants of Σ𝑇 through Hironaka’s resolution of singularities : a change of coordinates that untangles the singular set into simple products of crossings, a blow-up . In those resolved coordinates the KL divergence takes a normal-crossing form, and one reads off the real log canonical threshold 𝜆 , the asymptotic invariant governing the leading correction to the Bayesian free energy. 1

Both frameworks describe the same parameter space. Neither, on its own, answers the practitioner’s question. Information geometry presupposes the metric is well-defined and so is silent about the singular set. Watanabe’s 𝜆 is integrated over the asymptotic posterior and lives in resolved coordinates that one would have to compute by performing a Hironaka blow-up of the loss landscape, a non-trivial task on a network with millions of parameters. The gap is operational: information about the singular structure exists in Watanabe’s framework, but not in coordinates the practitioner has.   

> 1𝜆 takes rational values, a deep fact of algebraic geometry derived from Hironaka (1964)’s resolution; the resolution is guaranteed to exist but the construction is not algorithmic for typical neural-network loss landscapes.

Figure 1: The dead direction bridges the two traditions: the same unit vector is Amari’s kernel-approaching direction of the Fisher metric 𝐹 and Watanabe’s tangent to the singular set Σ𝑇 , with KL order 𝑘 the shared invariant. The trajectory Fisher-rate exponent 𝛼 = 2(𝑘 − 1),read in original parameter coordinates, recovers Watanabe’s local RLCT 𝜆 = 1/( 2𝑘 ) without a Hironaka resolution. This paper closes one face of that gap with a single primitive. Write 𝜃 (𝑡 ) for a path that approaches the singular set as the scalar 𝑡 → 0. A dead direction is a unit vector 𝑢 (a direction in parameter space) along which the Fisher quadratic form 𝑢 ⊤ 𝐹 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) 𝑢 , the metric’s measure of curvature in direction 𝑢 , decays to zero on that approach. From Amari’s vantage, it is the direction in which the foundational object of his framework loses non-degeneracy. From Watanabe’s, it is the original-coordinate shadow of a normal-crossing exponent: tangent to the smooth strata of Σ𝑇 , with KL order 𝑘 ≥ 1 set by how fast the KL divergence vanishes on approach, 𝐾 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) = 𝑐 𝑡 2𝑘 + 𝑂 (𝑡 2𝑘 +1). The divergence has a zero of order 2𝑘 in 𝑡 , twice the KL order. The two readings refer to the same vector. We use the KL order, accessible in either framework, as the bridge invariant (Figure 1). 

The rate primitive. Along a dead direction with KL order 𝑘 , the directional Fisher quadratic form satisfies 𝑢 ⊤ 𝐹 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) 𝑢 = Θ(𝑡 2(𝑘 −1) ) (Theorem 2). The smallest Fisher eigenvalue decays with a slope that reads off 𝑘 : slope 0 at the regular case 𝑘 = 1, slope 2 at 𝑘 = 2, slope 4 at  

> 𝑘

= 3 (Figure 2(a)). The proof is a score expansion in 𝐿 2 ( 𝑝 ∗) with a Schur-complement bound on the non-degenerate Fisher block in adapted coordinates. The exponent reads in original parameter coordinates, with no Hironaka resolution required. It also identifies algebraically with Amari’s degenerate direction, so the KL order is one invariant that both traditions can compute. The rest of the paper accounts for how this one exponent reorganises across a deep network: factorising over layers (Figure 2(b)) and descending to the gauge quotient under loss symmetries (Figure 2(c)). Figure 2: Three views of the dead-directions framework. (a) The rate primitive: along a dead direction with KL order 𝑘 , the smallest Fisher eigenvalue decays as 𝜆 min (𝐹 ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝑘 −1) ); the slope reads off 𝑘 , recovering Watanabe’s RLCT contribution 1/( 2𝑘 ) from a single trajectory in original coordinates. (b) The K-FAC ladder at depth 𝐿 = 4: the same exponent factorises across layers as 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ), decreasing toward the input; the dual activation factor 𝜆 min ( 𝐴 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(ℓ−1) ) makes the product layer-independent at Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −1) ). (c) The gauge quotient: under a continuous loss symmetry, the rate descends to Θ/𝐺 ; SGD on a 

𝐺 -invariant metric realises it as gradient flow, while Adam’s per-coordinate preconditioner is not equivariant and the trajectory leaves the orbit foliation. 

Watanabe-side organisation. On the SLT side, the rate corresponds to one of Watanabe’s invariants. A selection rule for smooth singular fibres (Theorem 3) recovers the single-direction contribution 𝜆 = 1/( 2𝑘 ) to the local RLCT: the Fisher slope, measured in original coordinates, is Watanabe’s invariant. A Fisher–curvature–volume rate chain (Section 6) ties the same 𝑘 to three jointly-determined geometric exponents. 

Architecture-side organisation. On the deep-learning side, the same exponent factorises across the layers of a network. A multi-layer K-FAC bridge (K-FAC: Kronecker-Factored Approximate Curvature, Martens and Grosse, 2015; the layer-wise factorisation of the Fisher into an activation factor 𝐴 ℓ and a gradient factor 𝐺 ℓ) gives a per-layer rate ladder 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) =

Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ) (Theorem 21), with a forward–backward duality that makes the product layer-independent (Corollary 25). Section 7 then instantiates the bridge at the architectural primitives of modern networks: rectangular widths, residual stacks, normalisation layers, and attention, each with closed-form rates derived from the primitive’s algebra. 

Trajectory-readability. On the optimiser side, the rate descends to the gauge quotient. For any continuous Lie group symmetry 𝐺 of the loss, the trajectory rate is well-defined on the quotient Θ/𝐺 (Corollary 78). Projected SGD on a 𝐺 -invariant metric realises that rate, as gradient flow on the quotient through a Riemannian submersion (Corollary 79). Adam behaves differently: its per-coordinate 1/√ˆ𝑣 preconditioner is not 𝐺 -equivariant, so the trajectory-rate predictions do not transfer to Adam-class dynamics in closed form (Remark 80). A closed-form per-trajectory rate on the alignment-rotated manifold under such non-equivariant preconditioners remains open analytical work. We take a constructive route: we build an Adam-family preconditioner whose update map is 𝐺 -equivariant by design (Algorithm 2, Corollary 86), the Adam instance of a broader dead-direction conditioner (DDC) family of Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 5

equivariant preconditioners. The trajectory-rate predictions therefore apply to canonical-aligned SGD on a 𝐺 -invariant metric and to the equivariant preconditioner family. The architecture-side and norm-side organisation above does not depend on this optimiser scope. Section 13 carries the bridge into Watanabe’s broader inventory: multi-component normal crossings, the multiplicity 𝑚 , the singular fluctuation 𝜈 as a universal function of the KL order for 1D dead directions, prior-induced RLCT shifts, and tempered posteriors at 𝛽 ≠ 1. The genuinely open agenda (Hironaka resolution at 𝑟 > 3, non-i.i.d. data, a trajectory-rate analog of 𝜈 in original coordinates, theoretical 𝛽 -sweep of (𝜆, 𝑚 ), Amari-side dual-connections work, and a closed-form rate modifier for standard non-equivariant Adam) is collected in Section 14. None is, as far as we can see, structurally blocked. 

Reading guide. The three contributions carry different reach, and the paper keeps the levels separate. Section 12 sorts every load-bearing result into one of three tiers (Table 6): a universal tier that holds for any analytic model with smooth singular fibres, an architecture-specific tier that requires the layered K-FAC structure, and a trajectory-readability tier that requires a gauge-equivariant optimiser. The rate-to-RLCT recovery itself is exhibited numerically in Appendix B, where measured Fisher slopes return the predicted 𝜆 = 1/( 2𝑘 ) on three controlled families. A reader who wants only the central result can take the rate primitive below, the selection rule of Section 5, and Figure 2(a); the deep-network and optimiser machinery builds on that one exponent. 

Notation and conventions. Throughout, { 𝑝 𝜃 } is a smooth parametric family on a parameter space Θ ⊆ R𝑑 . For a fixed task 𝑇 , 𝑝 ∗ denotes the data-generating distribution, Σ𝑇 = {𝜃 : 𝑝 𝜃 =

> 𝑝 ∗

} the analytic singular set, 𝐾 (𝜃 ) := KL ( 𝑝 ∗ ∥ 𝑝 𝜃 ) the KL divergence, and 𝐹 (𝜃 ) the Fisher information matrix at 𝜃 . A path 𝜃 (𝑡 ) approaches a singular minimum 𝜃 0 ∈ Σ𝑇 as 𝑡 → 0. We write 𝑓 (𝑡 ) = Θ(𝑔 (𝑡 )) for 𝑐 1 𝑔 (𝑡 ) ≤ 𝑓 (𝑡 ) ≤ 𝑐 2 𝑔 (𝑡 ) on a punctured neighbourhood of 0, with 

0 < 𝑐 1 ≤ 𝑐 2.

# 2 Background: two traditions and their invariants 

Section 1 named the two traditions and the gap between them. This section sets out the appa-ratus each provides: the metric structure information geometry assumes, and the resolution-of-singularities machinery singular learning theory uses to compute invariants where that structure fails. 

Information geometry. Information geometry (Amari, 2016) treats a parametric family 

{ 𝑝 𝜃 : 𝜃 ∈ Θ} as a Riemannian manifold under the Fisher information metric, whose (𝑖, 𝑗 )

entry is  

> 𝐹

(𝜃 )𝑖 𝑗 = E𝑥 ∼𝑝 ∗

 𝜕 log 𝑝 𝜃 (𝑥 ) 

> 𝜕𝜃 𝑖
> 𝜕

log 𝑝 𝜃 (𝑥 )

> 𝜕𝜃 𝑗



> .

Together with the dual (∇ , ∇∗) connection structure, the metric supports natural gradient as steepest descent in the Kullback–Leibler divergence and the exponential / mixture flatness duality of standard families. The apparatus is complete and actionable when the family is regular: 𝐹 non-singular, the model identifiable, and the parameter space a clean Riemannian manifold. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 6

Singular learning theory. Singular learning theory (Watanabe, 2009) addresses the regime where these conditions fail. Many models of practical interest (mixture models, hidden Markov models, deep neural networks) are non-identifiable: distinct parameter values map to the same distribution (Watanabe, 2007), and overparametrised networks sit squarely in this regime (Wei et al., 2023). At points where the model captures the data-generating distribution, the Fisher matrix is singular along the analytic set Σ𝑇 = {𝜃 : 𝑝 𝜃 = 𝑝 ∗}.Watanabe’s framework computes invariants of Σ𝑇 via resolution of singularities. A sequence of analytic blow-ups (Hironaka’s theorem) recasts the KL divergence in resolved coordinates as a normal-crossing form 𝐾 = 𝑢 (𝑔 ) Î𝑖 𝑔 2𝑘 𝑖  

> 𝑖

, with normal-crossing exponents (𝑘 1, . . . , 𝑘 𝑑 ) paired with Jacobian exponents (ℎ1, . . . , ℎ 𝑑 ) from the blow-up. The real log canonical threshold is  

> 𝜆

= min 𝑖 (ℎ𝑖 +1)/( 2𝑘 𝑖 ), an integrated invariant of the resolved structure. It governs the leading-order correction to the Bayesian free energy: 𝐹 𝑛 = 𝑛𝐿 𝑛 + 𝜆 log 𝑛 − ( 𝑚 − 1) log log 𝑛 + 𝑂 𝑝 (1),where 𝑚 is the multiplicity of the minimum. With the singular fluctuation 𝜈 , 𝜆 also determines the asymptotic generalisation error via Watanabe’s information criteria. 

The shared geometry. The two apparatus describe the same geometry (Figure 1): the Fisher metric is the Hessian of 𝐾 at 𝜃 ∗ when 𝐾 is twice-differentiable, and information geometry’s metric-degenerate locus is Watanabe’s singular set Σ𝑇 . The remainder of the paper makes the KL order trajectory-readable in original parameter coordinates, then lifts that reading from a single direction to a deep network’s layered Fisher spectrum and to the gauge quotient both traditions already encode. 

Relationship to the local learning coefficient line. Closest in spirit to the construction below is the local learning coefficient (LLC) programme of Lau et al. (2025), which makes Watanabe’s 𝜆 pointwise by estimating it numerically via SGLD-based posterior sampling around a checkpoint. The LLC programme has since branched into a refined-LLC variant (Wang et al., 2024) that estimates 𝜆 restricted to a chosen weight subset or data subset, yielding a per-module / per-data complexity number, and a stagewise-development reading (Hoogland et al., 2024) that tracks LLC change-points across training checkpoints. The trajectory-rate framework of this paper is a deterministic alternative to the same family of observables: it exposes Watanabe’s 𝜆 as a rate exponent on the Fisher metric along an approach (Theorem 2), computable from forward and backward passes without posterior sampling, at the cost of requiring canonical alignment of the dead direction across layers. The trade-off is direct. The LLC family delivers a numerical Bayesian-complexity number per module and per checkpoint via SGLD; the trajectory-rate framework delivers a deterministic rate exponent per K-FAC layer (Theorem 21), a residual-stream 𝜎 min readout (Corollary 58), an algebraic LN-kernel direction (Proposition 63), and the rest of the architectural roster of §7. Theorem 3 is the formal statement of where the two converge: on the smooth-fibre, single-component slice with Jacobian exponent set to zero. Section 13 carries the recovery further, into the multi-component case where the singular set is several smooth sheets meeting transversally (a normal crossing) and each sheet contributes its own KL order; there the bridge recovers the per-component orders and Watanabe’s (𝜆, 𝑚 ) pair. The LLC keeps coverage the rate readout lacks at singularities that resist a normal-crossing description or whose component normals are not identifiable, where its posterior sampling still returns an aggregate 𝜆 . We read the two programmes as complementary within a shared singular-learning agenda. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 7

Parallel programs in the SLT-DL landscape. Several other lines operationalise different aspects of singular structure in deep networks, and a single paragraph cannot do them justice; we name each and indicate the relationship to the trajectory-rate framework here, with the detailed forward-look in §15. Susceptibilities (Baker et al., 2025; Gordon et al., 2026) probe how the loss responds to localised perturbations of a trained network’s behaviour, treating the loss landscape as a thermodynamic medium and clustering directions by response signature. 

The loss kernel (Adam et al., 2025) is a geometric probe of model internals (the covariance of per-sample losses under low-loss-preserving perturbations), extended to higher-order-degeneracy-sensitive data attribution by the Hessian-free Bayesian influence functions of Kreer et al. (2025); Lee et al. (2025). Compressibility and minimum description length (Urdshals et al., 2025) formalise the bit-length of a singular minimum, recovering classical MDL in the regular case and refining it in the degenerate one. Programs as singularities (Murfet and Troiani, 2025) connects degenerate statistical models to the geometry of programs. Modes of sequence models (Chen and Murfet, 2025) characterises the sensitivity of LLC estimation to input-distribution patterns. Each line probes a different slice of the singular geometry; the trajectory-rate framework is the deterministic, per-K-FAC-block, sampling-free reading of Watanabe’s 𝜆 . We engage each line again in §15 as a forward-look. Watanabe (2009, 2018) are the canonical references for the foundations. 

# 3 Setup and notation 

We collect the formal setting in which the rest of the paper operates. 

The singular set. Fix a smooth parametric family { 𝑝 𝜃 : 𝜃 ∈ Θ} on a parameter space Θ ⊆ R𝑑 

and a data-generating distribution 𝑝 ∗. The singular set for the task 𝑇 = ( 𝑝 ∗) is  

> Σ𝑇

:= {𝜃 ∈ Θ : 𝑝 𝜃 = 𝑝 ∗},

the set of parameter values that exactly capture 𝑝 ∗. For a regular (identifiable) family, Σ𝑇 

is either empty or a single point. For a non-identifiable family, Σ𝑇 is in general a positive-dimensional analytic set: a finite union of smooth strata of varying dimension, possibly with crossings. Throughout this paper we assume Σ𝑇 contains at least one smooth stratum near a point 𝜃 0 of interest, which is the regime of Watanabe (2009)’s normal-crossing analysis after resolution. 

KL divergence and KL order. For 𝜃 0 ∈ Σ𝑇 and a smooth path 𝜃 (𝑡 ) with 𝜃 (0) = 𝜃 0, the KL divergence 𝐾 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) := KL ( 𝑝 ∗ ∥ 𝑝 𝜃 (𝑡 ) ) vanishes at 𝑡 = 0. The KL order along the path is the integer 𝑘 ≥ 1 such that  

> 𝐾

(𝜃 (𝑡 )) = 𝑐 𝑡 2𝑘 + 𝑂 (𝑡 2𝑘 +1), 𝑐 > 0.

On a regular family with non-singular Fisher, every path through 𝜃 0 has KL order 𝑘 = 1

(and 𝑐 = 12 𝑢 ⊤ 𝐹 (𝜃 0)𝑢 > 0 for direction 𝑢 = ¤𝜃 (0)); Theorem 2 reads vacuously here, with rate 

> Θ

(𝑡 0) = Θ(1). The interesting cases are 𝑘 ≥ 2, which occur precisely when the path is tangent to a smooth stratum of Σ𝑇 ; the rate theorem requires 𝑘 ≥ 2 to produce a non-trivial decay. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 8

Fisher information. Recall the Fisher information 𝐹 (𝜃 ) from §2. At 𝜃 = 𝜃 0 ∈ Σ𝑇 , 𝐹 has tangent directions to Σ𝑇 in its kernel: by gauge invariance of the KL (moving along Σ𝑇 leaves 𝑝 𝜃 

unchanged), the score 𝜕 𝜃 log 𝑝 𝜃 vanishes in 𝐿 2 ( 𝑝 ∗) along these directions at 𝜃 = 𝜃 0. The metric is non-singular only transversal to Σ𝑇 , and even there it loses rank as the path approaches Σ𝑇 

tangentially. The behaviour of 𝐹 along an approach to Σ𝑇 is the central object of this paper. 

K-FAC factorisation. For a layered model with parameter blocks 𝜃 = (𝑊 1, . . . , 𝑊 𝐿 ) corre-sponding to weight matrices, the Fisher’s per-layer block has a Kronecker-factored approxi-mation 

𝐹 ℓ ≈ 𝐴 ℓ ⊗ 𝐺 ℓ,

where 𝐴 ℓ := E[𝑋 ℓ−1 𝑋 ⊤

> ℓ−1

] is the activation covariance at the input of layer ℓ and 𝐺 ℓ := E[𝛿 ℓ𝛿 ⊤ 

> ℓ

]

is the gradient (back-propagated) covariance (Martens and Grosse, 2015). 2 The factorisation discards cross-layer Fisher blocks and treats each layer’s Fisher as a separable Kronecker product. We use the K-FAC structure not as a numerical approximation but as a coordinate-aware projection of 𝐹 that makes its singularity structure layer-readable: the gauge kernel of 𝐺𝐿 (ℎ)𝐿 −1 acting on cross-layer blocks lives in the discarded part, and what remains is the transversal singular structure (Section 7). 

The bridging primitive. With this setting in place, we define the object that is visible in both Amari’s and Watanabe’s languages (Figure 3).

0 as 𝑡 → 0. The KL order along 𝑢 is the integer 𝑘 ≥ 1 with 𝐾 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) = 𝑐 𝑡 2𝑘 + 𝑂 (𝑡 2𝑘 +1) for some 

𝑐 > 0, so 𝐾 has a zero of order 2𝑘 in 𝑡 .

A dead direction is any approach with KL order 𝑘 ≥ 2.Part II The rate primitive 

Part I named the dead direction and the bridge thesis; this part builds the rate primitive that carries it. The shared invariant, the KL order 𝑘 , is so far an abstract property of the singular geometry. Here it becomes a number read off an approach to the singular set. Section 4 establishes the core translation: the directional Fisher rate exponent recovers the KL order 

𝑘 in original parameter coordinates. Section 5 turns that exponent into Watanabe’s local RLCT 𝜆 = 1/( 2𝑘 ) through a selection rule on smooth singular fibres. Section 6 shows the same 𝑘 governs two further geometric observables, the Fisher-Riemannian curvature and the high-curvature volume, so the three move together as one rate chain. The rest of the paper instantiates or extends this single exponent. 

Reach. The results in this part hold for any analytic algebraic-statistical model with smooth singular fibres: Gaussian mixtures, hidden Markov models, reduced-rank regression, exponen-tial families, and neural networks with analytic activations all qualify. They are statements about the singular geometry of the model space, derived in original parameter coordinates without resolution of singularities. The later parts narrow this reach as deep-network ge-ometry (Part III) and optimiser dynamics (Part IV) add architectural and trajectory-level conditions. 

# 4 Fisher rate decay 

The KL order 𝑘 of §3 is a property of the singular geometry; the question is how to read it off. The natural way is to construct a Hironaka resolution of 𝐾 at 𝜃 0, push the monomialised form into normal-crossing coordinates, and inspect the leading exponent. The resolution is constructive in principle but intractable in practice for any non-trivial parametric family, and impossible at the scale of a deep network. The Fisher information matrix offers a more direct read: it is the second derivative of 𝐾 in the regular case, and its small eigenvalues track 𝐾 ’s vanishing in the singular case. The result of this section is that the connection is exact at the level of rate exponents. Along a dead direction with KL order 𝑘 , the directional Fisher quadratic form 𝑢 ⊤ 𝐹 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) 𝑢 decays as Θ(𝑡 2(𝑘 −1) ): a rate exponent in original parameter coordinates that recovers 𝑘 without resolution. The proof is a score expansion in 𝐿 2 ( 𝑝 ∗) together with a Schur-complement bound on the non-degenerate Fisher block in adapted coordinates. After the theorem and proof, two worked examples (a Gaussian dead direction and a two-layer linear–ReLU network) show the rate exponent computed from first principles.

Three readings of the rate. The same content takes three useful forms. As a slope. Plot 

log 𝑢 ⊤ 𝐹𝑢 against log 𝑡 along the approach. The asymptotic slope is 2(𝑘 − 1), so ˆ𝑘 = 1 + 𝛼 /2

𝐾 (𝑡 ) = 𝑐𝑡 4 (𝑘 = 2) gives 𝛼 = 2. A sextic well 𝐾 (𝑡 ) = 𝑐𝑡 6 (𝑘 = 3) gives 𝛼 = 4. Each integer KL order corresponds to a basin whose volume grows like 𝜀 1/( 2𝑘 ) at tolerance 𝜀 ; the rate exponent is the slope of the log-volume-versus-log-tolerance line. Figure 2(a) shows the three cases overlaid. 

Scope of assumption (iii); discrete distributions. Assumption (iii) requires all directional log-likelihood derivatives to lie in 𝐿 𝑝 ( 𝑝 ∗) for every finite 𝑝 . This covers the smooth parametric families used in this paper (Gaussian, softmax cross-entropy on classifiers with bounded logits, ReLU MLPs at non-zero singular configurations). Discrete distributions where 𝜕 𝛼 log 𝑝 ∗

is not uniformly 𝐿 𝑝 (e.g., distributions with mass at the boundary of the support, certain mixture-degeneration limits) require strengthening (iii) at the affected boundary.

As Section 13 shows, 𝜈 is an integral over the renormalised posterior on the singular fibre; it is universal, a function of the KL order alone (Theorem 96), with the closed form 𝜆 − ( Γ(𝜆 + 1/2)/ Γ(𝜆 )) 2, 𝜆 = 1/( 2𝑘 ), giving only its data-fluctuation-free component (the full value is numerical). Verified on the 2-component Gaussian mixture (asymmetric merge, 𝑤 ≠ 1/2), where 𝐹 2𝑘 /𝐾 2𝑘 +2 = 6(20 𝑤 2 − 20 𝑤 + 3)/( 8𝑤 2 − 8𝑤 + 1) varies smoothly from 18 (at 𝑤 = 0, degenerate) through 12 (at 𝑤 = 1/2, symmetric merge), tracking the asymmetry of the singular geometry. 

Population Fisher, the loss-gradient covariance, and what the rate needs. Theorem 2 concerns the population Fisher 𝐹 (𝜃 ) = E𝑝 ∗ [𝑠 𝑠 ⊤] evaluated on the controlled parametric approach 𝜃 (𝑡 ) = 𝜃 0 + 𝑡𝑢 . Two things stand between that statement and a number read off a network. First, the estimator : pipelines compute the loss-gradient covariance b𝐺 (𝜃 ) =  

> 𝑁 −1

Í𝑖 𝛿 𝑖 𝛿 ⊤ 

> 𝑖

(the back-propagated gradient covariance the K-FAC bridge of §7 uses), which equals the population Fisher only at a well-specified configuration where the residual variance is the model noise, the empirical-Fisher limitation of Kunstner et al. (2019). Second, the 

configuration : the theorem describes a controlled approach along a fixed dead direction, not the endpoint of an optimiser. On the controlled parametric approach (the freeze-probe of §B, where 𝑡 is set by hand and the residual stays Θ(1)) both issues are absent, and the leading exponent 2(𝑘 − 1) is recovered to three decimals across activations and families. On a learned trajectory both bite: a global ∥𝛿 ∥2 prefactor multiplies the spectrum, so at a fitted optimum 𝛿 → 0 collapses every eigenvalue together (no isolated dead direction) and on a stalled high-noise plateau the dead block never enters the descent window (Remark 11); the canonical-aligned eigenvector lineage can also rotate. More insidiously, when the prefactor itself carries a power of the trajectory parameter (as for a squared-error loss, whose residual scales with the loss), the readout returns a clean power law at a shifted exponent (the noise-free exponent shift of §12 and Remark 23): a confident fit to the wrong rate, not an obvious failure, so fit quality alone does not certify the exponent. The rate is therefore validated on the controlled population-Fisher approach, and a learned-trajectory readout recovers it only for a well-specified model inside the asymptotic window with the lineage preserved. Higher-order coefficients are data-dependent regardless. The coefficient at order 𝑡 2𝑘 −1 (the early 𝜈 -candidate that §13 rules out) is zero under symmetric base distributions by Hermite orthogonality and generically non-zero under asymmetric data. Leading-rate slope fits (log 𝜆 min , log det 𝐹 ) are robust singular-geometry diagnostics under the controlled conditions above; reported on a learned trajectory they must carry the estimator and regime explicitly.

4.2 Worked example: Gaussian dead direction 

The simplest setting in which a non-trivial KL order arises is a one-parameter sub-family of Gaussian distributions where the mean and variance are linked. Let 𝑝 𝜃 = N ( 𝜃 𝑘 , 1) with 𝜃 ∈ R

This example illustrates the rate identity in its simplest form. Two observations carry through to richer settings. First, the score-expansion identity 𝑎 1 = · · · = 𝑎 𝑘 −1 = 0 in 𝐿 2 ( 𝑝 ∗) is an inductive consequence of the KL identity (the expansion of 𝐾 has no terms below 𝑡 2𝑘 ), and the leading non-vanishing 𝑎 𝑘 produces the 𝑡 2(𝑘 −1) rate exactly. Second, Watanabe’s RLCT and the directional Fisher rate are the same invariant in two frames.

The dead-hidden-unit configuration is the canonical example used in the SLT-side study of two-layer phase transitions (Carroll, 2021; Farrugia-Roberts, 2022) and of functional-equivalence regions in tanh networks (Farrugia-Roberts, 2023); we use it only to read off the per-layer rate exponent. The canonical-aligned move at the dead unit ℎ0 steps both of its layers at once: the outgoing weight (𝑊 2)ℎ0 ↦ → 𝑡 and the incoming row (𝑊 1)ℎ0,: ↦ → 𝑡 𝑣 for a fixed unit row 𝑣 ∈ R𝑑 . Write

For 𝐿 = 3, an analogous canonical-aligned move along three layers gives 𝐾 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) = Θ(𝑡 6) (KL order 𝑘 = 3; the analytic output difference 𝑓 𝜃 (𝑡 ) (𝑥 ) − 𝑓 𝜃 0 (𝑥 ) is Θ(𝑡 3), regime-independent), and the per-layer rates form the ladder 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ) = Θ(𝑡 4), Θ(𝑡 2), Θ(𝑡 0) for ℓ = 1, 2, 3.The product 𝜆 min ( 𝐴 ℓ) 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −1) ) = Θ(𝑡 4) holds at every layer. For ReLU activation, the same canonical-aligned move produces the same rate ladder: the ReLU introduces a Bernoulli-like factor in the score expansion (the activation pattern of the dead unit is a function of 𝑥 ), but the leading 𝑡 2𝑘 structure of 𝐾 is preserved, with the rate exponent unchanged. The score-expansion proof of Theorem 2 accommodates this directly: the leading non-vanishing 𝑎 𝑘 (𝑥 ) now depends on the activation pattern through a multiplicative factor that is in 𝐿 2 ( 𝑝 ∗) under standard assumptions on the input distribution, but its presence does not change the exponent. This example illustrates two features that carry through to deeper architectures. First, canonical alignment is the precondition that makes the per-layer rates separate cleanly: when the dead direction at every layer is the same coordinate, the K-FAC factorisation isolates the per-layer rate. Second, the rate ladder 2(𝐿 − ℓ) is invariant under nonlinearities that admit the score expansion: ReLU, GELU, and linear all produce the same rate exponents at the same layers. 

# 5 The selection rule and RLCT recovery 

Theorem 2 reads a rate exponent from a single dead direction. A deep network’s Fisher has many directions, and the singular set has both tangent and normal components at any smooth Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 16 

point. Tangent directions carry gauge zeros (the metric vanishes identically along them); normal directions carry the rate primitive when they are dead. The question this section answers is: how does one tell the two apart from a measured spectrum, and what does the transversal rate compute? Under a transversality genericity condition (G) on the analytic Taylor data at 𝜃 0, the Fisher spectrum splits cleanly (tangential and transversal eigenvalue groups become separable by their rates), and the transversal rate recovers Watanabe’s single-direction contribution to the local RLCT. The transversal rate exponent 𝛼 transv gives the directional KL order ˆ𝑘 = 1+𝛼 transv /2, and the single degenerate transversal direction contributes 1/( 2 ˆ𝑘 ) to the local RLCT, read in original parameter coordinates with no resolution of singularities. The recovery holds numerically on controlled testbeds. For a deep-linear reduced-rank regression with true KL order 𝑘 = 2 (so 

𝜆 = 1/4), the measured transversal slope 1.96 ± 0.07 returns ˆ𝜆 = 0.255 ± 0.009 ; two- and three-component Gaussian mixtures recover the same 𝜆 to within a few percent (Appendix B). Section 13 extends the recovery to Watanabe’s multi-component normal-crossing form. This result extends Theorem 2 to smooth-fiber families. It is conditional in two ways that should be flagged at the outset. First, parts (b) and (c) require a spectral-genericity condition (G) under which the tangential and transversal eigenvalue groups have separable rates; (G) is open dense on the analytic Taylor data of log 𝑝 at 𝜃 0 but is not unconditional. Second, the RLCT statement in part (c) recovers a single-direction contribution 1/( 2𝑘 ) on a smooth-fiber singularity after tangential reduction. The general Watanabe normal-crossing form 𝐾 = 𝑢 (𝑔 ) Î𝑖 𝑔 2𝑘 𝑖

in Watanabe’s full RLCT min 𝑖 (ℎ𝑖 + 1)/( 2𝑘 𝑖 ) enter as a prior choice that an 𝜀 -scan volume observable resolves (Remark 92).

Three readings of the selection rule. The same content takes three useful forms. As a spectral classification. Fit a rate exponent 𝛼 𝑖 to each Fisher eigenvalue of 𝐹 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) along the approach. Eigenvalues with 𝛼 𝑖 ≈ 0 are tangential to the singular fibre (gauge zeros along the orbit); the eigenvalue with 𝛼 𝑖 ≈ 2(𝑘 − 1) is transversal and carries the rate. Under (G), the two groups are separable by their exponents alone. As an RLCT estimator. The transversal exponent 𝛼 transv recovers ˆ𝑘 = 1 + 𝛼 transv /2, and the contribution of that one direction to the local RLCT is ˆ𝜆 = 1/( 2 ˆ𝑘 ). With ℎ𝑖 = 0 (uniform prior in original parameter coordinates), this is Watanabe’s single-direction contribution. As a basin picture. Figure 4 shows the smooth fibre 𝑆 ⊂ Σ𝑇 at a point 𝜃 with tangent space 𝑇 𝜃 𝑆 and normal space 𝑁 𝜃 𝑆 split apart. Eigenvalues that live on 𝑇 𝜃 𝑆 flat-line at Θ(1) or decay at the tangential rates Θ(𝑡 2 𝑗 𝑎 ); the eigenvalue that lives on 𝑁 𝜃 𝑆 decays at Θ(𝑡 2(𝑘 −1) ) and is the rate-carrying direction. 

Status of genericity assumption (G). Part (a) is unconditional given (i*)–(iii); parts (b) and (c) require the spectral genericity (G). On the analytic Taylor data of log 𝑝 at 𝜃 0, (G) is the open dense condition that no tangential exponent 2 𝑗 𝑎 accidentally equals the transversal exponent 2(𝑘 − 1). Lemma 5 below proves that under (G) the Fisher’s eigenvalue spectrum splits into transversal, tangential, and non-degenerate-normal groups by polynomial spectral gaps, with eigenvectors converging to the corresponding coordinate basis vectors: exactly the structure parts (b) and (c) require. Failures of (G) (where a tangential and transversal exponent coincide) are non-generic in the analytic-data sense and would require a finer block-eigenvalue argument; we do not encounter them in any verified example. A small auxiliary non-degeneracy is built into the lemma’s hypothesis (the tangential leading constant 

𝛼 𝑎 > 0 after Schur subtraction); this is generic by Cauchy–Schwarz and corresponds to the natural extension of (ii*) to tangential directions.

Remark 6 (Operational uses of the transversal/tangential split) . Beyond RLCT recovery, the transversal/tangential decomposition has several operational consequences that we record here for completeness. 

• Gauge-vs-true-low-curvature separation. The protocol that reports 𝜆 𝑝 −1 (𝐹 pop  

> ℎ

) rather than raw 𝜆 min for 𝑝 -class cross-entropy on hidden-layer projections is exactly the selection rule applied to the gauge-orbit smooth fiber: gauge zeros are tangential along the orbit; the smallest non-zero transversal carries the rate. The selection rule promotes this from a per-loss patch to a generic protocol: fit a power-law slope to each top-𝐾 eigenvector versus 𝜎 min , classify rate ≈ 0 as tangential and rate ≈ 2(𝑘 − 1) as transversal. 

• LoRA placement. Inserting a low-rank update inside the tangential subspace of the current task’s singular fiber adds capacity without inducing transversal motion that would increase distortion at leading order. Validating this reduces to applying the selection rule at a base-model layer and checking that LoRA’s measured rate sits in the tangential cluster. 

• Continual learning safe directions. A direction tangential to the current task’s fiber but transversal to the next task’s is, by the selection rule’s classification, the principled definition of a safe direction: one that can carry new-task gradient without disturbing the old-task minimum at leading order. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 22 

• Rank-collapse early warning. A previously-tangential direction transitioning to a transver-sal rate during training is the rate-language statement of the new dead direction emerg-ing: the selection rule’s exponent classification is what makes the transition detectable on the spectrum rather than only on the residual-stream 𝜎 min .The post-final-LN kernel direction 𝛾 −1/∥ 𝛾 −1 ∥ of Proposition 63 is structurally tangential at the post-LN node by the same selection-rule classification: it lives in the kernel of cov (LN (𝑋 )) for any input distribution. This unifies the LN-kernel finding with the residual-stream 𝜎 min depth-invariance result of Corollary 58 as instances of the same tangential-direction phenomenon. 

Remark 7 (Extension beyond smooth fibers) . The smooth-fiber assumption covers a wide range of parametric families relevant to deep learning. It admits the following specialisations and extensions: 

• Isolated singularities (dim 𝑆 = 0, i.e., 𝑟 0 = 0, Π = 𝐼 𝑑 ) are the special case of Theorem 3 where the normal-bundle subfamily is the full family and there are no tangential directions. The theorem applies directly without modification. 

• Stratified singular sets where multiple coordinate-aligned conditions hold simultaneously (multi-component mixtures with several pairwise collapses, rank-stratified reduced-rank regression) can be handled by piecewise application of the theorem to each stratum, provided the strata satisfy a transverse-intersection (Whitney-style) regularity condition so that the local product structure 𝑢 2𝑘 + 𝑣 21 + · · · holds on each stratum’s normal bundle. Establishing this transversality is model-specific. 

• Genuinely non-stratified singularities (cuspidal or Whitney-umbrella loci) require Hiron-aka resolution (Hironaka, 1964) of 𝐾 to a normal-crossing form before the local-RLCT computation applies; this is left as future work. The singularity patterns encountered in the parametric families studied here fall within the smooth-fiber / isolated / stratified cases. 

5.1 Operational selection rule 

The text of Theorem 3 states that the transversal eigenvalue has exponent 2(𝑘 −1); the natural way to test this is to measure 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) (the smallest eigenvalue of the layer-ℓ K-FAC gradient factor) along the trajectory and fit a rate. In a parametric setting without gauge freedom this is sufficient. In a real neural network, raw 𝜆 min is a worse observable than the theorem suggests: the theorem’s rate is established via a dead-direction Schur reduction (Lemma 17), which produces a single rate-carrying eigenvalue per dead direction. Raw 𝜆 min confounds this with non-rate-carrying directions of two kinds: eigenvalues below floating-point precision relative to the matrix trace register as zero (numerical floor), and cross-weight gauge symmetries (e.g., the joint (𝑊 𝑄 , 𝑊 𝐾 ) → ( 𝛼𝑊 𝑄 , 𝑊 𝐾 /𝛼 ) freedom from the attention dot-product structure; the joint (𝑊 𝑉 , 𝑊 𝑂 ) → ( 𝑊 𝑉 𝑀, 𝑀 −1𝑊 𝑂 ) freedom from attention output composition) produce zero-eigenvalue directions in a single-component 𝐺 ℓ that have nothing to do with singularity approach. We therefore use the following operational version when applying the theorem to a transformer: 1. At each measurement checkpoint, record the top-𝐾 and bottom-𝐾 eigenvalues of 𝐺 ℓ for a tracked component (we use 𝐾 = 16 ; the parameter is only a storage budget). Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 23 

2. Fit 𝛼 𝑖 for each eigenvalue rank 𝑖 via log–log regression across the training window, using any stable 𝑡 -axis (the model-free 1/step , the MSE-analog test_loss 1/( 2𝐿 ) , or the Corollary 26 axis 𝜎 min (𝑋 ℓref ), the smallest singular value of layer ℓref ’s activations). 3. Exclude ranks whose trajectory-averaged magnitude is within a tolerance of machine precision relative to the trajectory’s maximum eigenvalue, so floor-trapped ranks do not masquerade as rate-carrying ones. 4. Among the non-excluded ranks, identify the transversal as the rank whose fitted 𝛼 𝑖 

is closest to the theorem-predicted 2(𝐿 − ℓ), provided |𝛼 𝑖 − 2(𝐿 − ℓ)| ≤ 1. If no rank matches within tolerance, report the component as “theorem-reach exceeded” rather than forcing a match. The rank-based pairing relies on the assumption that eigenvalue crossings are rare between consecutive measurements. A stronger version tracks eigenvectors via continuity across checkpoints and matches by eigenvector identity; this is a tractable joint-scope extension (eigenvector continuity is a standard perturbation-theoretic computation that can be added without changing the underlying selection rule), not generic future work. The current chunk operates at the cheapest tier of the four-tier observable hierarchy ( 𝜎 min as the 𝑡 -axis, eigenvalue ranks as the rate-carriers); periodic-tier 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) would deliver tighter rate fits but at higher sample-budget cost. Both readings are used in practice: the direct 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) rate-fit (the theorem’s literal statement) in clean parametric settings and on gauge-light layers, and the rank-aware operational version above where gauge zeros or the numerical floor would confound raw 𝜆 min .

5.2 Empirical illustration: deep-linear reduced-rank regression 

Reduced-rank regression is the standard Watanabe-side benchmark for the selection rule: a rectangular linear network 𝑋 ↦ → 𝑊 3𝑊 2𝑊 1 𝑋 trained on a rank-𝑟 teacher has, along eigendi-rections approaching the rank-𝑟 singular fibre, integer KL orders with directional RLCT computable in closed form via Aoyagi’s resolution (Aoyagi and Watanabe, 2005; Aoyagi, 2024; Lau et al., 2025). On the controlled population-Fisher approach (the freeze-probe of §B), the transversal Fisher exponent on a deep-linear 6 → 8 → 4 model is 1.96 ± 0.07 over 

5 seeds, recovering ˆ𝜆 = 0.255 against the predicted 1/( 2𝑘 ) = 0.25 (Table 8), alongside the same recovery on the 2- and 3-component Gaussian-mixture merges. 

Learned trajectories: what transfers. The selection rule is a statement about the popula-tion Fisher on the parametric approach, and its clean validation is the freeze-probe above. On a learned SGD trajectory through the rank-deficit cascade the reading is weaker, for the reasons in Remark 4: the stored per-layer object is the loss-gradient covariance, whose global 

∥𝛿 ∥2 prefactor collapses the spectrum at a well-fit optimum and stalls below the asymp-totic window otherwise, and the bottom-𝑘 eigenvector lineage rotates during descent. A direct re-analysis of learned RRR trajectories confirms this: the per-eigenvector slopes do not partition into the predicted integers {0, 2, . . . , 2𝑟 }: a spurious intermediate cluster near  

> 𝛼

≈ 1.4 appears and the higher bands stay unpopulated. What does survive is a coarser signal: the count of rate-carrying transversal lineages tracks the rank deficit, consistent with the cross-model rank-ranking the same observable provides. The integer-exponent reading Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 24 

requires the controlled freeze-probe; the learned trajectory recovers rank, not per-direction order. This matches the regime split of §12.1: the static rank count survives because it does not depend on a singular approach, while the per-direction exponents, which do, require the controlled approach the freeze-probe supplies in place of a learned descent that never enters a deep compression phase. 

# 6 Fisher–curvature–volume rate chain 

Watanabe gives the function-value volume law Vol ({ 𝐾 < 𝜀 }) ∼ 𝜀 𝜆 as an algebraic invariant of the resolved structure. Information geometry gives the Fisher metric and, with it, sectional curvature and a Riemannian volume form. The KL order 𝑘 does not show up only in the Fisher metric: the same exponent that controls the directional Fisher rate also controls two further geometric quantities along the approach to Σ𝑇 . The Fisher–Riemannian sectional curvature blows up at rate Θ(𝑡 − ( 2𝑘 −1) ), and the Fisher–Riemannian volume of the high-curvature set scales as Θ(𝑀 −𝑘 /( 2𝑘 −1) ). Each is a re-expression of the same invariant. The Fisher rate is what we measure on a trajectory; the curvature divergence is what shapes natural-gradient dynamics near the singular set; the volume scaling is the pointwise analog of Watanabe’s RLCT volume law. The three together form a rate chain: knowing one determines the others (Figure 5). The three observables (Fisher smallest eigenvalue, Fisher-Riemannian sectional curvature, and high-curvature volume) are not independent diagnostics of a singular minimum: all three are derived from the same KL leading-order expansion 𝐾 (𝜃 0 + 𝑡𝑢 ) = 𝑐𝑡 2𝑘 + 𝑂 (𝑡 2𝑘 +1). The rate 

2(𝑘 − 1) in Fisher decay (Theorem 2), −( 2𝑘 − 1) in curvature blow-up (Proposition 8), and 

−𝑘 /( 2𝑘 − 1) in Fisher-Riemannian volume scaling (Corollary 9, with Lebesgue counterpart 

−1/( 2𝑘 − 1)) are three faces of the same KL-order invariant 𝑘 . Each face requires its own genericity condition and is independently measurable: score independence (Theorem 2); a non-degenerate metric entry with non-zero gradient in the degenerate direction (Proposi-tion 8); inversion of the curvature bound onto the high-curvature set (Corollary 9). They are not, however, independent claims: they share the same load-bearing invariant. This rate chain is the paper’s mathematical backbone. It quantifies the relationships left qualitative in classical singular learning theory: Watanabe (2009) establishes the directional RLCT 𝜆 = 1/( 2𝑘 ) and the function-value volume scaling Vol ({ 𝐾 < 𝜀 }) ∼ 𝜀 𝜆 as algebraic invariants; Amari et al. (2006) characterise the rank-loss direction qualitatively. The chain below converts each into a pointwise rate exponent in the original parameter coordinates, with the multi-direction extension in Proposition 10. The rate-side of the chain (Theorem 2) is operationalised at scale via the multi-layer K-FAC bridge (Theorem 21) and the residual-stream 𝜎 min corollary (Corollary 58); the curvature and volume companions are validated parametrically on families with known 𝑘 (Validation paragraph below). 

Proposition 8 (Curvature divergence near singularities) . Under the assumptions of Theorem 2 and the additional genericity condition that there exists a non-degenerate direction 𝛼 0 with

Comparison to RLCT volume. Watanabe’s RLCT formula gives the Lebesgue volume of the function-value level set {𝐾 < 𝜀 } as ∼ 𝜀 𝜆 · (− log 𝜀 )𝑚 −1 with 𝜆 = min 𝑖 (𝑎 𝑖 + 1)/( 2𝑘 𝑖 ) in the resolved-Hironaka monomial form 𝐾 = Î𝑖 𝑢 2𝑘 𝑖  

> 𝑖

direction (min over 𝑖 ), while the per-direction Fisher-curvature volume above is a product over all directions because the curvature integral factorises across the additive normal form. The Hironaka-resolved monomial-form analysis is left to future work. 

Validation on parametric families with known 𝑘 . The chain is validated on analytic families with known KL order 𝑘 , on the controlled population-Fisher approach (the freeze-probe of §B, where the residual stays Θ(1) and the rate readout is not subject to the learned-trajectory confound of Remark 4). The rate side (Theorem 2) is the selection-rule recovery of Table 8: transversal exponents matching 2(𝑘 −1) on the 2- and 3-component Gaussian-mixture merges and on deep-linear reduced-rank regression, recovering ˆ𝜆 ≈ 1/4. The curvature side (Proposition 8) is verified symbolically: on the 2-component mixture merge ( 𝑘 = 2, 𝑤 = 0.35 

to break the equal-weight symmetry of Remark 12), |𝐾 sect | ∼ 𝛿 −3, matching the predicted 

−( 2𝑘 − 1) = −3. The volume side (Corollary 9) follows analytically from the curvature rate by integrating the Fisher volume form over the high-curvature tube ( −𝑘 /( 2𝑘 −1) = −2/3 at 𝑘 = 2); it is a consequence of the curvature face rather than an independent measurement. The rate and curvature faces are thus the two independent checks, on singularities of qualitatively different structure (multi-degenerate merges and gauge-coupled rank deficiency). 

Remark 11 (Asymptotic-regime measurement precondition) . Theorems 2 and Proposition 10 state pointwise rate exponents in the asymptotic limit 𝑡 → 0. Remark 12 (Genericity limits of the curvature rate) . The condition 𝜕 1𝑔 𝛼 0𝛼 0 ≠ 0 in Proposi-tion 8 is generic but not universal. At equal-component mixture weights ( 𝑤 = 1/2) the non-degenerate metric is symmetric in the degenerate direction, 𝜕 1𝑔 𝛼 0𝛼 0 = 0, and the sub-leading Christoffel contribution gives a slower divergence; in some cross-planes the Riemann-tensor contributions cancel and 𝐾 sect remains finite. Empirically, the 2-component Gaussian mixture at 𝑤 = 1/2 has 𝐾 sect → − 1/2 (finite) in the (𝛿, ¯𝜇 ) plane (verified by di-rect symbolic computation). The full 3-parameter model has empirical 𝐾 max ∼ 𝛿 −6 in the 

( ¯𝜇, 𝑤 ) cross-plane. This empirical 𝛿 −6 rate is consistent with (but not derived from) Propo-sition 10: with two simultaneously-degenerate directions (𝑘 1, 𝑘 2) = (2, 2), the cross-plane sectional curvature can pick up the rates of both directions multiplicatively, giving formally 

Θ(𝛿 −3) · Θ(𝛿 −3)/ Θ(1) = Θ(𝛿 −6) via a heuristic two-direction Christoffel argument. A rigorous derivation of multi-direction sectional curvature requires extending Proposition 8’s Christoffel analysis to the cross-plane case, which is left to future work; we present the 𝛿 −6 here as a target prediction that the multi-direction extension should reproduce. We collect here the supporting definitions and remarks that frame the rate-chain results of §6 (Theorem 2, Theorem 3, Proposition 8, Corollary 9, Proposition 10). The content below names the genericity hypotheses, makes the Fisher matrix structure explicit, compares the rate exponents to Watanabe’s RLCT, and indicates how the smooth-fiber result extends to stratified and resolved singular sets. 

Definition 13 (Non-degenerate boundary approach) . A path 𝜃 (𝑡 ) → 𝜃 0 as 𝑡 → 0 approaches a singularity non-degenerately if: (i) 𝜆 min (𝐹 (𝜃 (𝑡 ))) → 0; (ii) some derivative 𝜕 𝑘 𝐹 𝑖 𝑗 is bounded away from zero; (iii) 𝜆 min (𝐹 (𝜃 (𝑡 ))) = Ω(𝑡 𝑞 ) for some 𝑞 > 0.For algebraic-statistical models (real-analytic 𝜃 ↦ → 𝑝 𝜃 with analytic singular locus), condi-tions (i)–(iii) hold automatically at every singularity (Watanabe, 2009). This covers Gaussian mixtures, Boltzmann machines, HMMs, reduced-rank regressions, and neural networks with smooth activations (tanh, sigmoid, softplus). Networks with piecewise-linear activations Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 28 

(ReLU) are not strictly real-analytic, but the singular locus structure is qualitatively similar (Watanabe, 2009); the formal results of this paper apply to the analytic case. 

Comparison to Watanabe’s RLCT theory. Watanabe’s RLCT theory (Watanabe, 2009) gives the asymptotic form of the marginal log-likelihood and the KL order 𝑘 along resolved coordinates obtained via Hironaka resolution. It does not give a pointwise rate for 𝑢 ⊤ 𝐹 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) 𝑢 

or for 𝜆 min (𝐹 (𝜃 (𝑡 ))) along an analytic path approaching the singular minimum: the resolved-coordinate analysis runs on the Jacobian of the blow-up, and the Fisher rate in original coordinates falls outside it. Theorem 2’s contribution is a rate on the unresolved Fisher in original parameter coordinates, expressed in terms of the same KL order 𝑘 .

Corollary 14 (Fisher matrix structure near a single-degenerate singularity) . Under the assumptions of Theorem 2, the Fisher matrix in adapted coordinates (𝜃 1 = 𝑢, 𝜃 𝛼 ) satisfies: 

𝐹 11 = 𝑐 𝐹 𝑡 2(𝑘 −1) + 𝑂 (𝑡 2𝑘 −1), 𝐹 1𝛼 = 𝑏 𝛼 𝑡 𝑘 −1 + 𝑂 (𝑡 𝑘 ), 𝐹 𝛼𝛽 = 𝑔 𝛼𝛽 + 𝑂 (𝑡 ),

where 𝑐 𝐹 = 2𝑐𝑘 2 > 0, 𝑏 𝛼 = 𝑘 E𝑝 ∗ [𝑎 𝑘 · 𝜕 𝛼 log 𝑝 ∗], 𝑔 𝛼𝛽 = E𝑝 ∗ [( 𝜕 𝛼 log 𝑝 ∗)( 𝜕 𝛽 log 𝑝 ∗)] is positive definite, and 𝑐 𝐹 > 𝑏 ⊤𝑔 −1𝑏 (assumption (ii) of Theorem 2). Proof. The expressions follow from the proof of Theorem 2 via 𝐹 𝑢 (𝑡 ) = E𝑝 ∗ [𝑠 2 

> 𝑢

exp 𝑓 ] and the score decomposition 𝑠 𝑢 = 𝑘 𝑎 𝑘 𝑡 𝑘 −1 + 𝑂 (𝑡 𝑘 ), 𝑠 𝛼 = 𝜕 𝛼 log 𝑝 ∗ + 𝑂 (𝑡 ). □

Fiber structure resolves the apparent circularity in selection. To apply the selection rule (Theorem 3), one must identify which eigenvalues are transversal. This requires knowing the fiber 𝑆 (from the model structure), not 𝑘 itself. At the Gaussian mixture merge, 𝑆 = {𝜇 1 = 𝜇 2}

is known from the model definition and the normal bundle is the (𝜇 1, 𝜇 2) subspace; for reduced-rank regression, 𝑆 includes the gauge orbit and the normal bundle contains the rank-deficiency direction. With the fiber fixed, the controlled freeze-probe recovers transversal exponents matching 2(𝑘 − 1) on all three families (Table 8). 

Extension to singular fibers. Theorem 3 requires 𝑆 to be a smooth submanifold (so that 

𝑁 𝜃 0 𝑆 and Π are well-defined). Two extensions beyond smooth fibers are possible without the full resolution machinery: 

(i) Isolated singularities. If 𝑆 is smooth except at finitely many points, puncture the singular points and apply Theorem 3 to 𝑆 \ { singular points }. The selection rule holds on any approach path avoiding these points. 

(ii) Stratified fibers. If 𝑆 decomposes into smooth strata (typically a Whitney stratification, of which normal-crossing varieties {𝑥 1 · · · 𝑥 𝑘 = 0} are the canonical example), apply Theorem 3 to each stratum separately; the eigenvalue rates depend on which stratum is approached. Practical machine-learning singular sets are almost always of this type because they arise from products of coordinate-aligned conditions: 

• Multi-component Gaussian mixtures with several pairwise collapses, e.g. for 𝐾 = 3

components the stratification {𝜇 1=𝜇 2} ∪ { 𝜇 2=𝜇 3} ∪ { 𝜇 1=𝜇 3} meeting along the triple-collapse line {𝜇 1=𝜇 2=𝜇 3}.

• Rank-stratified reduced-rank regression: {rank (𝑊 ) ≤ 𝑟 } decomposes into {rank =

𝑟 } ⊔ { rank ≤ 𝑟 −1} ⊔ · · · , each rank stratum smooth. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 29 

• Mixture models with simultaneous weight and center collapse ( 𝑤 𝑖 → 0 and 𝜇 𝑖 → 𝜇 𝑗 ); strata indexed by the combinatorial type. 

• Overparameterised neural networks with intersecting gauge orbits (dead-neuron loci, permutation symmetry, layer-wise rescaling), which generically meet transversally and form a normal-crossing pattern. On each stratum the selection rule applies; the rates one measures depend on which stratum the trajectory 𝜃 (𝑡 ) is converging to. 

(iii) Genuinely non-stratified fibers. For singular sets that are not unions of smooth pieces meeting transversally (cuspidal singularities 𝑦 2 = 𝑥 3, the Whitney umbrella 𝑥 2 = 𝑦 2 𝑧 , tangent self-intersections such as {𝑥 2 = 𝑦 4}, higher-codimension high-order vanishing { 𝑓 1 = 𝑓 2 = 0}

with both 𝑓 𝑖 vanishing to high order along the same locus), the smooth-fiber assumption fails and the piecewise machinery does not apply directly. Hironaka’s resolution of singularities (Hironaka, 1964) provides a birational map 𝜙 : Θ′ → Θ under which the resolved fiber 𝜙 −1 (𝑆 )

is smooth, so Theorem 3 applies in the resolved coordinates. Translating the eigenvalue rates back to the original coordinates requires controlling the Jacobian of 𝜙 , which has the structure |𝐽 𝜙 | ∼ Î |𝑢 𝑖 |𝑎 𝑖 . This pullback analysis is a natural direction for future work. We are not aware of standard deployed architectures whose loss landscape produces case (iii); the families validated in this paper (Gaussian mixtures, reduced-rank regression) all have smooth fibers, and the standard combinatorial multi-collapse cases above are covered by case (ii). 

Cross-coupling in the multi-degenerate case. The decoupled assumption in Proposition 10 excludes cross-coupling between degenerate directions. When Fisher cross-terms 𝐹 𝑢 𝑖 𝑢 𝑗 create super-degenerate eigenvectors ( 𝜆 min ∼ 𝑡 𝑎 1+𝑎 2 where 𝑎 𝑖 are individual exponents), curvature in the (𝑢 𝑖 , 𝑢 𝑗 ) cross-plane can diverge faster than in any single (𝑢 𝑖 , 𝑣 ) plane. Numerical verification for the 2-component Gaussian mixture merge (3-parameter model) shows |𝐾 max | ∼ 𝛿 −6 in the 

( ¯𝜇, 𝑤 ) cross-plane, versus 𝛿 −3 from the single-direction prediction. The product lower bound in Proposition 10 captures the per-direction contributions but not this cross-coupling effect, which can only increase the actual high-curvature volume. A tight multi-degenerate volume formula would require analyzing curvature in all  𝑟 

> 2

 cross-planes. 

Selection-rule recovery on Gaussian-mixture boundaries. The selection rule (Theorem 3) recovers ˆ𝜆 from the Fisher eigenvalue with exponent 2(𝑘 − 1) on each transversal singular family. We check the two canonical boundaries, the weight singularity 𝑤 → 0 and the merge  

> 𝜇 1

→ 𝜇 2.

Part (a): Weight singularity (tangential). At 𝑤 → 0, the degenerate direction is 𝜇 1 (the mean of the vanishing component), but this direction lies within the singular set {𝑤 = 0}:changing 𝜇 1 at 𝑤 = 0 does not change the distribution ( 𝐾 (𝑤 =0, 𝜇 1) = 0 for all 𝜇 1). Hence Theorem 2’s assumption 𝐾 (𝜃 0 + 𝑡𝑢 ) = 𝑐𝑡 2𝑘 along the degenerate direction is violated: the KL is identically zero, not a polynomial in 𝑡 .The Fisher diagonal entry 𝐹 𝜇 1 𝜇 1 = 𝑤 2 ∫ [( 𝑥 −𝜇 1)/ 𝜎 2]2𝜙 21/𝑝 𝜃 𝑑𝑥 . Naïvely, the integral con-verges to a constant as 𝑤 → 0, predicting 𝐹 𝜇 1 𝜇 1 ∼ 𝑤 2. However, near 𝑥 = 𝜇 1 where 𝑝 𝜃 ≈ 𝑤𝜙 1,the integrand is 𝑂 (𝜙 1/𝑤 ), contributing an 𝑂 (1/𝑤 ) correction to the integral. Hence 𝐹 𝜇 1 𝜇 1 ∼ 𝑤 

(not 𝑤 2) at leading order, with a slowly varying correction. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 30 

The remaining eigenvalues are not constant: 𝐹 𝑤𝑤 grows as 𝑤 → 0 (the score (𝜙 1−𝜙 2)/ 𝑝 𝜃 

diverges as 𝑝 𝜃 → 𝜙 2). Numerically, fitted exponents are 1.11 , −0.16 , −0.81 , giving 𝜅 ∼ 𝛿 −1.92 

and ˆ𝜆 = 0.256 . The approximate agreement with 𝜆 = 1/4 is empirical: the compounding of a slower-than-predicted 𝜆 min decay with 𝜆 max growth yields a condition number exponent that coincidentally approximates −1/( 2𝜆 ) = −2.

Part (b): Merge singularity (transversal). At 𝜇 1 → 𝜇 2, the degenerate direction is  

> 𝛿

= 𝜇 1 − 𝜇 2. We have 𝐾 (𝛿 ) = 𝑐𝛿 4 + 𝑂 (𝛿 5) for symmetric weights, so 𝑘 = 2. The merge direction is transversal to the singular set {𝛿 = 0}, satisfying Theorem 2’s assumption. Direct computation gives 𝜆 min (𝐹 ) = Θ(𝛿 2) (matching the prediction 2(𝑘 −1) = 2), recovering 

ˆ𝜆 = 1/( 2𝑘 ) = 1/4.Parts (a) and (b) bracket the rule’s precondition. At the merge, the degenerate direction leaves the singular set and the KL grows as 𝛿 4, so the recovery returns ˆ𝜆 = 1/4 exactly. At the weight boundary, the degenerate direction lies inside {𝑤 = 0} where the KL stays identically zero; Theorem 2’s polynomial-KL hypothesis fails there, and the near-match to 

1/4 is coincidental, the condition-number exponents happening to compound to −2. The rule reaches transversal degeneracies; a tangential one enters only after the fiber and its normal bundle are identified (the fiber-structure remark above). The same transversal recovery holds across the three families of Table 8: on the deep-linear reduced-rank model the full Fisher splits into ∼55 gauge-killed eigenvalues, ∼15 

constant, and 5–6 transversal at exponent ≈ 2(𝑘 − 1), the structure the selection rule predicts. Generalisation to arbitrary analytic families is open, but the consistency across qualitatively different singularity structures (multi-degenerate merges and gauge-coupled rank deficiency) suggests the rule is general for transversal singularities. Part III The bridge framework for deep networks 

Part II read the rate off a single dead direction in one Fisher matrix. A trained network is deeper and messier: its Fisher is layered, carries a reparameterisation gauge that contributes many uninformative near-zero eigenvalues, and is assembled from repeated architectural primitives. This part lifts the rate primitive to that setting, the bridge’s deep-network half, in three moves. The multi-layer K-FAC bridge (Section 7) carries the exponent across layers as a per-layer rate ladder, read in coordinates that quotient out the gauge zeros; within it, a forward-backward duality (Section 7.1) makes the two K-FAC factors mirror images whose product is depth-independent. Composition additivity (Section 8) adds rates across stacked blocks where a scalar-transfer hypothesis holds, and marks where pure attention chains break it. The architectural instantiations (Section 9) then derive a closed-form rate for each primitive of a modern transformer from the primitive’s own algebra. One KL-order exponent runs through all three; only its reading point changes. 

Reach. These results are architecture-specific, the first narrowing from Part II’s model-agnostic reach. The K-FAC bridge assumes a layered architecture with canonical alignment of the dead direction across layers; composition additivity assumes the scalar-transfer hypothesis, which MLP and pre-norm residual chains satisfy and pure attention chains break at depth 

≥ 4; and each architectural instantiation carries its own assumptions, named where it is stated. The exception is the 𝜎 min depth-invariance corollary (Corollary 58): it makes no canonical-alignment assumption, depending only on the additive-identity skip structure, and holds for any residual DAG (the directed acyclic computation graph of a residual network) with exact-identity skips regardless of what lies on the weight edges. 

# 7 The multi-layer K-FAC bridge 

Lifting the single-direction rate to a deep network runs straight into the reparameterisation gauge. A layered Fisher has ∼ ℎ2 𝐿 parameters and an inner-matrix gauge group 𝐺𝐿 (ℎ)𝐿 −1 that contributes ∼ ℎ2 (𝐿 − 1) near-zero eigenvalues independent of the singular geometry, so the obvious diagnostic 𝜆 min (𝐹 raw ) on the full Fisher is dominated by gauge zeros and says nothing about the singularity rate. K-FAC (Martens and Grosse, 2015) is the fix: factorise the per-layer Fisher as 𝐹 ℓ ≈ 𝐴 ℓ ⊗ 𝐺 ℓ. The block-diagonal restriction discards the cross-layer Fisher blocks where the gauge kernel lives, leaving per-layer factors whose smallest eigenvalues carry the singularity signal directly. This section lifts the rate into those coordinates. Under canonical alignment of the dead direction across layers, the per-layer K-FAC gradient factor inherits a layer-dependent rate 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ), and the dual activation factor 𝜆 min ( 𝐴 ℓ) =

31 Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 32 

Θ(𝑡 2(ℓ−1) ). The product is layer-independent: 𝜆 min ( 𝐴 ℓ) 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −1) ) at every ℓ. The proof reduces to a Schur complement on the non-dead Fisher block at each layer, controlled by the gauge-kernel structure of 𝐺𝐿 (ℎ)𝐿 −1 acting on cross-layer Fisher blocks. We carry the framework in coordinates Amari’s lineage already uses; what is new is the rate. The dead-direction rate Θ(𝑡 2𝐾 (ℓ) ) across every bridge result in this paper (the feedforward case (Theorem 21), the cross-entropy case (Theorem 36), the residual-DAG case (Theo-rem 43), the rectangular-width case (Theorem 103), the multi-direction asymmetric case (Theorem 106), the bias-augmented case (Theorem 112), and the single-head attention case (Theorem 66)) is established by a single shared mechanism: the dead-direction Schur reduction of Lemma 17 below. Each architectural extension is an instantiation that verifies (i) canonical alignment of the dead direction at every layer (or its rectangular / multi-direction / residual-DAG generalisation), (ii) Schur-reducible Fisher block in canonical coordinates (dead diagonal Θ(𝑡 2𝐾 (ℓ) ), dead-row off-diagonals 𝑂 (𝑡 𝐾 (ℓ) ), non-dead block ≻ 0 at Θ(1)), and (iii) dead-direction-only 𝑡 -scaling on the backward chain. The architecture-specific work is the verification; the rate conclusion follows from the lemma. We refer to the result of Lemma 17 as the Bridge Framework Lemma and the architecture-specific verifications above as instantiations ; Theorem 21 is the canonical instantiation, and the architectural subsections that follow instantiate the framework on the relaxations relevant to actual neural networks. Remark 19 (Scope of the quantitative constant; per-instantiation status) . Lemma 18 pins down the Schur-cancellation constant 𝑐 in the canonical setup of Lemma 17 (Theorem 21) (ReLU, isotropic Gaussian input, canonical-coordinate alignment). For parametric models where all parameters are meaningful (no overparameterization), Algorithm 1 simplifies: compute the full Fisher eigenvalues and distinguish singularity types by eigenvalue degeneracy count. 

Estimand for quantitative recovery. The flagged G-factor is the empirical Fisher (training-loss gradients), the right object for detecting a transition. Quantitative ˆ𝜆 recovery instead reads the expected (true) Fisher: off the optimum the empirical G-factor carries a residual prefactor that corrupts the rate exponent (Remark 4, Corollary 39). 

Position in the observable hierarchy. Algorithm 1 sits at the periodic tier (per-𝐾 -epochs) of the four-tier observable hierarchy: 𝜎 min (𝑋 ℓ) on the residual stream is the real-time tier (cheap forward-pass at every step), 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) via K-FAC is the periodic tier as deployed here,  

> 𝑢 ⊤𝐺𝑢

at a fixed direction is the checkpoint tier (single backward pass on demand), and the full Fisher spectrum is the offline tier. All four are computable at LLM scale; the trade-off is cost-vs-information rather than feasibility. Algorithm 1 is appropriate when periodic structural-transition flags are needed during training. 

Detecting task expansion from the K-FAC G-factor. Task expansion is a common continual-learning transition: a trained classifier’s head grows to cover new classes, with the new outputs randomly initialised and the encoder reused. Such a transition is invisible to the loss alone, yet it leaves a sharp signature in the gradient geometry, because the fresh outputs inject a high-variance gradient block that the old, near-converged classes lack. The G-factor Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 37 

𝐺 head isolates that signature from the input-side conditioning, and the mechanism below shows 𝜅 (𝐺 head ) spikes at the transition. For a linear layer 𝑦 = 𝑊 𝑥 + 𝑏 , the K-FAC approximation (Martens and Grosse, 2015) gives 𝐹 ℓ ≈ 𝐴 ℓ ⊗ 𝐺 ℓ with 𝐴 ℓ = 1

> 𝑛

(input covariance) and 𝐺 ℓ = 1

> 𝑁

Í𝑛 𝑔 𝑛 𝑔 ⊤ 

> 𝑛

(gradient covariance).

The head’s 𝐺 head directly encodes the new/old class variance separation (part (a)); hidden layers see this through the linear transformation 𝐽 ℓ, which can amplify, attenuate, or rotate the anisotropy depending on its singular value structure. The bound 𝜅 (𝐽 ⊤ 𝐴𝐽 ) ≤ 𝜅 (𝐽 )2𝜅 ( 𝐴 ) gives an upper bound on 𝜅 (𝐺 ℓ) in terms of 𝜅 (𝐺 head ) and 𝜅 (𝐽 ℓ), but does not imply 𝜅 (𝐺 ℓ) ≤ 𝜅 (𝐺 head ):hidden layers could in principle have larger 𝜅 if 𝐽 ℓ concentrates the anisotropy. What (b) establishes is that the head is the origin of the task-change signal: hidden layers’ anisotropy is derived from the head’s via 𝐽 ℓ, not generated independently. The temporal ordering of the cascade (head first, then deeper layers) is an empirical observation consistent with 𝐽 ℓ initially attenuating the signal, but this ordering is not a formal consequence of (A1)–(A3) alone.

Scope. Parts (a)–(b) are proved under assumptions (A1)–(A3), which hold by construction in the controlled task-expansion verification setup. Extension to natural structural transi-tions (data quality changes, fine-tuning) lies outside these assumptions. The connection Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 38 

between 𝜅 (𝐺 ℓ) and RLCT singularities is established for 𝐿 -layer networks with smooth or ReLU activations (Theorem 21).

Remark 22 (Three readings of the ladder) . The same content takes three useful forms. As a per-layer ladder. 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) has slope 2(𝐿 −ℓ) versus log 𝑡 , monotone-decreasing toward the input: the deepest hidden layer reads Θ(1), the shallowest reads Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −1) ). The slope of each rung is one integer step lower than the rung above. As an A–G duality. Whichever side of the K-FAC product carries the rate at layer ℓ, the other side compensates: 𝜆 min ( 𝐴 ℓ)𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −1) )

is layer-independent. The forward and backward signals share one global exponent; only the location of that exponent on the ladder rotates with ℓ. As a gauge-quotient statement. The raw Fisher’s ∼ ℎ2 (𝐿 − 1) near-zero eigenvalues are gauge zeros of 𝐺𝐿 (ℎ)𝐿 −1 acting on cross-layer Fisher blocks. K-FAC’s block-diagonal restriction projects orthogonal to those gauge directions and leaves the per-layer block to carry the singularity signal. The ladder is what the rate primitive looks like in coordinates aligned to the network’s layered structure rather than its raw parameter vector. Remark 23 (Regime conditions on Theorem 21) . The setup of Theorem 21, made fully explicit in Lemma 17, places the trajectory in the noisy regime : target 𝑦 = 𝑀 ∗ 𝑥 + 𝜀 with 

𝜀 ∼ N ( 0, 𝜎 2 𝐼 ℎ) along the dead direction 𝑒 ℎ. The output backward signal 𝛿 (𝐿,ℎ ) = −𝜀 (ℎ) + Θ(𝑡 𝐿 )

inherits its Θ(1) leading magnitude from the noise variance 𝜎 2, which then propagates inward as Θ(𝑡 𝐿 −ℓ) to give the stated 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ).In the corresponding noise-free regime (𝜎 = 0, target 𝑦 = 𝑀 ∗ 𝑥 exactly), the same algebra (Lemma 16 applied with the residual −𝜀 (ℎ) replaced by 𝑡 𝐿 𝑥 (ℎ) ) shifts the output backward signal to magnitude Θ(𝑡 𝐿 ), propagating inward to Θ(𝑡 2𝐿 −ℓ) at layer ℓ. This raises every per-layer rate by +2𝐿 in the exponent ( +4 at 𝐿 = 2, +6 at 𝐿 = 3, etc.): 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(2𝐿 −ℓ) ),slope 2(2𝐿 − ℓ) at 𝜎 min (𝑊 ℓ) ∼ 𝑡 . The two regimes share the proof framework but produce different exponents, so a trajectory measurement that quotes the rate must declare which regime it is in. A task with irreducible empirical loss (for example the sparse-parity task of Barak et al., 2022) sits in the noisy regime; static observables such as the residual-stream 

𝜎 min (Corollary 58) and the LN-kernel direction (§9.3) are regime-independent and carry no such caveat.

Corollary 27 (Quotient global Fisher rate matches parametric theory) . On the gauge quotient 

¯Θ = Θ/𝐺𝐿 (ℎ)𝐿 −1 (the parameter space with the inner-matrix reparameterisation redundancy di-vided out; Corollary 78), the whole-network (global) Fisher ¯𝐹 satisfies 𝜆 min ( ¯𝐹 ( ¯𝜃 (¯𝑡 ))) = Θ(¯𝑡 2(𝐿 −1) ),matching Theorem 2 with 𝑘 = 𝐿 . The raw-parameter Fisher on Θ itself has ℎ2 (𝐿 − 1) gauge-null directions independent of 𝑡 (the inner-matrix reparameterisation orbit, see Theorem 21(d)) and so does not attain this rate at 𝜆 min (𝐹 raw ); the rate is recovered on the quotient or, equivalently, via the per-layer K-FAC blocks 𝐹 ℓ which are gauge-orthogonal to the inner-block 𝐺𝐿 (ℎ) null directions by construction (each 𝐹 ℓ lives on the per-layer parameter slice transverse to the cross-layer gauge action). Proof. The per-layer K-FAC blocks satisfy 𝐹 ℓ ≈ 𝐴 ℓ ⊗𝐺 ℓ exactly for linear (P1) and approximately for nonlinear (Martens and Grosse, 2015); around the canonical singular point the activation– gradient cross-block coupling does not affect the per-block dead-direction rate. The cross-block coupling is either the same order 𝑡 2(𝐿 −1) as the within-block rate, in which case it is a 𝐺𝐿 (ℎ)𝐿 −1

gauge direction that the block-diagonal restriction discards (below), or it is Θ(1) and confined to the non-dead subspace, where it cannot enter the per-block smallest eigenvalue. (The within-block rates of Theorem 21 are exact statements about 𝐴 ℓ and 𝐺 ℓ as defined; only the identification with the global quotient Fisher in this corollary uses the K-FAC block-diagonal restriction.) Each block’s smallest eigenvalue is 𝜆 min ( 𝐴 ℓ) · 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −1) ) by Corollary 25. The minimum over ℓ is therefore Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −1) ), attained equally at every layer. The raw Fisher’s ℎ2 (𝐿 − 1) gauge-null directions live on cross-layer Fisher blocks (they require non-zero off-block entries to be nontrivial) and are absent from the K-FAC block-diagonal restriction by construction. Identifying K-FAC’s per-layer blocks with the horizontal lift of the quotient Fisher (Lemma 76: the Fisher vanishes on vertical 𝐺𝐿 (ℎ)𝐿 −1 orbit directions), the per-layer K-FAC eigenvalue equals the quotient Fisher eigenvalue at the corresponding lift. The minimum on the quotient is therefore min ℓ 𝜆 min (𝐹 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −1) ). □Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 41

7.1 The A–G duality 

Corollary 25 makes the forward and backward halves of the Fisher’s K-FAC factorisation dual. 

𝜆 min ( 𝐴 ℓ) encodes the rate at which forward signal in the dead channel decays from the input, and 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) encodes the rate at which backward gradient decays from the output. Their exponents add: 2(ℓ − 1) + 2(𝐿 − ℓ) = 2(𝐿 − 1) at every layer. The product 𝜆 min ( 𝐴 ℓ) 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ)

recovers Theorem 2’s global rate exponent at 𝑘 = 𝐿 , independent of which layer one measures. The duality is a statement entirely in Amari’s metric language (it depends only on the Kro-necker structure of the per-layer Fisher), but its exponent is Watanabe’s KL order propagating through the layered architecture. Measuring either side alone recovers the global rate. The duality pairs a gradient-covariance dead direction 𝑔 min with an input-covariance dead direction 𝑎 min . Their Kronecker product names the flattest parameter direction of the layer, which gives a constructive way to read the dead direction off the two small factors. 

Corollary 28 (Kronecker lift of the dead direction) . Adopt the setup of Theorem 21, with per-layer K-FAC factor 𝐹 ℓ ≈ 𝐴 ℓ ⊗𝐺 ℓ, input covariance 𝐴 ℓ, and gradient covariance 𝐺 ℓ. The eigenvectors of 𝐴 ℓ ⊗ 𝐺 ℓ are the Kronecker products 𝑎 𝑖 ⊗ 𝑔 𝑗 of the factor eigenvectors, with eigenvalue 𝜆 𝐴,𝑖 𝜆 𝐺, 𝑗 .The smallest-eigenvalue parameter direction of layer ℓ is the bottom product 𝑎 min ⊗ 𝑔 min , the rank-one weight increment

It lifts the gradient-covariance dead direction 𝑔 min to parameter space through the input factor 𝑎 min . At the canonical singular configuration 𝑎 min = 𝑔 min = 𝑒 ℎ, so 𝑢 ℓ = 𝑒 ℎ𝑒 ⊤

(2023), who characterise the relationship between Watanabe’s free-energy phase transitions and the dynamical phase transitions seen under Adam training; the parametric freeze-probe reading below is complementary, isolating the static rate exponent that the bridge theorem predicts at canonical alignment. The probed direction matters too. Computing 𝑢 ⊤𝐺𝑢 at the canonical dead direction gives slope 2.000 ±0.001 at the hidden layer, matching the directional rate prediction independently of the spectrum-bottom reading. The two channels (the bottom Fisher eigenvalue and the directional Fisher rate along the canonical dead direction) agree to three decimals ( 1.9999 vs 

2.000 ), as required by the bridge theorem in the canonical-aligned regime. This is the parametric-freeze-probe form of the bridge prediction: 30 -seed multi-direction noise on 𝑣 0, no SGD, no optimiser-induced drift, and the rate exponents come out at integer values to three decimals. A separate trajectory reading of TMS, in which the same architecture is trained with Adam and exhibits a phase transition, is governed by Remark 80 (Adam non-equivariance under the gauge symmetries of TMS) and is outside the scope of the bridge theorem’s canonical-aligned regime; the parametric reading above is the cleanest theorem-match available, free of the optimiser-side caveats. 

# 8 Composition additivity and its failure 

Theorem 21 covers a single feedforward stack of weight matmuls. Real networks compose het-erogeneous blocks (MLPs, attention, residual sub-paths), each with its own internal singular structure. The question this section asks is: given the per-block rate of each constituent, what rate does the composition exhibit? Under a scalar-transfer hypothesis that holds for MLP and pre-norm residual chains, the per-block KL orders compose additively: the dead-direction Fisher rate at the input of block 𝐵 𝑖 in a chain 𝑀 = 𝐵 𝑛 ◦ · · · ◦ 𝐵 1 is Θ(𝑡 2 Í𝑗 ≥𝑖 𝑘 bk  

> 𝑗

). Pure attention chains break this hypothesis at depth ≥ 4. Softmax cross-block coupling produces an empirical saturation at a rank-collapse depth 𝑘 ★, with refined closed-form rates 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 = 4 min (𝑘, 𝑘 ★),

𝛼 𝑊 𝑉 = 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 + 2, and 𝛼 𝑊 𝑄 = 𝛼 𝑊 𝐾 = 4𝑝 + 2 at a probe block at position 𝑝 in a chain of length 𝑁 

with 𝑘 = 𝑁 − 1 − 𝑝 . The 𝑊 𝑉 offset and the 𝑊 𝑄 = 𝑊 𝐾 formula are parameter-free and match the measured rates to integer slope precision; the 𝑊 𝑂 formula matches once its saturation depth 𝑘 ★ is fit to the data (a closed-form expression for 𝑘 ★(𝑑, 𝑛 ℎ, seq _len ) is open).

For pure attention chains at depth 𝑛 ≥ 4,the scalar-transfer hypothesis fails: softmax couples the dead-direction backward (resp. forward) signal at 𝐵 𝑖 to the cumulative forward (resp. backward) signal at upstream (resp. downstream) blocks, so the per-block transfer becomes context-dependent rather than scalar, and additivity breaks (Remark 32).
