Remark 32 (Attention composition anomaly) . The clean additivity of Theorem 30 does not 

extend to pure attention chains (non-residual sequential self-attention blocks). Parametric probes at 𝑛 ∈ { 4, 6}, 𝑑 = 16 , 𝑛 ℎ = 2 give component-wise rates that deviate systematically from the naive 𝑘 bk attn = 2 per-block additivity: 

• 𝑊 𝑂 at probe position ℓ saturates at 𝛼 ≈ 8 for 𝑛 − 1 − ℓ ≥ 3 instead of growing additively as 6, 10 , 14 , . . . ;

• 𝑊 𝑄 and 𝑊 𝐾 rates scale with ℓ (position from the input ) rather than 𝑛 − 1 − ℓ (position from the output), consistent with their gradient path coupling to the cumulative forward 

signal through softmax Jacobians rather than the cumulative backward chain; 

• the invariant 𝛼 𝑊 𝑉 −𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 = 2 is preserved across all (𝑛, ℓ ) tested, matching the single-block standalone offset (every attention block has one Linear’s worth of gradient-side 𝑡 -factor, and the softmax coupling does not alter this within-block sequence). Block-composition validation at 𝑛 = 2 (the four ordered attention/MLP block pairs at 𝑑 = 4)was consistent with simple additivity; the breakdown is specific to deeper attention-only stacks ( 𝑛 ≥ 4). The structural mechanism is formalised in Proposition 69 as three closed-form formulas ( 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 ( 𝑝, 𝑁 ) = 4 min (𝑁 −1− 𝑝, 𝑘 ★), 𝛼 𝑊 𝑉 = 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 +2, 𝛼 𝑊 𝑄 = 𝛼 𝑊 𝐾 = 4𝑝 +2) derived from the cumulative softmax-Jacobian rank collapse mechanism. The 𝑊 𝑉 offset ( 𝛼 𝑊 𝑉 − 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 = 2) and the  

> 𝑊 𝑄

= 𝑊 𝐾 = 4𝑝 + 2 formula are parameter-free and match the measured rates to integer slope precision across all 24 measurement cells at (𝑁, 𝑝 ) ∈ {( 4, 0), (4, 2), (4, 3), (6, 0), (6, 3), (6, 5)} ;the 𝑊 𝑂 formula matches once its saturation depth 𝑘 ★ is fit to the 𝑊 𝑂 data. The saturation depth 𝑘 ★ is empirically 2 at (𝑑, 𝑛 ℎ, seq _len ) = (16 , 2, 4); a closed form for 𝑘 ★ in terms of these parameters is open. On practical residual-wrapped transformers the anomaly is bypassed: Corollary 58’s skip edge gives 𝐾 fwd = 0 regardless of what lies on the weight edges, so residual-stream 𝜎 min predictions are unaffected. The anomaly affects internal component rates ( 𝑊 𝑂 , 𝑊 𝑉 , 𝑊 𝑄 , 𝑊 𝐾 ) within each block, not the residual stream observable. 

# 9 Architectural instantiations 

Modern networks are built from a small set of repeated primitives, and the bridge instantiates at each. The primitives cover a standard pre-norm transformer’s forward map end to end: the structural primitives that carry the forward map (rectangular widths for embeddings and MLP projections, residual DAGs, biases), the normalisation layer (layer normalisation, with RMSNorm as its companion), the gated feedforward block (SwiGLU, the gated MLP of modern large language models; GeGLU and ReGLU follow the same factorisation), and the 

sequence-mixing block (single-head attention). Three analysis-side extensions broaden the rate theorem’s hypotheses to the settings these architectures train in: multi-direction singularities, cross-entropy loss, and non-canonical alignment. Each instantiation has the same shape: a per-block KL order, the rate composing through the additivity theorem (Section 8) where the scalar-transfer hypothesis holds, and the resulting singular-geometry consequence. Figure 6: Composition additivity (Theorem 30). The two regimes the theorem distinguishes. (a) MLP chains at 𝑁 ∈ { 6, 8, 12 }, 𝑑 = 16 : per-component measured slopes lie on the 𝑦 = 𝑥

saturates at 𝛼 = 8 for 𝑘 > 𝑘 ★ = 2, deviating from naive additivity 4(𝑁 −1−𝑝 ) (dotted line). The refined formula 4 min (𝑘, 𝑘 ★) (solid line) matches the measurement; the saturation is the softmax-Jacobian rank-collapse mechanism. Practical residual-wrapped transformers bypass this saturation: the skip dominates the residual stream regardless of what lies inside the attention block (Cor. 58). predicted rate is checked by a static parametric freeze-probe on the theorem’s own trajectory; these validations are collected in Appendix B.2 and summarised in Figure 9. Table 3 lays out how each primitive acts on the canonical rate ladder, and the table splits the primitives into the two groups this section treats differently. Most leave the ladder intact: rectangular widths, multi-direction singularities, and biases carry the per-block KL order through composition unchanged, the SwiGLU gate contributes a fixed forward order, and non-canonical alignment marks the boundary where a learned trajectory leaves the regime the ladder describes. These carry no behaviour beyond what the composition theorem already gives, so we record their rates in the table and develop them, with their freeze-probe validations, in the architectural catalogue of Appendix A. The four primitives developed here are the ones that change the picture, each introducing a phenomenon the bare ladder does not capture: cross-entropy loss moves the analysis onto the expected Fisher and brings in the logit-shift gauge (Section 9.1); the residual skip makes the bottom-of-spectrum 𝜎 min depth-invariant and readable from a single forward pass (Section 9.2); LayerNorm both lowers the integer ladder to a fractional rate and pins an algebraic kernel direction (Section 9.3); and stacking single-head attention breaks composition additivity through the cumulative softmax Jacobian (Section 9.4). Two of these four are of a different character from the rate-decay theorems, and they hold across the optimiser regimes considered in this paper. The LayerNorm kernel direction 

𝛾 −1/∥ 𝛾 −1 ∥ (Proposition 63) is an exact algebraic identity: cov (LN (𝑋 )) 𝑣 ∗ = 0 holds for any input distribution because of LN’s mean-subtraction projector, with 𝑣 ∗ fixed by the affine parameter alone. The residual-DAG bottom-of-active-spectrum statement (Corollary 58) follows from the path-cost decomposition of the forward chain rule under a non-cancellation Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 47 

Table 3: How each architectural primitive acts on the canonical rate ladder. The five entries above the rule carry the ladder through composition (or, for non-canonical alignment, mark where it ceases to hold) without introducing new phenomena; they are stated and freeze-probe-validated in the architectural catalogue, Appendix A. The four below the rule each introduce behaviour the bare ladder does not capture and are developed in this section.                               

> Primitive Effect on the canonical ladder Result Rectangular widths preserved, per factor Thm 103 Multi-direction / asymmetric per-direction ladder Thm 106 Biases preserved Thm 112 SwiGLU gate forward order 𝑘 =3Prop 114 Non-canonical alignment ladder ceases to hold Prop 119 Cross-entropy loss expected-Fisher rate; logit-shift gauge Thm 36 Residual DAG 𝜎 min depth-invariant (rate 0)Cor 58 LayerNorm fractional rate; kernel 𝛾 −1/∥ 𝛾 −1∥Thm 60, Prop 63 Single-head attention standalone 𝑘 =2; composition anomaly Thm 66

hypothesis on the residual branch, and is empirically falsifiable per architecture from a single forward pass. Both depend on the forward-map algebra rather than on a canonical-aligned trajectory, which is why they hold outside the optimiser-regime scope that bounds the rate predictions. The list is not exhaustive. It covers the primitives that compose a pre-norm transformer’s forward map, with residual DAGs extending to ResNet-style skips. Primitives outside that slice, including multi-head and grouped-query attention, rotary position embeddings, and mixture-of-experts routing, are natural extensions following the same per-primitive recipe (a per-block KL order entering the composition) and are not treated here. 

9.1 Cross-entropy loss 

Theorem 21 is stated for squared-error loss with Gaussian noise, where the output-layer  

> 𝐺 𝐿

= 𝜎 −2 𝐼 is exactly isotropic and the residual at convergence is the noise 𝜀 . For supervised classification the loss is cross-entropy, and at a memorising parameter the empirical output gradient 𝑔 head = softmax (𝑧 ) − 𝑒 𝑦 collapses to zero: the same denominator that kept the MSE rate readable is gone. The natural move is to replace the empirical Fisher with the 

expected Fisher (sample the label from the model’s own predictive distribution rather than the training label); the data-averaged softmax Hessian 𝐻 (𝑥 ; 𝜃 ) is bounded below on {1}⊥

by a non-degeneracy assumption on the data-averaged class distribution, and the base case of the bridge proof carries through with 𝑐 0 replacing 𝜎 −2. The rest of the backward-chain argument transfers unchanged.

Remark 37 (Static-Fisher rate vs. Adam+CE trajectory observable) . Theorem 36 is a state-ment about the static expected-Fisher spectrum at the parametric trajectory 𝜃 (𝑡 ). CE in-troduces the logit-shift gauge 𝐺 = R as the loss’s continuous symmetry; under Adam, the empirical observation of gauge-mode drift on this gauge (Remark 80) is what makes rate-fitting on an Adam+CE trajectory return drift rather than the theorem’s exponent. The robust static-checkpoint observable on Adam+CE is 𝜎 min (𝑋 ℓ) on the residual stream (Corollary 58). To recover the trajectory exponent under CE one applies a gauge fix at training time (the auxiliary 𝑧 -loss penalty (Shazeer et al., 2018; Zoph et al., 2022) is the standard construction) combined with an optimizer whose residual non-equivariance under remaining symmetries is bounded; Remark 80 discusses both routes. 

Remark 38 (Joint scope with the rectangular extension) . The CE replacement in Lemma 33 touches only the ℓ = 𝐿 output-head base case; the rectangular extension touches only the non-dead-block control through the Schur reduction (off-diagonal 𝐴 ℓ entries under ℎℓ ≠ ℎℓ−1)at the hidden layers. The two extensions therefore commute, and the joint statement holds: for any 𝐿 -layer feedforward chain with rectangular widths ℎ1, . . . , ℎ 𝐿 −1 and output width  

> ℎ𝐿

When approaching the theorem’s assumption boundaries (rectangular widths, or transformers where cross-weight gauges contribute extra null directions), the dead-direction entry remains well-defined and carries the predicted rate, while raw 𝜆 min may pick up gauge zeros or plateau eigenvalues. An operational spectral-identification rule recovers the dead-direction entry from an unlabeled top-𝐾 eigenvalue spectrum by rate-matching to the theorem’s prediction. Corollary 39 (Empirical Fisher collapse under memorization) . Let 𝐹 emp (𝜃 ) =

∇𝜃 log 𝑝 𝜃 ( 𝑦 obs | 𝑥 ) ∇ 𝜃 log 𝑝 𝜃 ( 𝑦 obs | 𝑥 )⊤ denote the empirical Fisher, where D

is a fixed data distribution of (𝑥, 𝑦 obs ) pairs with marginal 𝑞 on 𝑥 . Suppose the trajectory 

𝑡 ↦ → 𝜃 (𝑡 ) satisfies 𝑝 𝜃 (𝑡 ) ( 𝑦 obs | 𝑥 ) → 1 for D-almost-all (𝑥, 𝑦 obs ) as 𝑡 → 0 (“perfect memorization of D”). Then 𝐹 emp (𝜃 (𝑡 )) → 0 as 𝑡 → 0, while 𝐹 exp (𝜃 (𝑡 )) continues to satisfy the rate structure of Theorem 36. Proof. Under perfect memorization, for each (𝑥, 𝑦 obs ) in the support of D we have 𝑝 𝜃 (𝑡 ) ( 𝑦 obs |

𝑥 ) → 1, hence 𝛿 (𝐿 ) (𝑥, 𝑦 obs ; 𝜃 (𝑡 )) = softmax (𝑧 (𝑥 ; 𝜃 (𝑡 ))) − 𝑒 𝑦 obs → 0. The per-sample gradient 

∇𝜃 log 𝑝 𝜃 ( 𝑦 obs | 𝑥 ) = 𝐽 (𝑥 ; 𝜃 )⊤𝛿 (𝐿 ) (𝑥, 𝑦 obs ; 𝜃 ) therefore vanishes, and 𝐹 emp collapses as the expectation of a vanishing PSD quantity. 

𝐹 exp does not collapse because its integrand averages over 𝑦 ∼ 𝑝 𝜃 (· | 𝑥 ), yielding 𝐻 (𝑥 ; 𝜃 )

(Lemma 33), which is bounded below on {1}⊥ by Assumption 34; the rate structure then follows from Theorem 36. □

Remark 40 (Why 𝐹 emp and 𝐹 exp do not PSD-dominate each other in general) . It is tempting to claim 𝐹 emp ⪯ 𝐹 exp via Jensen’s inequality, but this is incorrect in general. Both quantities are averages of rank-one matrices 𝛿 (𝐿 ) (𝛿 (𝐿 ) )⊤ over different joint distributions of (𝑥, 𝑦 ): D

versus (𝑞 ⊗ 𝑝 𝜃 ). Neither average PSD-dominates the other without additional structural assumptions tying the two distributions together (e.g., if 𝑦 obs (𝑥 ) ∼ 𝑝 𝜃 (· | 𝑥 ), the two are equal in expectation). Corollary 39’s content is strictly about the trajectory-limit 𝐹 emp → 0, not about any PSD ordering. Corollary 39 is the cross-entropy instance of the estimator scope set out in Remark 4: on post-memorization training trajectories, the empirical Fisher loses its rate signal to numerical floor even though the rate structure is still encoded in eigenvalue ratios. Measurement protocols on trained classifiers should therefore use the expected Fisher rather than the empirical Fisher when rate-fitting is the goal. 

Scope. Theorem 36 preserves the remaining scope limitations of Theorem 21 not addressed here: feedforward chain without residuals (addressed by Theorem 43), canonical-basis-aligned dead direction (addressed partially in §A.5), element-wise hidden activation (the attention case is below). The square-hidden assumption is removed separately by the rectan-gular extension; the joint rectangular × CE statement is given by Remark 38, and Table 4’s 

6 → 8 → 4 rectangular configuration is a strict instance of that joint statement. The trajectory-rate scope under Adam+CE is governed by Remark 37 and Corollary 78. 

9.2 Residual DAGs and 𝜎 min depth-invariance 

Theorem 21, Theorem 36, and Theorem 103 assume a feedforward chain with no skip connections. Modern networks (ResNets, transformer FFN blocks, transformer attention blocks) all wrap sub-chains in additive-identity residual connections of the form 

ℎout = ℎin + 𝐹 (ℎin ),

where 𝐹 is a short feedforward sub-chain. An identity skip is not a weight matmul, so it does not carry a 𝑡 -factor in the dead direction’s chain-rule expansion. The natural question is how the rate ladder of Theorem 21 adjusts when skips are present, and whether a clean Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 52 

Table 4: Cross-entropy vs. MSE eigenvalue scaling at 𝐿 = 2 (5 seeds, 6 → 8 → 4 network, rank-2 target). Parametric freeze-probe: the dead-direction entry 𝑢 ⊤ 

> ℓ

𝐺 ℓ (𝜃 (𝑡 )) 𝑢 ℓ is fit against 

𝑡 along the canonical construction, with the cross-entropy rows under the expected Fisher (Lemma 33). Predicted exponent: 2.000 for 𝑘 = 2.

Activation Loss Exponent 𝛼 Match? Identity MSE 1.995 ± 0.003 yes Identity Cross-entropy 1.972 ± 0.025 yes GeLU MSE 2.066 ± 0.008 yes GeLU Cross-entropy 2.044 ± 0.068 yes Tanh MSE 1.914 ± 0.003 yes Tanh Cross-entropy 1.900 ± 0.036 yes 

graph-distance reading exists. This subsection answers both: an additive identity skip is a zero-weight shortcut, so the dead-direction rate at any layer is determined by the shortest 

weighted -path distance from that layer to the output, with weight edges costing 1 and skip edges costing 0. The empirical characterisation that motivated this construction is summarised below; the formal statement follows. 

Setup. A residual computational graph has nodes 𝑉 = {𝑣 0, 𝑣 1, . . . , 𝑣 𝐿 } representing pre-activations ( 𝑣 0 input, 𝑣 𝐿 output) and two kinds of edges, which may coexist at the same endpoints: 

• Weight edges 𝑊 1, . . . , 𝑊 𝐿 , each from 𝑣 𝑖 −1 to 𝑣 𝑖 , representing the linear map (optionally followed by elementwise activation 𝜙 ) of a feedforward layer. 

• Identity-skip edges skip ⊆ 𝑉 × 𝑉 , each (𝑣 𝑗 , 𝑣 𝑘 ) with 𝑘 > 𝑗 , representing the additive identity ℎ𝑘 += ℎ𝑗 . An additive-identity residual block from 𝑣 𝑗 to 𝑣 𝑘 is encoded by simultaneously having the weight-edge chain 𝑣 𝑗 → 𝑣 𝑗 +1 → · · · → 𝑣 𝑘 and the skip edge 

(𝑣 𝑗 , 𝑣 𝑘 ): the forward computation is ℎ𝑘 = 𝐹 (ℎ𝑗 ) + ℎ𝑗 where 𝐹 is the weight-chain output. Hence the computational graph can have multiple forward paths between two nodes when skips are present; the backward-delta receives a sum of contributions from each such path. The symmetric dead-aligned perturbation perturbs each weight 𝑊 𝑖 in the dead direction by 

𝑡 : 𝑊 𝑖 (𝑡 ) = 𝑊 ∗ 

> 𝑖

+ 𝑡𝑢 𝑖 𝑢 ⊤

> 𝑖 −1

, with 𝑊 ∗ 

> 𝑖

a rank-deficient singular configuration (per the setup of §A.1) and 𝑢 𝑖 the dead direction at node 𝑣 𝑖 . Skip edges are fixed identities and are not perturbed.

Remark 45 (Trained-network non-cancellation) . Lemma 42 establishes non-cancellation under the canonical symmetric perturbation 𝑊 𝑖 (𝑡 ) = 𝑊 ∗ 

> 𝑖

On a trained network whose dead direction 𝑢 is identified post-hoc as the bottom singular direction of the activation/Fisher spectrum (operational selection rule, §5.1), the canonical-trajectory bridge of Corollary 58 applies: the dead direction is the trained Fisher null direction, the path-product expansion is computed in the trained coordinates, and non-cancellation follows from the Schur-form positivity of the trained-network’s non-dead block (an empirical non-dead-block-control hypothesis adapted to the trained spectrum). Residual-stream 𝜎 min depth-invariance observed on pretrained transformers is consistent with this hypothesis: there is no leading-order cancellation at the residual stream. 

Corollaries and concrete cases. Corollary 46 (Pure feedforward) . With no residual edges, 𝐾 (ℓ) = 𝐿 − ℓ, recovering Theorem 21. 

Corollary 47 (Per-layer residuals at every layer) . If every layer is wrapped in a per-layer residual, 𝐾 (ℓ) = 0 for all ℓ (the shortest path takes every identity skip). The leading-order rate collapses to Θ(1); the rate-carrying signal degenerates to a finite-𝑡 correction. 

Corollary 48 (Single block residual over a 𝑘 -layer sub-chain) . Consider a feedforward chain of 𝐿 weight layers (weight 𝑊 𝑖 maps 𝑣 𝑖 −1 → 𝑣 𝑖 ) with exactly one additive-identity residual Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 55 

block spanning weights 𝑊 𝑝 , 𝑊 𝑝 +1, . . . , 𝑊 𝑝 +𝑘 −1, that is, a skip edge from 𝑣 𝑝 −1 (block input) to 𝑣 𝑝 +𝑘 −1 (block output) coexists with the weight chain 𝑣 𝑝 −1 → 𝑣 𝑝 → · · · → 𝑣 𝑝 +𝑘 −1. Let 

𝐾 post := 𝐿 − ( 𝑝 + 𝑘 − 1) be the post-block feedforward distance. Then the shortest-weighted-path distance is 

𝐾 (ℓ) =

Intuition: the skip lets any backward path that reaches 𝑣 𝑝 −1 bypass the entire 𝑘 -weight block via the identity edge (cost 0 instead of 𝑘 ), saving exactly 𝑘 weights. A path with target ℓ strictly inside the block ( 𝑝 ≤ ℓ ≤ 𝑝 + 𝑘 − 2) cannot use the skip (the skip’s entrance is at 𝑣 𝑝 −1, below 

ℓ’s position), so it must traverse the weights from 𝑣 𝑝 +𝑘 −1 back through 𝑊 𝑝 +𝑘 −1, . . . , 𝑊 ℓ+1 into the block, paying ( 𝑝 + 𝑘 − 1) − ℓ weights plus the post-block 𝐾 post . Verified empirically: for 

𝐿 = 4, 𝑝 = 3, 𝑘 = 2, the formula gives 𝐾 (1) = 1, 𝐾 (2) = 0, 𝐾 (3) = 1, 𝐾 (4) = 0 with predicted 

𝛼 = (2, 0, 2, 0); a parametric freeze-probe on a two-layer residual block at the chain end reports 

𝛼 = (2.20 , 0.20 , 2.02 , 0.02 ), matching up to the expected ∼ 0.2 residual-skip correction. 

Scope. Theorem 43 covers any additive-identity residual DAG. It does not cover: pre-activation residuals with learnable projection (where the skip is not pure identity), LayerNorm, attention’s softmax nonlinearity inside 𝐹 , or gated residuals (highway networks, GRU- and LSTM-style gates, Mamba-style selective skips). For the first, the projection matrix on the skip path is itself a perturbed weight, so the skip edge has cost 1 rather than 0 in the graph distance; 𝐾 (ℓ) remains integer-valued, but the rate formula 2𝐾 (ℓ) now counts the projection weights too. The formalism extends directly with this reassignment of edge costs. For gated residuals of the form 𝑋 out = (1 − 𝑔 ) ⊙ 𝑋 in + 𝑔 ⊙ 𝐹 (𝑋 in ) where the gate 𝑔 is itself learned, the skip edge cost in 𝐾 (ℓ) depends on whether 𝑔 stays Θ(1) or admits a dead-direction perturbation; a separate edge-cost analysis tracking gate factors would be required, and we do not provide it here. For LayerNorm and attention’s softmax, see the corresponding extensions below. 

Activation-side dual: 𝜎 min under residuals. The A-factor / 𝜎 min dual of Theorem 43 is obtained by mirroring the shortest-weighted-path construction on the forward graph.

Corollary 52 (Per-layer residuals: 𝜎 min preserved) . If every layer is wrapped in an identity-skip residual, then 𝐾 fwd (ℓ) = 0 for all ℓ ≥ 0 (the skip chain from 𝑣 0 reaches 𝑣 ℓ via identities only). Hence 𝜎 min (𝑋 ℓ) = Θ(√𝑁 ), constant in 𝑡 at leading order: the residual stream preserves the input 

𝜎 min signal through the entire depth. 

Remark on forward/backward duality under residuals. On pure feedforward chains, 

𝐾 fwd (ℓ − 1) = ℓ − 1 and 𝐾 (ℓ) = 𝐿 − ℓ, so 𝐾 fwd (ℓ − 1) + 𝐾 (ℓ) = 𝐿 − 1, reproducing Corollary 25. Under residuals this generalizes to 

𝜆 min ( 𝐴 ℓ) · 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ  𝑡 2(𝐾 fwd (ℓ−1)+ 𝐾 (ℓ) ) ,Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 57 

which is layer-dependent when skips are present: a layer whose input or output is bypassed by a skip sees a reduced exponent. In particular, with per-layer residuals everywhere, both 

𝐾 fwd ≡ 0 and 𝐾 ≡ 0, and the A-G product becomes Θ(1): no rate signal remains in either factor. 

Remark on biases. Corollary 58 is stated for the unbiased setup inherited from §9.2. Under the bias augmentation of Theorem 112, each bias 𝑏 ℓ contributes an additive Θ(𝑡 ) to the dead-direction pre-activation at 𝑣 ℓ (the augmented-weight column perturbation is 𝑡 ). For ℓ

with 𝐾 fwd (ℓ) ≥ 2, this bias contribution would dominate the weight-chain contribution at leading order, capping the effective forward dead-direction rate at 1. Thus the 𝜎 min corollary is not directly bias-stable in the way 𝐺 -factor rate is. In practice this matters only when 

𝐾 fwd (ℓ) ≥ 2; under per-block residuals (the pre-norm transformer case), 𝐾 fwd ≡ 0 at block outputs and biases do not disturb the rate-0 conclusion. 

Remark on LayerNorm inside residual sub-paths. Pre-norm transformer blocks take the form 𝑋 out = 𝑋 in + 𝐹 (LN (𝑋 in )) where 𝐹 = 𝑊 2 · 𝜙 (𝑊 1 · · ) is a two-weight feedforward. LN applied to 𝑋 in mean-centers and variance-normalizes row-wise; the dead channel of LN (𝑋 in ) is 

(𝑋 (ℎ) 

> in

− 𝜇 (𝑋 in ))/ 𝜎 (𝑋 in ), and because non-dead channels of 𝑋 in are Θ(1) while the dead channel is Θ(𝑡 𝐾 fwd (𝑣 in ) ), the mean 𝜇 (𝑋 in ) = Θ(1) dominates, giving LN (𝑋 in ) (ℎ) = Θ(1) regardless of the upstream rate. Propagating through 𝐹 yields 𝐹 (LN (𝑋 in )) (ℎ) = Θ(𝑡 𝑘 𝐹 ) where 𝑘 𝐹 is the number of weight layers in 𝐹 (rather than Θ(𝑡 𝐾 fwd (𝑣 in )+ 𝑘 𝐹 ) as would hold without LN). At the block output,

at rate 0 holds exactly, independent of 𝑘 𝐹 . (This analysis assumes the LN affine parameters at default 𝛾 = 1, 𝛽 = 0. Under trained 𝛾 ≠ 1, the dead direction of LN’s forward map is 

𝛾 −1/∥ 𝛾 −1 ∥ rather than 1𝑑 /√𝑑 per Proposition 63; the rate-0 skip-bypass conclusion at the residual stream is unaffected, but the dead direction itself rotates with 𝛾 .) Two distinct measurement sites must be distinguished: 

• Block output (post-residual-add, pre-next-LN): 𝜎 min (𝑋 block _out ) tracks min (𝐾 fwd (𝑣 in ), 𝑘 𝐹 ).With per-block residuals this reduces to 𝐾 fwd on the residual DAG, consistently rate 0.This matches the empirical signature where 2-, 3-, and 4-block LN stacks show 𝜎 min 

log–log slope ≈ 0 at every block output while 𝑢 ⊤𝐺𝑢 gives fractional non-integer rates in the bound-gap of Theorem 60. 

• Post-LN (inside block, at LN output): LN outputs are mean-centered row-wise, so LN (𝑋 ) ∈ 

R𝑁 ×ℎ has the uniform direction 1/√ℎ in its left-kernel: 𝜎 min (LN (𝑋 )) = 0 identically, an architectural null-space degeneracy independent of 𝑡 . Post-LN 𝜎 min is therefore not avalid rate observable without first projecting out the uniform direction and measuring 

𝜎 min on the (ℎ − 1)-dimensional range. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 58 

Consequently, the activation-side rate observable on residual+LN architectures is well-defined only at the residual-stream content (block-output sites, before the next LN application). 

Scope: post-norm LN and attention. The remark above assumes pre-norm LN (LN inside each residual block’s sub-path 𝐹 ). In post-norm architectures 𝑋 out = LN (𝑋 in + 𝐹 (𝑋 in )) , LN acts on the residual-add output, so 𝑋 out is mean-centered row-wise and 𝜎 min (𝑋 out ) = 0 identically. Post-norm stacks therefore admit no rate-bearing 𝜎 min observable at block outputs without explicit projection onto {1}⊥. Softmax-based attention inside 𝐹 does not affect Corollary 58 at block outputs (the skip bypasses 𝐹 entirely), but the in-block dead-direction rate through softmax remains open. 

Composition additivity on heterogeneous blocks. Theorem 43 establishes the 𝐾 -distance framework when the graph’s elementary operations are weight matmuls (cost 1) and identity skips (cost 0). Real architectures compose higher-level blocks (attention, MLP FFN, pre-norm residual sub-chains), each with its own internal bridge analysis. The composition rule for these is Theorem 30 (§8): for a sequential composition 𝑀 = 𝐵 𝑛 ◦ · · · ◦ 𝐵 1 with per-block backward and forward rates 𝑘 bk  

> 𝑖

, 𝑘 fwd  

> 𝑖

(Definition 29) on a shared canonical dead direction, and under the scalar-transfer hypothesis, the backward rate at the input of 𝐵 𝑖 is Í𝑗 ≥𝑖 𝑘 bk  

> 𝑗

Remark 55 (The invariant works as a stable probe of attention internal structure) . Propo-sition 54 is stable across all tested configurations: 𝑛 ∈ { 1, 4, 6} attention chains, with or without LN, 𝑑 ∈ { 16 , 64 , 128 , 768 }, 𝑛 ℎ ∈ { 1, 2, 4, 8}. The observed differences 𝛼 𝑊 𝑉 − 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 are 

2.000 ± 0.000 in every case, even where the individual rates 𝛼 𝑊 𝑉 and 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 exhibit the softmax-coupling anomalies of Remark 32. The invariant follows from the sequential 𝑊 𝑉 → 𝑊 𝑂 

structure within each attention block (one Linear’s worth of 𝑡 -factor in grad-output) and is therefore preserved under any deformation of the rest of the network that doesn’t alter the within-block sequence. This makes it a clean diagnostic observable: measuring 𝛼 𝑊 𝑉 − 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 and confirming it equals 2 is a lightweight consistency check for the attention block’s canonical structure. 

Remark 56 (Residual bypass: the softmax-coupling anomaly is moot in practical transformers) .

The softmax-coupling anomaly of Remark 32 is a property of non-residual sequential attention stacks, which do not appear in practical architectures. Every attention block in ViT, BERT, GPT, and LLaMA-family transformers is residual-wrapped: 𝑦 = 𝑥 +Attn (LN (𝑥 )) . Under Corollary 58, the residual skip dominates: 𝑘 res block = 0 at the block-output level (forward 𝐾 -distance 0 via the identity path), regardless of what happens inside Attn . Consequently, rate predictions at the residual stream of a practical transformer are governed by the skip, not by the attention internals; the softmax-coupling anomaly is bypassed entirely. The anomaly does affect the internal component rates ( 𝑊 𝑂 , 𝑊 𝑉 , 𝑊 𝑄 , 𝑊 𝐾 ) within each block, but those are not the primary predictive observables at practical scale: 𝜎 min on the residual stream is. 

Corollary 57 (Attention as a composition corollary of its standalone rate) . The attention block’s standalone backward rate 𝑘 bk attn = 2 is established in §9.4 (Theorem 67), so Theorem 30 yields the rate for any composition involving attention blocks, including residual transformer stacks (via Corollary 53 with residual-block rates being 0). The composition framework reduces the attention extension from “attention inside arbitrary architecture” to “attention block in isolation,” the smaller target that §9.4 resolves. 

Remark on block-rate canonical alignment (NDC). Theorem 30 assumes each block shares the canonical dead direction 𝑢 = 𝑒 ℎ. If blocks use different canonical coordinates (e.g., a rotation is applied between blocks), the composition rate can exceed Í𝑗 𝑘 𝑗 (misalignment breaks the factored structure). This is the Non-Dead-Canonical (NDC) assumption: the dead direction is coherent across composed blocks. Proposition 118 (the rotation case) handles the per-block rotation separately; composition with rotated blocks follows by treating each rotated block as a separate rate-carrying unit. For full-rank inputs ( 𝑟 0 = 𝑑 ), 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) = 𝜎 min and the statement reduces to 𝜎 min (𝑋 ℓ (𝜃 (𝑡 ))) = √𝑁 · Θ(𝑡 𝐾 fwd (ℓ) ). Three readings of the depth-invariance. The same content takes three useful forms. 

As a graph distance. The dead-direction rate at any node 𝑣 ℓ is determined by the shortest weighted path from 𝑣 0 to 𝑣 ℓ, with weight edges costing 1 and identity skips costing 0. With per-layer skips everywhere, every node has 𝐾 fwd (ℓ) = 0 and the rate is Θ(1) across all depth. 

As a structural-health diagnostic. The ratio 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) (𝑋 ℓ)/ 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) (𝑋 0) should remain Θ(1) across a residual transformer’s depth at any training checkpoint. A measured dip (pronounced U-shape, monotone decline) is the falsifier: it indicates the no-cancellation hypothesis breaks Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 61 

for that architecture, that the skip’s signal is being subtracted out by the residual branch in the bottom-of-active-spectrum direction. As a rate-collapse contrast. A skipless pure-attention chain collapses this observable doubly-exponentially with depth; the residual skip instead preserves it at rate 0, which is what the corollary makes precise. 

Structural status of Corollary 58. The mathematical content of Corollary 58 is elementary: with an additive-identity skip 𝑋 ℓ+1 = 𝑋 ℓ + 𝐹 (𝑋 ℓ) and a non-cancellation hypothesis on the residual branch, the bottom singular value cannot decrease at leading order. The shortest-weighted-path formalism is correct but follows from basic graph theory once the per-edge factorisation is in place. We state and label the result because it gives a single observable,   

> 𝜎 (𝑟 0)

(𝑋 ℓ)/ 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) (𝑋 0), on which the no-cancellation hypothesis is empirically falsifiable on a per-checkpoint basis; the corollary is best read as the analytic packaging of that diagnostic. The genuinely architectural content lies in the prerequisites of the bridge framework (Theorem 21, the architectural instantiations, the attention composition anomaly), which this corollary applies as a downstream observable. 

Position relative to prior rank-collapse and neural-collapse work. The same activation-side observable that Dong et al. (2021); Noci et al. (2022) establish collapses doubly-exponentially in pure-attention chains is here shown to be rate-zero preserved along any residual DAG: the identity skip is the operational mechanism that turns the rank-collapse signal into depth-invariance, recovering the empirical residual-stream stability observed in modern transformers. Neural collapse (NC1) (Papyan et al., 2020) is the special case of  

> 𝜎 min

on a class-mean matrix at the terminal phase of training; the corollary is the per-layer during-training generalisation along a residual DAG. The rank-aware 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) form keeps the prediction measurable when the input residual stream’s smallest active singular value sits near the numerical floor. 

Architectural vs training-conditional content. The rate-0 conclusion 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) (𝑋 ℓ)/ 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) (𝑋 0) =

> Θ

(1) is a property of the additive-identity skip structure: under canonical alignment, the per-edge dead-direction Jacobians have the path-wise factorisation of the proof, so the identity skip dominates the bottom-of-active-spectrum at any 𝜃 for which the no-cancellation hypothesis (Remark 59) holds. This includes random initialisation; the corollary’s rate-0 pass/fail is therefore an architectural diagnostic, not a training-signal indicator. The training-conditional content is the magnitude of the depth profile (the amplification factor between consecutive blocks), not the rate-0 conclusion itself. When using the corollary as a structural-health probe at LLM scale, the training signal is read off the magnitude, not the pass/fail of the architectural prediction.

Remark 59 (Scope at trained-network scale and the no-cancellation assumption) . The proof above uses canonical-aligned coordinates where each weight edge contributes a non-negative dead-direction factor 𝑐 𝜋 ≥ 0, so the identity skip’s contribution 1 cannot be cancelled by the residual branch. On a general trained residual network (not on the canonical trajectory) the residual branch 𝐹 ℓ in 𝑋 ℓ+1 = 𝑋 ℓ + 𝐹 ℓ (𝑋 ℓ) is not constrained to act with non-negative coefficients on a singular direction, so a sign-flipped residual contribution could in principle cancel the identity at leading order. The trained-network conclusion 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) (𝑋 ℓ+1) ≥ 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) (𝑋 ℓ) at leading order (where 𝑟 0 = rank (𝑋 0) is the input rank, as in the rank-aware corollary statement) therefore requires a generic non-cancellation assumption: there exists a constant 𝑐 > 0 such that ∥𝐹 ℓ (𝑋 ℓ)𝑣 ∥2 ≤ ( 1 − 𝑐 )∥ 𝑋 ℓ 𝑣 ∥2 does not hold for the bottom-of-active-spectrum singular direction 𝑣 of 𝑋 ℓ, equivalently, the residual branch does not partially cancel the identity in that direction with bounded relative magnitude. The link to the canonical-trajectory statement is that under the canonical approach the depth profile ℓ ↦ → 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) (𝑋 ℓ) is flat at leading order (the corollary’s "constant in 𝑡 "); on a trained network at fixed parameters the analogous statement is 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) (𝑋 ℓ) non-decreasing with depth. For full-rank inputs the rank-aware statement reduces to the literal 𝜎 min (𝑋 ℓ)/ 𝜎 min (𝑋 0) ≥ 1.The rank-aware form remains the theoretically correct generalisation when 𝑋 0 has a true numerical-zero kernel; in practice on trained transformers the boundary between "true kernel" and "precision artefact" depends on the covariance accumulator. With fp 32 accumulation, the noise floor 𝜎 max (𝑋 0) · √𝜀 fp32 rises above the bottom of the active spectrum on inputs with  

> 𝜎 max

≳ 10 5 (the regime documented in the massive-activations literature (Sun et al., 2024), related to the low-effective-rank class structure of deep-learning spectra (Papyan, 2020)), and the input reads as if numerically rank-deficient. With fp 64 accumulation the noise floor drops by eight orders of magnitude, so an input that read as rank-deficient at fp 32 resolves as full-rank and 𝜎 (𝑟 0 ) = 𝜎 min identically. The rank-aware form is the general statement; in practice it reduces to 𝜎 min as a measurement effect of fp 64 precision. The rank-aware form is the natural form of the prediction once the input rank is allowed to be 𝑟 0 ≤ 𝑑 . A rate-level guarantee under structural assumptions on 𝐹 ℓ (Lipschitz contraction, angular bounds) is left as future work. 

Scope under non-linear residual-branch wrappings (Peri-LN / sandwich-norm). The proof above factorises the dead-direction component 𝑋 (ℎ) 

> ℓ

along weighted paths in the residual graph, with each weight edge contributing a non-negative scalar factor and identity skips contributing 1. The factorisation requires the residual-branch update 𝑋 ℓ+1 = 𝑋 ℓ + 𝐹 ℓ (𝑋 ℓ) to enter additively. When the weight branch is wrapped in a non-linear normalisation operator before the residual addition (the sandwich-norm or Peri-LN block pattern (Kim et al., 2025)), the post-norm RMSNorm renormalises the sublayer output before it enters the residual stream, and the per-edge factorisation no longer applies. The corollary’s strict statement is therefore not extended to Peri-LN architectures by the proof above. A formal extension covering non-linear residual-branch wrappings is left open. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 64 

9.3 LayerNorm and the LN-kernel direction 

Residual identity skips contributed zero-weight edges to the backward chain, and the rate ladder remained graph-distance-readable. LayerNorm is different: LN (ℎ) = (ℎ − 𝜇 )/ 𝜎 is a genuinely non-linear, non-element-wise per-sample operation, and its backward Jacobian couples every coordinate to every other through the mean and variance. The per-edge factorisation that propagated the rate through residual DAGs no longer holds. Two things become available instead: a bracket on the rate (rather than an exact graph-distance formula), and a separate algebraic null direction in the post-LN covariance that is readable from the affine parameter alone, no forward pass required. This subsection proves the bracket and characterises empirically where the upper and lower bounds collapse onto a single rate; the algebraic null direction is the subject of Proposition 63. With the setup that follows, we take 𝛾 = 1, 𝛽 = 0 at the singular configuration. Unlike resid-ual identity skips (Theorem 43) which contributed zero-weight edges to the backward chain, LN’s backward Jacobian couples the dead direction to all other coordinates. This subsection states the partial result we can prove cleanly and presents an empirical characterization of where the proof technique comes up short. 

Setup. Let the base setup (square widths, single dead direction 𝑢 = 𝑒 ℎ∗ , feedforward chain) be augmented with LN operations inserted at a subset 𝑆 ⊆ { 1, . . . , 𝐿 − 1} of positions (an LN at position 𝑘 sits between the post-activation ℎ𝑘 and the weight matmul 𝑊 𝑘 +1). Define 𝐾 upper LN (ℓ) = 𝐿 − ℓ (the no-LN weight count above ℓ) and 𝐾 lower LN (ℓ) = the number of weight matmuls above ℓ that are not preceded (going backward from the output) by an LN crossing, i.e. weight matmuls strictly above the highest LN in 𝑆 that is at-or-above ℓ.

Proposition 60 (LayerNorm rate bracket, partial) . Under the LN-augmented setup, activation classes (P1)–(P3), and the symmetric single-direction approach, the dead-direction entry of the G-factor at layer ℓ satisfies 

> Ω

 𝑡 2𝐾 lower LN (ℓ)  ≤ (𝐺 ℓ (𝜃 (𝑡 ))) 𝑢 ℓ𝑢 ℓ ≤ 𝑂  𝑡 2𝐾 upper LN (ℓ) .

The upper bound recovers Theorem 21 (treating LN as a worst-case-preserving operation); the lower bound reflects that LN crossings cannot increase the dead-direction rate beyond plateau. The precise asymptotic rate within this range depends on the geometry of 𝜇, 𝜎 near the dead direction and does not reduce to a graph-distance formula in general. 

Tight regimes (where the upper and lower bounds coincide). (i) When 𝑆 ∩ { ℓ, . . . , 𝐿 } = ∅

(no LN at or above ℓ), 𝐾 lower LN (ℓ) = 𝐾 upper LN (ℓ) = 𝐿 − ℓ and the dead-direction rate is exactly 2(𝐿 − ℓ),recovering Theorem 21. (ii) When the topmost LN in 𝑆 at-or-above ℓ sits at level ℓ + 1 (an LN sits immediately above the layer being probed), 𝐾 lower LN (ℓ) = 𝐾 upper LN (ℓ) − 1 collapses to 𝐿 − ℓ − 1 for the lower-bound part counting only weights above the topmost LN, and the rate is 2(𝐿 − ℓ − 1)

at leading order. The remaining cases (LN strictly above ℓ + 1, or multiple LNs above ℓ) admit only the bracket; closing the bracket into a closed-form rate requires the finite-𝑡 refinement of Theorem 122 (§A.6.1) for the MLP case, or Conjecture 123 for the attention case. Proof sketch. Upper bound : at every backward chain step, LN’s Jacobian is a bounded operator (Lipschitz in its inputs at the singular configuration), so it cannot amplify the dead-direction component beyond its pre-LN magnitude. The feedforward rate 𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) is thus an upper bound. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 65 

Lower bound via the no-LN prefix : let 𝑘 ∗ = max {𝑘 ∈ 𝑆 : 𝑘 ≥ ℓ} (or 𝑘 ∗ = 𝐿 if no LN at or above ℓ). The backward chain from 𝛿 𝐿 down to the nearest LN crossing (if any) traverses only weight matmuls, specifically 𝐿 − 𝑘 ∗ of them. These contribute a clean 𝑡 𝐿 −𝑘 ∗

scaling to the dead-direction entry by Lemma 16. After crossing LN at 𝑘 ∗, the subsequent weight matmuls (from 𝑘 ∗ down to ℓ, i.e. 𝑘 ∗ − ℓ of them) continue to contribute their own 𝑡 -scaling on top of whatever plateau LN introduces. The dead-direction magnitude at 𝛿 ℓ is at least 𝑡 𝐾 lower LN (ℓ) where  

> 𝐾 lower LN

(ℓ) = 𝐿 − 𝑘 ∗ (the weights strictly above the topmost LN); squaring gives the claimed lower bound. □

Empirical characterization. Direct freeze-probe measurement (the static parametric pro-tocol of Appendix B.2, Table 10) at 𝐿 = 4, ℎ = 6, linear activation, four LN patterns and three activations: 

• none (control): recovers Theorem 21 rate 2(𝐿 − ℓ) exactly. 

• after_first (LN at 𝑘 = 1): measured 𝛼 = (0.06 , 4, 2, 0). For ℓ ≥ 2, no LN is in the backward chain, rate is standard. For ℓ = 1, 𝐾 lower LN (1) = 0 (LN is directly above), rate is destroyed. Lower and upper bounds for ℓ = 1 are 0 and 𝐿 − 1 = 3 respectively (i.e., the bracket 𝛼 1 ∈ [ 0, 6]); the measured 𝛼 1 = 0.06 saturates the lower bound, confirming tightness in the LN-immediately-above case (regime (ii) of Theorem 60). 

• after_last_hidden (LN at 𝑘 = 3): measured 𝛼 = (4.3, 2.3, 0.3, 0). For each ℓ: lower bound 𝐾 lower LN (ℓ) = 4 − 3 = 1 (just the one weight 𝑊 4 above the topmost LN), upper 

= 𝐿 − ℓ. Measured rates ∼ ( 4, 2, 0, 0) asymptotically (the +0.3 uniform offset is finite-𝑡 ), with 𝛼 1 = 4 exceeding the lower bound of 2: the post-LN weights 𝑊 2, 𝑊 3 contribute in practice. 

• every (LN at every hidden layer): measured 𝛼 = (2.25 , 1.32 , 0.37 , 0) in the fit window. Asymptotic behavior (from raw dead-coord traces at small 𝑡 ) shows 𝛼 → ( 1, ≈ 0, 0, 0) as  

> 𝑡

→ 0: the plateau dominates at deeper layers and only a single 𝑊 2 between the bottom two LN and layer 1 contributes a non-trivial 𝑡 -scaling. The lower bound 𝐾 lower LN (1) = 0 is loose; the actual rate is ∼ 1.

What the partial result covers and what it does not. Theorem 60 gives an exact rate whenever LN sits directly above ℓ (rate is the same as the all-weights-preserved upper bound, applied above the LN); for LN strictly above ℓ, the theorem brackets the rate between the two bounds but does not pin it down. The gap corresponds to the contributions of weights between the LN and layer ℓ: empirically these do contribute 𝑡 -scaling, but the contribution is not a clean integer exponent in general. It depends on the LN’s backward-Jacobian structure, which in turn depends on the value of (ℎ − 𝜇 )/ 𝜎 at the singular configuration. A sharper theorem (recovering a graph-distance-style formula 𝛼 ℓ = 2 · 𝐾 (ℓ) with 𝐾 counting only post-LN weights) would require either (a) a stronger assumption on the non-dead activations at the singular configuration, or (b) a refined Jacobian analysis that tracks the non-diagonal contributions through LN. Both are open problems; the empirical LayerNorm-pattern sweep constrains the form of any such refinement. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 66 

Component-wise LN rate shift. Within the LN rate-shift window for pre-norm transformer blocks the freeze-probe slope on each component 𝑐 takes the empirical form  

> 𝛼 𝑐

= 2 𝐾 (𝑐 ) + 𝑞 LN (𝑑, 𝑁, 𝑡 train ) · 𝐾 eff LN (𝑐 ),

where 𝐾 (𝑐 ) is the standard no-LN backward distance (Theorem 21), 𝑞 LN is a per-setup scalar measuring the rate shift at an MLP fc2 reference component, and 𝐾 eff LN (𝑐 ) is a component-specific effective count of LN crossings. The formula describes the finite-𝑡 crossover regime 

traversed by SGD trajectories; the strict asymptotic 𝑡 → 0 rate matches the no-LN prediction (Remark 62). 

Remark 61 (LN rate-shift formula scope: static-Fisher vs trajectory readout) . Theorem 60 and the empirical formula 𝛼 𝑐 = 2𝐾 (𝑐 ) + 𝑞 LN · 𝐾 eff LN (𝑐 ) are static-Fisher statements at the parametric trajectory. Under Adam, gauge-mode drift on the loss’s continuous symmetries (Remark 80) confounds direct rate readout from 𝑢 ⊤𝐺𝑢 inside the LN-affected sub-chain; the robust observable on Adam+CE is 𝜎 min on the residual stream (Corollary 58). 

Remark 62 (LN’s rate shift is a finite-𝑡 phenomenon, not an asymptote) . A parametric freeze-probe (set the probe block’s weights directly at a grid of 𝑡 -values, measure 𝑢 ⊤𝐺 𝑐 𝑢 at each, no SGD trajectory) reveals that at true asymptotic 𝑡 → 0, the rates under pre-norm LN match the no-LN predictions exactly. Across 36 (𝑑, 𝑛 ℎ, seed ) combinations spanning  

> 𝑑

∈ { 8, 16 , 32 , 64 , 128 , 256 , 384 , 768 } and 𝑛 ℎ ∈ { 1, 2, 4, 8}, fit on 𝑡 ≤ 10 −2, all component slopes are 𝛼 fc2 = 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 = 0.000 ± 0.000 , 𝛼 fc1 = 𝛼 𝑊 𝑉 = 𝛼 𝑊 𝑄 = 𝛼 𝑊 𝐾 = 2.000 ± 0.000 , giving  

> 𝑞 LN

= 0.000 ± 0.000 asymptotically. The non-zero 𝑞 LN observed in SGD trajectories arises in the crossover region 𝑡 ∈ [ 0.1, 1] where LN’s mean-subtraction Jacobian contributes subleading terms that dominate the local slope. SGD trajectories at finite training time typically terminate in this crossover region, so the measured rates are non-trivial but not the leading-order asymptote. The crossover region, not the 𝑡 → 0 asymptote, is where a non-zero 𝑞 LN is measured. The closed-form derivation that quantifies this crossover-region slope (and explains  

> 𝑞 LN

as an integrated local slope rather than a fundamental constant) is Theorem 122 for the MLP case and Conjecture 123 for the attention case (§A.6.1). 

Relation to Proposition 63 (LN-kernel direction on activations). Theorem 60 concerns the dead-direction rate of the G-factor under a parametric singular approach. Proposition 63 (§9.3.1) concerns the static null direction of LN’s Jacobian at any single checkpoint, which is 𝑣 ∗ = 𝛾 −1/∥ 𝛾 −1 ∥ (independent of 𝑡 ). The two are complementary observables: the bound theorem governs how a known dead direction scales along an approach, while the kernel proposition tells one how to read the dead direction off the LN affine parameter without a forward pass. On post-final-LN sites, the two combine: the kernel direction 𝛾 −1/∥ 𝛾 −1 ∥ is the dead direction at that checkpoint, and Theorem 60 bracket-bounds the corresponding G-factor entry’s rate. This combination is what makes the LN 𝜎 min fingerprint (Corollary 58) and the kernel-direction test (Proposition 63) both operationally cheap at LLM scale: 𝑢 ∗ is read from the affine parameter, and the rate is bounded above by the no-LN feedforward count. 9.3.1 Normalisation kernels: LN vs RMSNorm

The dichotomy ties to the framework’s rate primitive via the selection rule (Theorem 3, Remark 6): the LN kernel direction is structurally tangential at the post-LN node, so LN’s algebra creates a tangential direction that the rate-classification machinery would identify regardless of training. The corresponding observation for RMSNorm is the differential negative.

Remark 65 (Three readings of the LN kernel direction) . The same content takes three useful forms. As an algebraic identity. Cov (LN (𝑋 )) 𝑣 ∗ = 0 at 𝑣 ∗ = 𝛾 −1/∥ 𝛾 −1 ∥ for any input distribution 𝑋 with 𝑃𝑋 ≠ 0 a.s. The identity is exact, not asymptotic, and depends only on LN’s mean-subtraction projector 𝑃 and the affine 𝛾 . As a forward-pass-free dead direction. 

The selection rule of Theorem 3 classifies eigenvalues of the Fisher metric into tangential and transversal groups by their rate exponents along an approach. The LN kernel 𝑣 ∗ is structurally tangential at the post-LN node: it lives in ker Cov (LN (𝑋 )) algebraically, and any rate-classifier applied to the spectrum identifies it as a tangential (gauge) direction regardless of the trajectory or training state. The direction is readable from 𝛾 alone: no forward pass, no gradient, no sampling. As a falsifiable architectural dichotomy. LN admits the kernel by virtue of its mean-subtraction; RMSNorm does not (Proposition 63(b)). The empirical test is direct: read 𝑣 ∗ = 𝛾 −1/∥ 𝛾 −1 ∥ off any LN-equipped model, compute the centered-covariance bottom singular direction 𝑢 ⊥ on a calibration batch, and measure | cos (𝑢 ⊥, 𝑣 ∗)| . LN models pass at 

| cos | ≥ 0.988 ; RMSNorm models fail at | cos | ≤ 0.17 at the same coordinate. The bound above is asymptotic; at the finite 𝑡 where SGD trajectories sit, the LayerNorm rate enters a crossover region whose closed form on the LN-equipped MLP block is given in Appendix A.6. 

9.4 Single-head attention 

Attention sits outside the bridge framework’s standard hypotheses on two counts. The softmax nonlinearity is not element-wise, so it falls outside activation classes (P1)–(P3). The bilinear  

> 𝑄𝐾 ⊤

structure couples 𝑊 𝑄 and 𝑊 𝐾 inside the softmax, so the dead-direction Jacobian no longer factorises through independent per-weight columns. The natural question is whether a closed-form rate survives. We answer in the affirmative for a standalone single-head block: the forward and backward block rates are both 𝑘 = 2, with the softmax contributions appearing only at sub-leading order. This subsection proves the standalone forward and backward block rates of a single-head self-attention block under canonical init, closing the attention extension at the single-block level. Combined with Theorem 30 (with the 𝑛 ≥ 4 scope caveat noted below), the single-block rate suffices for many compositions involving attention. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 69

Corollary 68 (Attention composition rates with anomaly scope) . Setting 𝑘 bk attn = 2 in The-orem 30, the heterogeneous composition rates on 𝑛 attn attention blocks and 𝑛 mlp two-weight MLP blocks in sequence (no residual) are 𝛼 = 2(𝑛 attn + 𝑛 mlp ) at the input, with internal rates 

𝛼 ℓ = 2(𝐿 − ℓ) for 𝐿 the total number of blocks. Block-composition validation at 𝑛 = 2 across the four ordered attention/MLP block pairs ( 10 seeds per configuration with seed std < 0.001 )matches the prediction to 4 decimals. 

𝑛 ≥ 4 scope caveat. Composition additivity holds for 𝑛 attn ≤ 3 in pure-attention chains; at 

𝑛 attn ≥ 4, softmax cross-block coupling produces a measurable deviation from naive additivity. The structural prediction is closed in Proposition 69: three closed-form formulas (the 𝑊 𝑉 offset and the 𝑊 𝑄 = 𝑊 𝐾 rate parameter-free; the 𝑊 𝑂 rate matching once its saturation depth 𝑘 ★ is fit) matching parametric data to integer slope precision across the tested 𝑁 ∈ { 4, 6} and probe positions 𝑝 ∈ { 0, 2, 3, 5}. The phenomenology ( 𝑊 𝑂 saturation at 𝛼 ≈ 8, 𝑊 𝑄 /𝑊 𝐾 input-position scaling, 𝛼 𝑊 𝑉 − 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 = 2 invariant) is what the theorem derives from a unified mechanism. On practical residual-wrapped transformers the anomaly is bypassed because the residual skip gives 

𝐾 fwd = 0 regardless of attention chain depth (Corollary 58).

Conjecture 70 (Cross-configuration validity of the attention-chain rate formulas) . The closed-form expressions of Proposition 69 parts (b), (c), and the un-saturated half of (a) hold for every (𝑑, 𝑛 ℎ, seq _len ) in the practical attention regime ( 𝑑 ≥ 8, 𝑛 ℎ ≥ 1, seq _len ≥ 2, dot-product softmax with 1/√𝑑 normalisation, canonical alignment). The saturation depth 𝑘 ★(𝑑, 𝑛 ℎ, seq _len )

is finite and bounded above by min (seq _len −1, 𝑛 ℎ · seq _len ) (the rank of the cumulative softmax-Jacobian product). Both claims are validated at (𝑑, 𝑛 ℎ, seq _len ) = (16 , 2, 4) to integer slope precision; cross-configuration validation and a closed-form expression for 𝑘 ★ are open. 

Remark 71 (Scope of Proposition 69) . Components (b), (c), and the un-saturated half of (a) are derived from the bridge framework. The saturation onset 𝑘 ★ in (a) is empirical: its quantitative depth bound is not derived, and closing it is the analytical residual (Remark 72). Cross-configuration validity is the subject of Conjecture 70; the formulas are config-specific in the constant 𝑘 ★ but config-independent in the structural form. 

Remark 72 (Open: closed-form for 𝑘 ★(𝑑, 𝑛 ℎ, seq _len )). The saturation depth 𝑘 ★ in Proposi-tion 69(a) is established empirically at (𝑑, 𝑛 ℎ, seq _len ) = (16 , 2, 4) as 𝑘 ★ = 2. A closed-form expression in terms of these parameters is open: it requires sharpening the cumulative-Jacobian rank-collapse mechanism into a quantitative depth bound, which would compose attention-rank-collapse rates (Dong et al., 2021; Noci et al., 2022) with the dead-channel projection. The structural prediction (the three formulas (a)–(c) with 𝑘 ★ as a parameter) is independent of the closed form and is the operationally useful statement; per-architectural-configuration 𝑘 ★ can be measured from a single 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 (𝑘 )-vs-𝑘 sweep. 

Remark 73 (Resolution of Remark 32’s three empirical findings) . The empirical signatures listed in Remark 32 are derived consequences of Proposition 69: 

• 𝑊 𝑂 saturation at 𝛼 ≈ 8 for 𝑛 − 1 − ℓ ≥ 3: from (a), 𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 ( 𝑝, 𝑁 ) = 4 min (𝑘, 2) at 𝑘 ★ = 2; the saturated value is 4 · 2 = 8.

• 𝑊 𝑄 , 𝑊 𝐾 rates scale with input position 𝑝 rather than output position 𝑘 : from (c), 

𝛼 𝑊 𝑄 ( 𝑝, 𝑁 ) = 4𝑝 + 2 depends only on 𝑝 .

• 𝛼 𝑊 𝑉 −𝛼 𝑊 𝑂 = 2 invariant: from (b), preserved under saturation as a within-block ordering consequence. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 73 

Scope. Theorems 66 and 67 cover single-head self-attention under canonical init with shared dead direction across {𝑊 𝑄 , 𝑊 𝐾 , 𝑊 𝑉 , 𝑊 𝑂 }. Extensions: 

• Multi-head, shared dead direction across heads. Each head has its own 𝑄, 𝐾, 𝑉 pro-jections. Under the assumption that the same dead direction 𝑢 = 𝑒 𝑑 acts on each head (a canonical-construction property, not generic), per-head analysis yields rate 

2 per head, and concatenation preserves it. A parametric freeze-probe sweep across  

> 𝑑

∈ { 16 , 32 , 64 , 128 , 256 , 384 , 768 } and 𝑛 ℎ ∈ { 1, 2, 4, 8} (all 𝑑 ℎ = 𝑑 /𝑛 ℎ ≥ 2), 3 seeds per configuration (72 combinations total), gives asymptotic rate (fit on 𝑡 ≤ 10 −2) of 

2.000 ± 0.000 on 𝑊 𝑄 , 𝑊 𝐾 , 𝑊 𝑉 across all configurations. 

• Multi-head, per-head independent dead directions. If each head carries its own dead direction in its 𝑑 ℎ-dimensional sub-block, the multi-direction theorem (Theorem 106) applies per head with 𝑚 ≤ 𝑛 ℎ directions; rates are independent across heads. Not separately tested at multi-head scale at the time of writing; natural follow-up. 

• Cross-attention with 𝑋 𝑞 ≠ 𝑋 𝑘 . The bilinear 𝑄𝐾 ⊤ entry (𝑄𝐾 ⊤)𝑛,𝑚 contains 𝑡 2 (𝑋 𝑞 )𝑛,𝑑 (𝑋 𝑘 )𝑚,𝑑 

at the dead channel; the rate-2 forward propagation extends if both E[( 𝑋 𝑞 )2 

> 𝑛,𝑑

] and 

E[( 𝑋 𝑘 )2 

> 𝑚,𝑑

] are Θ(1). If only one of 𝑋 𝑞 , 𝑋 𝑘 has a dead-channel contribution (e.g., a static encoder-side dead direction with no decoder-side counterpart), the rate degenerates to a single-side factor. 

• Masked / causal attention. 𝑆 𝑛,𝑚 is set to −∞ for 𝑚 > 𝑛 ; the structure of 𝐴 0 changes but the 𝑡 -scaling argument is unaffected. Rate 2 holds. 

• Residual attention (transformer block). Combined with Theorem 43, the residual skip dominates and the attention block’s 𝑘 attn = 2 is absorbed into 𝐾 fwd = 0 at block outputs. Standard for pre-norm transformers. 

• Position encodings (RoPE, learnable absolute, learnable relative). These compose with at-tention as natural follow-ups; closing each requires verifying that the position-encoding nonlinearity (e.g., RoPE phase mixing) preserves the dead-direction’s 𝑡 -scaling, which we have not done. The Gaussian input assumption is used only to ensure the ¯𝑋 𝑛,𝑑 second moment is Θ(1)

uniformly; any input distribution with E[𝑋 2 

> 𝑛,𝑑

] = Θ(1) and finite fourth moment suffices. 

Remark 74 (Static-Fisher rate vs. Adam +CE attention trajectory) . Theorems 66 and 67 are static-Fisher statements at the parametric trajectory. On a transformer block trained with Adam +CE, the per-component trajectory rate readout is doubly confounded: by the empirically-observed Adam non-equivariance under the CE-shift and ReLU-rescaling gauges (Remark 80), and by softmax cross-block coupling at 𝑛 ≥ 4 pure-attention chains (Remark 32). The robust transformer-scale observable is therefore residual-stream 𝜎 min (Corollary 58), not  

> 𝑢 ⊤𝐺𝑢

on attention weights.

(c) 𝛼 𝑊 𝑄 = 𝛼 𝑊 𝐾 = 4𝑝 + 2: the forward chain has no saturation; the rate scales with input position 𝑝 , not output position. (𝑑, 𝑛 ℎ, seq _len ) = (16 , 2, 4) to integer-slope precision.

The canonical-alignment assumption is WLOG in the linear case at any depth (rotation invariance), but is a genuine restriction in the nonlinear case at 𝐿 ≥ 3, where element-wise 𝜙 couples non-canonical directions through the forward-backward chain, breaking the dead-direction Schur complement reduction (Lemma 17) used in the proof. 𝐿 at the cost of stronger hypotheses. Joint-scope status of the closed extensions: Theorem 103 (rectangular widths) closes any 𝐿 , and Theorem 106 (multi-direction with asymmetric per-layer exponents) closes multi-rank singular configurations; these commute (the rectangular Schur reduction acts on the non-dead block, the multi-direction Schur reduction acts on the dead block) and the joint statement holds. Genuinely open: non-canonical dead directions at 

𝐿 ≥ 3 under nonlinear activations (Proposition 119’s negative result; near-canonical continuity remains open per Corollary 120). 

KL order scales with depth. The KL order 𝑘 = 𝐿 along the symmetric approach arises because activating a dead direction through the depth-𝐿 chain requires 𝐿 coordinated per-turbations (one per layer). Each factor contributes 𝑂 (𝑡 ); their product gives 𝑂 (𝑡 𝐿 ) in the effective weight and 𝑂 (𝑡 2𝐿 ) in the KL divergence. For 𝐿 = 2, this recovers 𝑘 = 2 (deep-linear reduced-rank-regression merge, analogous to the Gaussian mixture merge at 𝑘 = 2). For Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 76 

general 𝐿 , the KL order scales linearly with depth.

has fixed sign along the entire approach trajectory, so 𝜙 ′ (𝑎 (ℎ)

A formal distributional-derivative treatment for non-aligned dead directions would be an interesting extension. K-FAC approximation quality. The theorem uses the K-FAC factorization 𝐹 ℓ ≈ 𝐴 ℓ ⊗ 𝐺 ℓ. For linear models this is exact. For smooth and ReLU activations under MSE loss with Gaussian noise, 𝐷 𝑥 and 𝜀 are independent ( 𝜀 ⊥ 𝑥 ), so the factorization is exact at leading order. Under general loss (cross-entropy), K-FAC becomes an approximation; the error introduced is of order Cov (𝜙 ′ (ℎ)2, ∥𝑔 head ∥2), bounded and not affecting the leading-order rate. Empirically, close match across activations and losses (Tables 1, 5, 4) confirms the factorization is accurate near singularities. 

Regime axes that determine the trajectory exponent. Theorem 21 fixes the trajectory exponent 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ) under a specific combination of conditions: noisy target (𝜀 ≠ 0), canonical-aligned symmetric perturbation, transversal singular minimum, isotropic Gaussian input, MSE loss, and (P1)/(P2)/(P3) activation. Changing any of these conditions can shift the exponent. Remark 23 (in the body) covers the noisy/noise-free axis explicitly (a 

+2𝐿 exponent shift). The behaviour under non-canonical alignment at 𝐿 ≥ 3 with nonlinear activations is given a negative result by Proposition 119; near-canonical continuity at finite perturbation angle is open (Corollary 120). The behaviour under non-trivial preconditioners (Adam-like, sign-only, K-FAC, Muon, Shampoo) is the empirical landscape of the optimizer-characterisation appendix; closed-form rate predictions for each preconditioner family remain Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 77 

open. The remaining regime axes (initialization balance, sampling regime, data distribution, transient vs asymptotic behaviour, singularity type at 𝜃 ∗) each contribute a further dimension to the cell space; the present theorems populate the cell where these are all in their canonical sub-cases: balanced init under gradient flow, full-batch or large-batch SGD, isotropic Gaussian input, asymptotic 𝑡 → 0, transversal singularity. A full enumeration of which combinations are closed-form derivable versus require empirical characterisation is future work. Part IV The optimizer landscape 

The rate so far is a statement about geometry: it lives on the loss landscape, independent of how a network reaches a singularity. Training is dynamics, run by a particular optimiser, and real networks carry continuous symmetries (weight rescalings, logit shifts, rotations) that leave the function unchanged while moving the parameters. This part makes the rate respect those symmetries, then asks which optimisers let a practitioner read it off an actual trajectory. Section 10 settles the geometry: the directional rate descends to the gauge quotient Θ/𝐺 ,with the same KL-order exponent regardless of which gauge representative a run happens to pick. Section 11 turns to the dynamics: SGD on a 𝐺 -invariant metric realises the quotient rate as gradient flow, Adam’s per-coordinate preconditioner breaks the 𝐺 -equivariance the readout depends on, and a dead-direction conditioner (DDCAdam) restores it by construction. The exponent is the same bridge invariant throughout; the question here is whether the optimiser preserves it. 

Reach. The scope narrows once more, from architecture to optimiser. The quotient Fisher rate is a metric-level statement and holds for any continuous Lie group symmetry 𝐺 of the loss, with its exponent equal to the KL order of the dead direction. Reading that rate off a training run is conditional: it requires the optimiser to be 𝐺 -equivariant. SGD on a 

𝐺 -invariant metric qualifies, and DDCAdam qualifies by construction for the architectural gauge classes of §7; standard Adam does not, because the per-coordinate 1/√ˆ𝑣 does not commute with non-axis-aligned gauge actions. A closed-form rate modifier for standard Adam on the alignment-rotated manifold is left open. Standard Adam is the optimiser in widest practical use, so this gap directly limits how broadly the trajectory readout applies. Equivariance is necessary but not sufficient: even with an equivariant optimiser, reading the rate off a run additionally requires the trajectory to be in a compression phase (§12.1), since an equivariant optimiser preserves the rate but does not, on its own, make a network approach the singularity. 

# 10 Quotient Fisher rate 

Both information geometry and singular learning theory carry a notion of gauge invariance. In Amari’s framework, a continuous Lie group 𝐺 acting on Θ leaves the family { 𝑝 𝜃 } invariant when its orbits lie in level sets of 𝑝 𝜃 ; the Fisher metric is then 𝐺 -invariant, and the metric quotient Θ/𝐺 is a Riemannian submersion. In Watanabe’s, gauge orbits are precisely smooth singular fibres of Σ𝑇 . The two notions agree. This section extends the rate theorem to the gauge quotient: the directional Fisher rate is well-defined on Θ/𝐺 , and its exponent is intrinsic to the KL geometry, independent of the gauge representative chosen (Corollary 78). SGD on a 𝐺 -invariant metric is gradient flow on the quotient and realises the rate (Corollary 79); 78 Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 79 

we make this precise via two worked examples (cross-entropy logit shift; ReLU rescaling). The Adam-side scope, where the per-coordinate preconditioner breaks 𝐺 -equivariance, is the subject of the following section. 

10.1 Quotient Fisher rate: gauge orbit as smooth fiber 

The Fisher rate theorem extends to the gauge quotient Θ/𝐺 as a corollary of the selection rule (Theorem 3): the gauge orbit 𝐺 ·𝜃 0 is a smooth submanifold of the singular set 𝑆 = {𝜃 : 𝑝 𝜃 = 𝑝 ∗},and the selection rule applied with ˜𝑆 chosen as the gauge orbit and 𝑛 chosen as a horizontal direction yields the rate on the horizontal subspace, equivalently on the quotient. A trajectory on Θ projects to a trajectory on the quotient that realises this rate when its optimizer is 

> 𝐺

-equivariant (sufficient condition, Corollary 79); non-equivariant optimizers pick up a projection bias that obstructs direct rate readout (Remark 80). SGD on a 𝐺 -invariant metric is equivariant; Adam’s diagonal preconditioner generically is not, which grounds the gauge-mode drift behaviour characterised in Remark 80. 

Setup. Let 𝐺 be a Lie group acting smoothly, freely, and properly on a parameter open set  

> Θ◦

with loss 𝐿 : Θ◦ → R and model { 𝑝 𝜃 } both 𝐺 -invariant; we assume 𝜃 0 ∈ Θ◦ lies on the principal orbit type, so the action remains free in a neighbourhood of 𝜃 0 (orbit-type strata where the action degenerates require equivariant stratification machinery and are deferred). The quotient ¯Θ := Θ◦/𝐺 is a smooth manifold of dimension dim Θ − dim 𝐺 , and 𝜋 : Θ◦ → ¯Θ is a smooth submersion. Fix a 𝐺 -equivariant horizontal distribution 𝐻 𝜃 ⊂ 𝑇 𝜃 Θ transversal to the vertical 𝑉 𝜃 := 𝑇 𝜃 (𝐺 · 𝜃 ).

Choice of horizontal distribution by gauge. The natural choice is the orthogonal com-plement under a 𝐺 -invariant Riemannian metric on Θ◦:

• CE-shift gauge (𝐺 = R acting by translation on a coordinate block, e.g. adding a constant to all unembed logits): translation is an isometry of the Euclidean metric, so the standard Euclidean orthogonal complement is 𝐺 -invariant and provides 𝐻 𝜃 directly. 

• ReLU-rescaling gauge (𝐺 = (R+)𝐿 −1 acting on adjacent layer pairs (𝑊 ℓ, 𝑊 ℓ+1) by 

(𝑐𝑊 ℓ, 𝑐 −1𝑊 ℓ+1)): the action is not isometric for the Euclidean metric (it scales paired blocks oppositely), so the Euclidean orthogonal complement is not 𝐺 -invariant. One must instead use a 𝐺 -invariant Riemannian metric on Θ◦: natural choices are the Fisher information metric itself (which is 𝐺 -invariant by the same 𝐺 -invariance of 

> 𝑝 𝜃

) or a log-coordinate metric of the kind used by Tanaka and Kunin (2021) for kinetic-symmetry analysis. The asymptotic Fisher rate ( Θ(·) statement) is independent of the specific choice; the SGD trajectory-rate corollary below uses the 𝐺 -invariance of the metric to identify projected dynamics with gradient flow on the quotient.

Discrete vs. continuous SGD: Discrete SGD with step 𝜂 inherits this projection up to 𝑂 (𝜂 2) corrections that fail to be horizontal (a standard Euler-discretization bias for stochastic Riemannian flow); these corrections vanish in the small-step limit and are dominated by the canonical-aligned trajectory’s leading rate. Remark 80 (Adam’s non-equivariance and why the quotient rate is not Adam-trajecto-ry-readable) . Corollary 79’s projection step requires the optimizer’s update rule 𝑈 (𝜃, ∇𝐿 )

to be 𝐺 -equivariant: for every ℎ ∈ 𝐺 , (𝑑ℎ ) · 𝑈 (𝜃, ∇𝐿 (𝜃 )) = 𝑈 (ℎ · 𝜃, ∇𝐿 (ℎ · 𝜃 )) . SGD with  

> 𝑈

(𝜃, ∇𝐿 ) = −𝜂 ∇𝐿 satisfies this trivially because −𝜂 ∇𝐿 is linear in ∇𝐿 and 𝐺 acts compatibly on gradients. Adam’s diagonal preconditioner 𝑈 (𝜃, ∇𝐿 ) = −𝜂 · ∇ 𝐿 (√ˆ𝑣 + 𝜖 ) is not 𝐺 -equivariant in general. The two gauge mechanisms studied in this paper produce structurally distinct non-equivariance: 

• CE-shift gauge : the symmetry direction is a sum over output coordinates of the unembed weight 𝑊 3 (adding a constant to all logit rows). Adam’s per-coordinate ˆ𝑣 rescales each summand separately, so the summed direction is no longer preserved. 

• ReLU-rescaling gauge : the symmetry is a product over paired blocks (𝑊 ℓ, 𝑊 ℓ+1). Adam’s diagonal preconditioner rescales each block independently, breaking the product invari-ant. These are different mechanisms operating on different gauge groups; they share the structural feature that Adam’s per-coordinate ˆ𝑣 does not commute with non-coordinate-aligned gauge actions. Consequently, the projected Adam trajectory 𝜋 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) is not gradient flow on ¯Θ,and Corollary 79’s sufficient condition for trajectory rate-readout fails: this is a structural non-applicability of the rate-readout along an Adam trajectory. What an Adam trajectory does instead is gauge-mode drift at empirically-observed am-plitudes, rather than approach to the singular minimum; this is established as an empirical observation with the structural mechanism sketched here. We do not derive a rate modifier for standard Adam itself: the route taken by Algorithm 2 and Proposition 83 is to replace standard Adam’s per-coordinate preconditioner with a 𝐺 -equivariant variant (DDCAdam) for which Corollary 86 gives the trajectory-rate readout directly. Characterising standard (non-equivariant) Adam’s quantitative rate modifier, as opposed to constructing a 𝐺 -equivariant alternative, remains open. 

Per-coordinate non-equivariance: trajectory effects. The trajectory consequences depend on which signal the analytic gradient is small on: 

• Gauge-redundant losses. On a continuous loss symmetry where the analytic gradient is exactly zero, 1/√ˆ𝑣 amplifies the FP +finite-batch-noise floor non-uniformly across the symmetry direction’s coordinates, producing drift in the gauge mode that is absent under SGD (Adam +CE under the logit-shift gauge). 

• Non-canonical regimes (e.g., ReLU MLPs). On architectures where canonical preservation of the dead direction is not automatic, Adam-like preconditioning rotates the trajectory off the canonical dead-direction manifold over training. Whether a different observable, hyperparameter regime, or alignment-evolution model recovers a clean trajectory rate under such preconditioners is open. The mechanism is the trajectory’s relationship to canonical alignment: on canonical-aligned trajectories the rate prediction is preserved across preconditioner families. The unifying picture: the analytic gradient on the relevant signal is small (zero on a gauge orbit, small on the alignment-preserving update direction in a ReLU MLP), the finite-batch / Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 82 

finite-precision noise is non-zero, and Adam’s per-coordinate preconditioner amplifies that noise non-uniformly. Trajectory-rate readability under non-SGD optimizers requires either a 

> 𝐺

-equivariant preconditioner (constructed below as DDCAdam; DePavia et al., 2025 instead restore equivariance by reparameterising the objective) or alignment-preservation control on the trajectory in the non-canonical case. 

Constructive recovery of the rate readout on the gauge case (DDCAdam). For the architectural gauge classes of §7 (CE row-shift, CE shift bias, ReLU rescaling, LayerNorm scale), rate-readability is recovered constructively by Algorithm 2 (DDCAdam), an Adam-family preconditioner whose update map is 𝐺 -equivariant by Proposition 83; this is an equivariant alternative, not a rate modifier for standard Adam (which remains open, above). Corollary 86 then gives the trajectory-rate readout 𝑢 ⊤ ¯𝐹𝑢 𝑡 = Θ(¯𝑡 2(𝑘 −1) ) on the projected DDCAdam trajectory, the same rate as SGD on a 𝐺 -invariant metric (Corollary 79). DDCAdam is therefore theorem-compatible for trajectory rate-fits in the same regime as SGD on the supported gauge classes. What remains open after this closure is the non-canonical-regime case: closed-form deriva-tion of the per-trajectory rate on the alignment-rotated manifold is open work. Corollary 78 remains a statement about Fisher geometry. Restoring Adam-family trajec-tory rate-readability requires either (i) a 𝐺 -equivariant preconditioner (constructed for the architectural gauges of §7 by Algorithm 2 (DDCAdam) and shown to satisfy the rate readout in Corollary 86), or (ii) gauge-fixing the loss at training time. The auxiliary 𝑧 -loss penalty (Shazeer et al., 2018; Zoph et al., 2022) (a softmax-normaliser regulariser, distinct from the same-named spherical-family loss of de Brébisson and Vincent, 2016) adds a coercive term  

> 𝛼

E𝑥 [( log 𝑍 )2] that softly fixes a gauge section by penalising log-partition drift, suppressing logit-shift drift to 𝑂 (1/𝛼 ) rather than removing the gauge entirely; combined with an opti-mizer whose residual non-equivariance under the remaining symmetries (ReLU rescaling, etc.) is bounded, this is the practical route to rate-compatible CE training under adaptive optimizers without the explicit gauge spec required by route (i). 

Worked example 1: softmax cross-entropy and Z-loss as a gauge section. For supervised classification with softmax+CE on a 𝐶 -class problem with output bias 𝑏 𝐿 ∈ R𝐶 , the gauge group is 𝐺 = R acting by (𝑊 𝐿 , 𝑏 𝐿 ) ↦ → ( 𝑊 𝐿 , 𝑏 𝐿 + 𝑐 · 1𝐶 ) (and trivially on all other parameters). The action is free and proper; the vertical subspace 𝑉 𝜃 ⊂ 𝑇 𝜃 Θ is one-dimensional, spanned by the parameter-space direction 𝜕 /𝜕𝑏 𝐿 in the 1𝐶 component. The quotient ¯Θ has codimension 1

and is identified with any smooth 1-codimensional section of Θ◦ transversal to the orbit, e.g. the slice {𝑏 (𝐶 ) 

> 𝐿

= 0} (fix the last-class bias to zero), the slice {1⊤𝑏 𝐿 = 0} (zero-mean bias), or the slice {log Í𝑐 exp 𝑧 𝑐 (𝑥 0; 𝜃 ) = 0} at a fixed reference input 𝑥 0. The Euclidean metric on Θ is 

> 𝐺

Z-loss (or under SGD with any explicit hard gauge fix), the rate of 𝜆 min ( ¯𝐹 ) along the canonical-aligned horizontal approach equals ¯𝑡 2(𝑘 −1) at KL order 𝑘 , the same rate as the MSE bridge of Theorem 21. Under Adam on Z-loss, the CE-shift gauge is removed but the residual non-equivariance under other continuous symmetries (ReLU rescaling, LN scale) remains; Remark 80 predicts that drift persists; logit-level gauge-fixing under Adam reduces but does not eliminate the gauge-mode drift. 

Worked example 2: ReLU rescaling. For a network with ReLU activations between weight layers 𝑊 ℓ, 𝑊 ℓ+1, the rescaling (𝑊 ℓ, 𝑊 ℓ+1) ↦ → ( 𝑐𝑊 ℓ, 𝑐 −1𝑊 ℓ+1) for 𝑐 > 0 leaves the network function unchanged. The full ReLU gauge group on an 𝐿 -layer chain is 𝐺 = (R+)𝐿 −1, acting block-diagonally on adjacent weight pairs. The action is free and proper on the open subset where adjacent weight matrices are non-zero. Corollary 78 applies on the quotient ¯Θ =

> Θ◦

/( R+)𝐿 −1; the bridge rate 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ) of Theorem 21 is a horizontal-direction rate on this quotient (the canonical-aligned approach implicitly fixes a smooth section: the symmetric perturbation 𝑊 ∗ 

> ℓ

+ 𝑡 · 𝑒 ℎ𝑒 ⊤ 

> ℎ

at fixed-norm canonical 𝑊 ∗ 

> ℓ

picks one orbit representative per quotient point, so Theorem 21’s statement is Corollary 78 applied to that section). The Euclidean metric is not 𝐺 -invariant for this action, so Corollary 79 applies only up to the prefactor caveat: the asymptotic order Θ(¯𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ) is preserved but the prefactor is rescaled by the relative-metric Jacobian. The ReLU-rescaling gauge-mode drift under Adam is non-trivial relative to the SGD baseline, consistent with Adam’s non-equivariance under this group. 

Remark 81 (Connection to Noether and kinetic-symmetry-breaking analyses) . The conserved-charge analyses of Kunin et al. (2021) (Noether-style conservation laws for SGD on 𝐺 -invariant losses) and Tanaka and Kunin (2021) (Lagrangian formulation of kinetic symmetry breaking when the optimizer’s metric breaks the loss’s symmetry) characterise the same optimizer-symmetry interaction on the parameter side. Corollary 78 characterises it on the Fisher / observable side: under SGD the conserved charge is identically zero on the projected trajectory and the quotient rate is read directly; under Adam the kinetic symmetry breaks the conservation law, the projected dynamics acquires a non-equivariance bias, and the rate is not directly accessible. The two pictures are complementary: Noether for the dynamics, the quotient rate corollary for the geometric observable. 

# 11 Adam non-equivariance and its constructive closure 

Corollary 79 gives the sufficient condition for trajectory rate-readout: a 𝐺 -equivariant opti-miser projects to gradient flow on the quotient, so the rate exponent reads directly along the trajectory. SGD on a 𝐺 -invariant metric satisfies it; Adam, AdamW, and most adaptive optimisers in practice do not. A closed-form rate modifier for standard Adam on the alignment-rotated manifold would extend the bridge to Adam-class dynamics; deriving one is open analytical work, not pursued here. This section takes the constructive route instead: we replace Adam’s per-coordinate normalisation with a 𝐺 -equivariant alternative that satisfies the sufficient condition by design. We first establish the structural reason Adam fails, then construct DDCAdam , an Adam-family preconditioner whose update map is 𝐺 -equivariant for the architectural gauge classes of §7 (CE row-shift, ReLU rescale, LayerNorm scale). DDCAdam is the Adam instance of a broader dead-direction conditioner (DDC) family: the Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 84 

same vertical–horizontal orbit decomposition lifts any base preconditioner to a 𝐺 -equivariant variant. Adam’s per-coordinate preconditioner −𝜂 ∇𝐿 /( √ˆ𝑣 + 𝜖 ) does not commute with non-axis-aligned group actions: a rotation of parameter space mapping one gauge representative to another sends Adam’s preconditioned step to a different vector than the corresponding Adam step in the rotated frame. The asymmetry shows up along the trajectory in three ways: gauge-mode drift on cross-entropy losses (the logit-shift gauge), where Adam amplifies finite-sample noise on the gauge orbit by about 100 × over SGD; alignment rotation in non-canonical regimes (ReLU MLPs), where over training the preconditioner pulls the trajectory off the canonical dead-direction manifold, a drift absent under the 𝐺 -invariant-metric SGD of Corollary 79; and destabilisation of the rate readout itself, since the quotient theorem’s gradient-flow-projection condition fails. We name these mechanisms and scope their consequences. 

11.1 Constructive equivariant Adam: DDCAdam 

Construction: DDCAdam. A constructive resolution of the open question in Remark 80 requires an Adam-family preconditioner whose update rule is 𝐺 -equivariant for the architec-tural gauges of §9.1 (CE row-shift) and §A.1 (ReLU rescaling), and §9.3 (LayerNorm scale). 

DDCAdam is one such construction. The general principle is to decompose the second-moment estimator ˆ𝑣 into an orbit-collapsed vertical component (one scalar per gauge-orbit dimension) and a per-coordinate horizontal component, computed on a 𝐺 -invariant frame. The vertical component is then updated under one of three modes (frozen, SGD, or Adam) while the horizontal component runs standard per-coordinate Adam on the horizontally-projected gradient.

Multi-gauge composition. When several gauge specifications act on disjoint parameter blocks (e.g. CE row-shift on the unembed plus ReLU rescale on each adjacent MLP pair), the algorithm is applied per block with that block’s spec; parameters not covered by any spec receive standard per-coordinate AdamW. Block-disjointness is required for this per-block composition; non-trivially intersecting gauges (e.g. the chained ReLU rescale, where adjacent factors share a layer) require a single joint spec ( ChainedReLURescaleGauge below), not a Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 86 

composition of the pairwise specs.

The argument is not per-factor: adjacent rescales share a layer, so an individual pairwise sum log 𝜌 ℓ + log 𝜌 ℓ+1 is not invariant, only the global zero-mean/total-sum split is. Remark 84 (Numerical check) . A direct numerical check confirms the equivariance for all five gauge specs of Algorithm 2’s catalogue: starting from gauge-related initial conditions 

(𝑊 (0) , ℎ · 𝑊 (0) ) and applying DDCAdam with synchronised per-step gradients, the trajectories stay on the same gauge orbit to the level of accumulated round-off (relative residual ∼10 −7

at fp32, ∼10 −15 at fp64), as Proposition 83 predicts. 

Remark 85 (Relationship to DePavia et al. (2025)) . DePavia et al. (2025) show that Adam’s coordinate-wise preconditioner is not equivariant to rotations of parameter space, and restore equivariance by reparameterising the objective: a fixed change of basis applied around an unmodified optimiser. DDCAdam takes the complementary, preconditioner-level route (Proposition 83): an explicit closed-form equivariant construction for the architectural gauge classes of §7, with the orbit-collapsed second-moment estimator as the load-bearing design choice. 

Corollary 86 (Trajectory rate under DDCAdam) . Let 𝐿 : Θ → R be a 𝐺 -invariant loss for one of the gauge classes covered by Proposition 83. Let 𝜃 (𝑡 ) be the trajectory of the DDCAdam optimiser with hyperparameters (𝜂, 𝛽 1, 𝛽 2, 𝜖, 𝜆, vmode ) initialised at 𝜃 (0) ∈ Θ◦ on the canonical-aligned approach to a singular minimum 𝜃 ∗ ∈ Σ. Suppose Theorem 2 and Corollary 78 apply (canonical alignment, theorem-compatible loss, asymptotic regime). Then the projected trajectory 

¯𝜃 (𝑡 ) = 𝜋 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) ∈ ¯Θ satisfies  

> 𝑢 ⊤
