along any horizontal direction 𝑢 ∈ 𝑇 ¯𝜃 (𝑡 ) ¯Θ, where 𝑘 is the KL order at the singular minimum and 

¯𝑡 is the parametric distance along the horizontal section. Proof. Proposition 83 establishes that the DDCAdam update map is 𝐺 -equivariant for the sup-ported gauge classes. Equivariance makes the projection 𝜋 well-defined along the trajectory: the optimiser preserves the orbit foliation, so ¯𝜃 (𝑡 ) = 𝜋 (𝜃 (𝑡 )) is well-defined dynamics on ¯Θ (a preconditioned flow: the horizontal frame still carries a per-coordinate preconditioner, so it is not the gradient flow of the SGD case). The rate statement is geometric, not dynamical: Theorem 2 and Corollary 78 fix 𝑢 ⊤ ¯𝐹𝑢 as a function of position along the canonical-aligned horizontal approach with KL order 𝑘 , independent of the dynamics that traverse it. Since the equivariant trajectory stays on ¯Θ and approaches the singular minimum along that hor-izontal direction, the position-dependent rate Θ(¯𝑡 2(𝑘 −1) ) is what is read off it. (This is the same reasoning as the SGD corollary 79, but without claiming gradient flow: equivariance buys orbit-foliation preservation, which is all the rate readout needs.) Vertical-mode choice (frozen , sgd , adam ) does not change the horizontal-direction rate because the vertical update enters the gauge orbit (vertical subspace) and is filtered out by the projection 𝜋 . □

Remark 87 (Constructive recovery of the rate readout on the gauge case (DDCAdam)) .

Remark 80 flagged two routes for non-SGD preconditioners: a closed-form rate modifier Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 89 

for standard (non-equivariant) Adam, which is open, and a constructive 𝐺 -equivariant al-ternative. Algorithm 2, Proposition 83, and Corollary 86 realise the constructive route for the architectural gauge classes of §7: an Adam-family preconditioner that satisfies Corol-lary 78’s sufficient condition exists, with explicit gauge-spec formulas (its 𝐺 -equivariance proved for both gauge types; Proposition 83). Standard Adam’s per-coord normalisation is replaced by an orbit-collapsed vertical update plus per-coordinate Adam on a 𝐺 -invariant horizontal frame; under DDCAdam the trajectory rate 𝑢 ⊤ ¯𝐹𝑢 𝑡 = Θ(¯𝑡 2(𝑘 −1) ) is preserved on the projected trajectory, joining SGD as a theorem-compatible optimiser for trajectory rate-fits. Equivariance also bears on measurement reliability (Remark 4): by keeping the optimiser on the gauge orbit it removes the gauge-mode drift that rotates the eigenvector lineage under non-equivariant preconditioners, one of the confounds that breaks a learned-trajectory rate readout. The asymptotic-window and well-specified-model preconditions still apply; the gauge-drift failure mode is what equivariance closes. This is a constructive closure on the gauge case (CE row-shift, ReLU rescale, LayerNorm scale) and does not address the non-canonical-regime case (alignment-rotation under precon-ditioned dynamics on architectures where canonical preservation of the dead direction is not automatic), which remains open in the form noted at the end of Remark 80. 

Remark 88 (Practical scope) . DDCAdam is a constructive specialisation: it requires a gauge spec for each architectural symmetry one wishes to handle. Architectures with multiple coexisting gauges (e.g. a transformer with CE row-shift on the unembed plus LayerNorm scale on each residual block plus ReLU rescale on each MLP block) require a multi-gauge composition (Algorithm 2’s multi-block composition), which is well-defined only for block-disjoint gauges. Architectures with non-trivially interacting gauges, or with gauges not in the catalogue (e.g. attention-head permutation as a discrete symmetry), require either a new gauge spec or fall outside this corollary’s scope. Empirical validation covers the canonical-bridge testbed with ReLU rescale plus the multi-direction 𝐿 = 4 noisy bridge with rank deficit  

> 𝑟

= 2.Part V Scope and extensions 

# 12 Applicability conditions 

The trajectory-rate predictions hold under five conditions, named here to make each theorem’s scope precise and to mark where the framework’s reach ends. None is a hidden assumption; each appears in a theorem hypothesis or its proof. 

Canonical alignment. The dead direction must be the same coordinate at every layer of a deep network for the per-layer K-FAC rate ladder 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ) to hold cleanly. When the dead direction at successive layers is a rotation of the canonical frame, the ladder gets rotated; the global rate 2(𝐿 − 1) is preserved but per-layer readout is no longer transparent. Random initialisation typically violates canonical alignment; implicit-bias arguments suggest partial self-alignment along training under SGD, though no clean theorem establishes it. 

Optimizer family. The quotient theorem (Corollary 78) requires the optimizer to be 𝐺 -equivariant for the group 𝐺 of gauge invariances. SGD on a 𝐺 -invariant Riemannian metric is equivariant. Adam is not (Section 11). K-FAC and Shampoo are partially equivariant under structured groups; the trajectory-rate predictions hold for them in restricted regimes but the closed-form analysis is not yet complete. Muon’s Newton-Schulz orthogonalisation is a further non-SGD preconditioner with no derived trajectory rate. 

Noise level. Theorem 2 is stated in the noisy regime where the irreducible empirical loss 

𝜎 2 > 0 saturates the rate’s leading coefficient. In the noise-free regime 𝜎 2 = 0, every per-layer rate exponent shifts by +2𝐿 (Remark 23; e.g. slope 6 instead of 2 at 𝐿 = 2, ℓ = 1 on 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ)

vs 𝜎 min (𝑊 ℓ); slope 10 instead of 4 at 𝐿 = 3, ℓ = 1). The shift is consistent across the framework and is itself a quantitative prediction; the rate exponent therefore depends on which regime one measures in. 

Asymptotic regime. The theorem describes the leading non-vanishing Taylor coefficient of 

𝑢 ⊤ 𝐹𝑢 as 𝑡 → 0. At finite 𝑡 , subleading corrections of order 𝑡 2𝑘 (and higher) are present. On a trained network, the trajectory typically does not reach the strict 𝑡 → 0 limit; the rate read at finite 𝑡 is the asymptotic exponent plus a finite-𝑡 Taylor correction whose magnitude scales with 𝑡 . We treat the rate as a leading-order statement; finite-𝑡 deviations are small in the canonical-aligned, asymptotic regime our theorems target. 

Observable and estimator. The rate is a property of the population Fisher 𝐹 = E𝑝 ∗ [𝑠𝑠 ⊤]

on the controlled parametric approach. The object a pipeline actually computes is the loss-gradient covariance (the K-FAC G-factor), which coincides with the population Fisher only 90 Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 91 

at a well-specified configuration (Remark 4). On a controlled freeze-probe the two agree and the rate is recovered cleanly. On a learned trajectory the loss-gradient prefactor ∥𝛿 ∥2

confounds the readout: it collapses the whole spectrum at a well-fit optimum and stalls below the descent window otherwise. The trajectory-level rate is therefore reliable only inside the asymptotic window, for a well-specified model with the eigenvector lineage preserved. Whether a learned run produces a rate to read at all is a prior, dynamical question, taken up in §12.1. Beyond these five pivotal conditions, the rate predictions interact with several further axes (depth 𝐿 , layer index ℓ, initialisation balance, loss family, sampling regime, data distribution, normalisation class, singularity type, transient vs asymptotic measurement window), but each interaction is a parameter sweep within the pivotal frame and adds no independent dimension to the theory. The one dimension that is not such a sweep is the training phase itself: whether the learned dynamics are accumulating representational magnitude or compressing toward the singular structure decides whether a rate is present to read, and §12.1 takes it up. 

Practical use at scale. Large-scale trained networks satisfy the five conditions above only approximately: canonical alignment is rarely preserved exactly, optimisers are typically Adam-class (and increasingly Muon), the trajectory does not reach the strict 𝑡 → 0 regime, the noise level is set by data rather than by an idealised 𝜎 2, and the observable a pipeline computes is the loss-gradient covariance rather than the population Fisher. On such networks the framework consequently serves as a reference frame rather than an exact predictor. The rate primitive (Theorem 2) specifies what a canonical-aligned trajectory would exhibit, and any quantity derived from the bridge (the per-layer K-FAC ladder, the residual-stream 𝜎 min , the LN kernel direction, and future derived observables) inherits a definable target under the same reference frame. The deviation between observed and predicted at each checkpoint is itself a rate-grounded measurement. Section 15 takes up the diagnostic use of these residuals as an open empirical direction. 

Reach by tier. Each load-bearing result of the paper falls into one of three reach tiers, summarised in Table 6. The universal tier holds for any analytic algebraic-statistical model with smooth singular fibres, with no architectural specifics; the architecture-specific tier requires a particular network structure (layered with K-FAC factorisation, residual DAG, particular normalisation, etc.); the trajectory-readability tier requires the optimiser to be equivariant under the gauge group, separating the metric-level statement from the trajectory-level rate readout. 

12.1 Accumulation and compression regimes 

The five conditions above concern a given trajectory. A prior question is whether a training run produces a trajectory of the kind the theorem describes. Theorem 2 fixes the geometry of an approach to the singular set as 𝑡 → 0, but on a learned run 𝑡 is whatever the optimiser does to the weights, and the optimiser need not be moving toward Σ𝑇 . Two phases arise, and only one of them is a singular approach. In the accumulation phase the network fits the data by growing representational magnitude: weight norms and activation scales rise, the parameters move through the bulk of the space, and the smallest Fisher eigenvalue along a would-be-dead direction grows. There is no descent to read, because the trajectory is not Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 92 

Table 6: Reach of the paper’s load-bearing results, by tier. Universal results hold for any analytic algebraic-statistical model with smooth singular fibres and do not require architectural specifics. Architecture-specific results require a particular structure (layered K-FAC, residual DAG, etc.). Trajectory-readability results separate the metric-level statement (universal) from the trajectory-level rate readout (requires an equivariant optimiser). 

Result Tier Conditions Theorem 2 (Fisher rate decay) Universal Smooth singular fibre + score-expansion reg-ularity Theorem 3 (Selection rule, RLCT recov-ery) Universal Smooth fibre with transversal/tangential split Prop 8, Cor 9, Prop 10 (rate chain) Universal Same + curvature genericity Theorem 21 (Multi-layer K-FAC bridge) Architecture-specific Layered net + K-FAC factorisation + canonical alignment Cor 25 (A–G duality) Architecture-specific As Theorem 21 Cor 28 (Kronecker lift of the dead direc-tion) Architecture-specific As Theorem 21; identifies the dead direction from the K-FAC factors Theorem 30 (Composition additivity) Architecture-specific Heterogeneous block stack + scalar-transfer hypothesis Cor 58 ( 𝜎 min depth-invariance) Architecture-specific Residual DAG with exact-identity skips Architectural instantiations (rectangu-lar, multi-direction, CE, residual, non-canonical, biases, LN, attention, SwiGLU) Architecture-specific Each adds one architectural primitive or analysis-side extension Cor 78 (Quotient Fisher rate, metric level) Universal at metric level Continuous Lie group symmetry of the loss Cor 79 (SGD trajectory rate on quotient) Trajectory-readability 𝐺 -equivariant preconditioner; canonical-aligned trajectory Adam non-equivariance (mechanism + scope) Trajectory-readability Adam-class preconditioner; gauge-redundant loss DDCAdam (Alg 2, Cor 86) Trajectory-readability 𝐺 -equivariant preconditioner by construc-tion; gauge classes of §7 Theorem 89 (Multi-component crossing recovery) Universal Multi-component normal crossing; generic-transversal + 𝜀 -anchored rates Theorem 96 (Singular fluctuation 𝜈 ) Universal 1D dead direction; uniform prior 

an approach. In the compression (consolidation) phase, reached when training continues past the fitting point under sustained overtraining or on a task that rewards synthesis over memorisation, the network distils toward the minimal degenerate structure that generalises: the weight norm falls and the dead-direction eigenvalue descends along the approach the theorem describes. The grokking transition is the sharp instance (Power et al., 2022; Nanda et al., 2023): a memorising phase in which the weight norm and 𝜎 min climb gives way to a generalising phase in which the circuit collapses to its minimal form. Watanabe’s account places generalisation at exactly this degenerate structure, where the real log canonical threshold governs the leading free-energy correction, so the compression phase is the one in which the singular structure the rate measures is formed. The phase decides which observable carries signal. The trajectory-rate readings (the 

𝜎 min -descent slope of Theorem 2 and the per-layer K-FAC ladder of Theorem 21) presuppose an approach, so they are informative in the compression phase and empty in accumulation, where there is no descent to fit. The measurement-level conditions above are the estimator-Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 93 

and signal-to-noise shadows of the same split: the loss-gradient prefactor that collapses the spectrum at a well-fit optimum and stalls otherwise (Remark 4), and the asymptotic-window precondition (Remark 11). The static face of the framework is indifferent to the phase: the high-curvature Fisher volume and the effective rank it controls (Section 6) read the configuration at a single point, so they characterise the would-be singular structure in either phase and remain available where the rate reading is not. The order itself does not require the full descending sequence. Theorem 2 is symmetric in 𝑡 , so the leading exponent reads from the outward growth of 𝑢 ⊤ 𝐹𝑢 away from a near-singular configuration along a dead direction identified from the Kronecker-factored Fisher (Corollary 28), with no captured descent. The precondition is then structural rather than dynamical. A parameter-space dead direction is itself a product of compression, so accumulation offers no such configuration to scan, on or off the trajectory, while a single compression checkpoint carries the order once that structure has formed. This regime distinction is a reading of training dynamics grounded in the grokking tran-sition; the rate theorem fixes the geometry of the approach, while which phase a given optimiser and training budget occupy is a dynamical question we do not derive here. In practice, the trajectory-rate predictions are most directly testable in the compression regime, on overtraining and grokking testbeds, whereas a single-pass compute-optimal run sits in the accumulation phase throughout, where the static volume and rank readings are the available signal. 

# 13 Extensions to Watanabe’s broader inventory 

Watanabe’s framework is wider than a single rate exponent. The earlier sections of this paper carry the bridge for a single dead direction with a single KL order 𝑘 , and the consequences when this primitive composes across layers and architectural primitives. This section carries it further into Watanabe’s broader inventory: multi-component normal crossings, multiplicity, the singular fluctuation 𝜈 , prior-induced RLCT shifts, and tempered posteriors at 𝛽 ≠ 1. Each is closed in trajectory-rate form, on the same footing as the single-direction rate of Theorem 2. The genuinely-open items that remain after this section are collected separately in §14.

13.5 Watanabe’s summary triple from a checkpointed trajectory 

Taking stock of the extensions above: Watanabe’s summary triple (𝜆, 𝑚, 𝜈 ) for a singular minimum is recoverable from a checkpointed trajectory without posterior sampling. The local RLCT 𝜆 comes either from a single-direction Fisher rate (Theorem 2, Theorem 3) or from the multi-component generic-transversal protocol (Theorem 89); the multiplicity 𝑚 falls out of the recovered (𝑘 1, . . . , 𝑘 𝑟 ) tuple as |{ 𝑖 : 𝑘 𝑖 = max 𝑗 𝑘 𝑗 }| under uniform prior; the singular fluctuation 𝜈 is the trajectory-side gap observable, universal in the KL order 𝑘 (Theorem 96; its data-free component 𝑉 [√𝑇 ] has a closed form, the full value is numerical). Watanabe’s free-energy expansion 𝐹 𝑛 = 𝑛𝐿 𝑛 + 𝜆 log 𝑛 − ( 𝑚 − 1) log log 𝑛 + 𝑂 𝑝 (1), the WAIC-style estimator 

𝑇 𝑛 + 2𝜈 /𝑛 , and tempered-posterior 𝛽 -sweeps all follow as compositions of these primitives. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 96 

Most practically interesting Bayesian quantities (posterior predictive 𝐸 post [ 𝑝 ( 𝑦 new |𝑥 new , 𝜃 )] ,marginal likelihood, model selection, posterior-WAIC, restricted-LLC component decomposi-tions) are computable from the framework’s trajectory primitives without recourse to posterior sampling. Developmental-stage tracking is the SLT-side reading of training dynamics established by Hoogland et al. (2024), who detect stagewise development in transformers via LLC change-points across checkpoints, with the refined-LLC variant of Wang et al. (2024) attaching the change-points to specific weight subsets. The trajectory-rate framework gives a deterministic alternative: a local Fisher-rate change-point detector that operates on a single checkpoint’s for-ward and backward passes without posterior sampling. The two readings agree on transition timing; they differ in cost (SGLD posterior sampling vs deterministic Fisher rate) and in what they return per stage (Bayesian-complexity number vs rate exponent). The trajectory-side reading is what discriminative cross-variant workflows use.

= 𝛼 1 + 𝛼 2 + 2.4. Read Watanabe’s local RLCT (with uniform prior, ℎ𝑖 = 0) as 𝜆 = min (1/( 2𝑘 1), 1/( 2𝑘 2)) .The protocol requires identifying the component normals 𝑛 1, 𝑛 2 at 𝜃 0. For models with explicit singular fibers (mixture boundaries, rank-deficit fibers in matrix factorisation), these normals are explicit; for models where the component structure is implicit, identifying 𝑛 1, 𝑛 2

is open in general. For a layered network the per-layer K-FAC factors expose a constructive candidate generator (Corollary 28): the smallest-eigenvalue parameter direction of a layer is the Kronecker lift 𝑔 min 𝑎 ⊤

Remark 92 (Scope: Jacobian exponents ℎ𝑖 remain a prior choice) . Theorem 89 recovers the per-component KL orders (𝑘 1, 𝑘 2) but is silent on the Jacobian exponents (ℎ1, ℎ 2) of Watanabe’s RLCT formula 𝜆 = min 𝑖 (ℎ𝑖 + 1)/( 2𝑘 𝑖 ). In Watanabe’s framework, ℎ𝑖 arises as the order of vanishing of the prior measure in resolved coordinates, a property of the chosen prior, not of the model. With the natural Lebesgue prior in original parameter coordinates, 

ℎ𝑖 = 0 and the trajectory framework recovers the full RLCT 𝜆 = min 𝑖 1/( 2𝑘 𝑖 ). Non-zero ℎ𝑖 

requires a non-uniform prior 𝜋 (𝜃 ) ∝ Î𝑗 |⟨ 𝑛 𝑗 , 𝜃 − 𝜃 0⟩| ℎ𝑗 in original coordinates, in which case the volume integral Vol 𝜋 ({ 𝐾 < 𝜀 }) scales as 𝜀 𝜆 with the full 𝜆 = min 𝑖 (ℎ𝑖 + 1)/( 2𝑘 𝑖 ) as an 𝜀 -scan observable rather than a single-trajectory rate. {( 2, 3, 2), (3, 3, 3)} for 𝑟 = 3 on the canonical toy model.

The data average 𝑍 ≠ 0 tilts 𝜋 𝑍 and adds a strictly positive contribution (numerically the dominant part for 𝑘 ≥ 2), so 𝜈 LO (𝑘 ) > 𝜈 0 (𝑘 ). (Dropping the data average leaves only the data-fluctuation-free 𝜈 0 = 𝑉 [√𝑇 ]: a parity argument removes the term linear in 𝑍 , but the quadratic term, the variance of the tilt, survives and is the bulk of 𝜈 LO .) □

Remark 97 (Numerical values) . The regular case 𝑘 = 1 (added as a consistency anchor, outside the 𝑘 ≥ 2 hypothesis) returns the textbook value 𝜈 = 𝜆 = 1/2,which the data-fluctuation-free 𝜈 0 does not reproduce.

Remark 99 (Scope: dead direction only; non-uniform priors; multi-direction) . Theorem 96 is for a single 1D dead direction with uniform prior. Three explicit boundaries: 

• Regular directions (𝑘 = 1, 𝑎 1 ≠ 0 in 𝐿 2): 𝜈 LO is continuous with the regular case, returning 𝜈 = 𝜆 = 𝑑 /2 (= 1/2 in 1D), the standard realizable-Fisher value (Watanabe, 2018, Theorem 6). The data-fluctuation-free 𝜈 0 does not: it gives 1/2 − 1/𝜋 ≈ 0.182 ,which is one way to see that 𝜈 0 cannot be the singular fluctuation. 

• Non-uniform priors : a prior 𝜋 (𝜃 ) ∝ |⟨ 𝑢, 𝜃 − 𝜃 0⟩| ℎ shifts the renormalised shape parameter to 𝜆 + ℎ/( 2𝑘 ); the universality argument extends verbatim, with the data-averaged functional variance computed at the shifted shape (and its data-free component obtained by the same 𝜆 ↦ → 𝜆 + ℎ/( 2𝑘 ) substitution in 𝜈 0). • Multi-direction : along multi-component normal crossings (Theorem 89), the renor-malised posterior factorises into per-component pieces under the additive transversal normal form; the universality argument extends per-component, and the aggregate 𝜈 is

# 14 What remains open 

The closures in §13 carry the bridge across the bulk of Watanabe’s broader inventory. The remaining items are the following. None is, as far as we can see, structurally blocked; each is a research target. 

Hironaka resolution at 𝑟 > 3. Theorem 89’s joint-recovery protocol is established for 𝑟 = 2

and extended along the same lines to 𝑟 = 3 via Remark 95. For 𝑟 > 3 the multiplicative Hironaka normal form 𝐾 = 𝑢 (𝑔 ) Î𝑟 𝑖 =1 𝑔 2𝑘 𝑖  

> 𝑖

would need partial re-engagement of the resolu-tion machinery: the generic-transversal trajectory rate 𝛼 gen = 2(Í𝑖 𝑘 𝑖 − 1) still holds, but disambiguating individual 𝑘 𝑖 from the rate sum alone requires 𝑟 − 1 independent 𝜀 -anchored single-component rates, and choosing the 𝜀 -anchor scaling so the higher-component cross-terms remain subleading becomes delicate as 𝑟 grows. 

Trajectory-rate analog of 𝜈 in original coordinates. Theorem 96 establishes that 𝜈 LO (𝑘 )

is universal (a function of the KL order alone) and reports it numerically; only its data-fluctuation-free component 𝜈 0 (𝑘 ) = 𝑉 [√𝑇 ] = 𝜆 − ( Γ(𝜆 + 1/2)/ Γ(𝜆 )) 2 has an elementary closed form, and a closed form for the full 𝜈 LO (with the empirical-fluctuation average included) is itself open. A complementary observable would specify how the gen-train gap scales along an approach as a function of 𝜎 min in original parameter coordinates, anchored at the bridge framework’s predicted exponent 𝜆 . This is a distinct construction from the rate observables for 

𝜆 surfaced throughout the present work: it requires both a training-side and a population-side loss reading along the trajectory. Promoting it to a real-network observable is straightforward in principle (track training and validation loss alongside the existing Fisher diagnostics) but requires the Watanabe-side integral on the resolved fiber to be closed for the bridge framework’s K-FAC-decomposed Fisher, which we leave for follow-on work. 

Theoretical 𝛽 -sweep of (𝜆, 𝑚 ) at 𝛽 ≠ 1. The operational 𝛽 -rescaling of the gen-train gap (§13.4) lets the trajectory side track tempered posteriors. What the present theorems do not yet address is the theoretical 𝛽 -sweep of the RLCT and multiplicity themselves: under WBIC’s 𝛽 = 1/log 𝑛 tempering, the leading constants in Watanabe’s free-energy expansion are 𝛽 -rescaled but the qualitative structure (𝜆, 𝑚 ) is preserved. A precise statement of how the trajectory rate exponent shifts under 𝛽 ≠ 1, beyond the convention-preserving rescaling, remains open. 

Non-i.i.d. data. Watanabe’s later work extends the free-energy formula to Markov processes and time series. The trajectory-rate framework’s score expansion uses i.i.d. samples; the 102 Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 103 

extension to non-i.i.d. data (sequence-data SLT) has not yet been addressed and is a natural follow-on direction. 

Posterior-sampling tasks with arbitrary 𝑓 (𝜃 ). Bayesian quantities of the form Epost [ 𝑓 (𝜃 )] 

for an arbitrary 𝑓 that was not observed per checkpoint are structurally outside what a deterministic-trajectory framework can reach: the integrand cannot be re-evaluated from stored trajectory data alone. SGLD-based posterior sampling remains the native tool. The boundary is methodological: the trajectory rate covers everything readable from a per-checkpoint deterministic observable, and arbitrary- 𝑓 posterior expectations belong to the stochastic-sampling side. 

Amari-side connections not yet drawn on. The dual (∇ , ∇∗) connections of information geometry interact with the gauge-quotient picture of §10 in ways the present framework has not exploited. The exponential / mixture flatness duality of standard families has not yet been used to characterise which families are “simple” (single-direction, low KL order) versus “complex” (multi-component) from the rate viewpoint. Both are natural directions for follow-on theory. 

Closed-form rate modifiers for non-equivariant preconditioners. Section 11’s construc-tive resolution provides a 𝐺 -equivariant Adam-family preconditioner that satisfies the quotient theorem’s sufficient condition. A closed-form per-trajectory rate modifier on the alignment-rotated manifold for standard non-equivariant Adam remains open, as it does for the other non-SGD preconditioners (K-FAC, Shampoo, Muon), none of which has a derived trajectory rate. Pesme et al. (2021) give the analogous analysis for diagonal linear networks under SGD. 

Trajectory-rate exponents for normalisations beyond LayerNorm. The mean-subtraction dichotomy of Proposition 63 fixes which production normalisations carry an algebraic kernel direction, but the trajectory-rate exponent under BatchNorm, GroupNorm, and the RMSNorm variants is not derived. 

# 15 Discussion 

What the bridge gives each tradition. Singular learning theory gains a rate axis: ques-tions about how fast a singularity is approached, under what dynamics, and with what optimiser-specific corrections become well-posed at the trajectory level rather than only at the asymptotic-posterior level. Theorem 3 makes the single-direction contribution 1/( 2𝑘 )

to the local RLCT recoverable from a Fisher rate exponent on smooth singular fibres under the transversality genericity condition (G), in original parameter coordinates rather than in resolved coordinates. Theorem 89 extends the recovery to the multi-component normal-crossing form 𝐾 = 𝑢 (𝑔 ) Î𝑖 𝑔 2𝑘 𝑖  

> 𝑖

, giving the joint (𝑘 1, . . . , 𝑘 𝑟 ) tuple from trajectory rates; the Jacobian exponents ℎ𝑖 in Watanabe’s full RLCT enter as an explicit prior choice. Theorem 96 carries the bridge to the singular fluctuation 𝜈 , the second summary invariant of singular generalisation, as a universal function of the KL order for 1D dead directions with uniform prior (its data-fluctuation-free component has the closed form 𝜆 − ( Γ(𝜆 + 1/2)/ Γ(𝜆 )) 2; the full value is numerical). Information geometry gains an extension to its degeneracy locus: Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 104 

dead directions are objects in Amari’s language, with rates that are properties of the Fisher metric in a regime where the metric itself ceases to be invertible; the A–G duality is stated entirely in Kronecker-factored Fisher language; and the multi-layer K-FAC bridge extends the natural-gradient lineage to the singular regime where natural gradient is not defined. 

Position within the broader SLT-DL landscape. The trajectory-rate framework sits among a small cluster of recent programs operationalising different aspects of singular structure in deep networks. Each one starts from Watanabe (2009) but goes in its own direction; we name each and indicate the relationship to the present framework. The local learning coefficient (Lau et al., 2025; Wang et al., 2024; Hoogland et al., 2024) estimates Watanabe’s  

> 𝜆

numerically via SGLD posterior sampling around a checkpoint, with a refined variant attaching the estimate to specific weight subsets and a stagewise reading tracking 𝜆 across training. The trajectory-rate framework is the deterministic, K-FAC-block-local, sampling-free alternative: same 𝜆 , different operationalisation. The susceptibility programme (Baker et al., 2025; Gordon et al., 2026) probes the response of the loss landscape to localised perturbations of network behaviour, clustering directions by their susceptibility signature; the trajectory-rate framework provides a per-K-FAC-block rate that susceptibilities can be conditioned on. The 

loss kernel of Adam et al. (2025) is a geometric probe of model internals (the covariance of per-sample losses under low-loss-preserving perturbations), with the Hessian-free Bayesian influence functions of Kreer et al. (2025); Lee et al. (2025) extending it to data attribution sensitive to higher-order degeneracy; these are geometric companions to the leading-rate readout. Compressibility and minimum description length (Urdshals et al., 2025) formalise the bit-length of a singular minimum and recover MDL in the regular case; the trajectory-rate framework gives the rate exponent per direction, which compressibility integrates over. 

Programs as singularities (Murfet and Troiani, 2025) connects degenerate statistical models to the geometry of programs; the trajectory rate is one observable on the singular-program manifold this line constructs. Modes of sequence models (Chen and Murfet, 2025) characterises the sensitivity of LLC estimation to input-distribution patterns; the trajectory-rate framework’s expected-Fisher protocol (§9.1) is the corresponding sensitivity statement on the rate side. None of these lines is a competitor; each probes a slice of the singular geometry the present framework also touches, and each provides a falsifier for the trajectory-rate readings on architectures where the lines overlap. 

Forward-looking directions the bridge unlocks. The theorems above give concrete lever-age on several practical problems. We phrase each as an open question that the framework provides the analytic target for, rather than as a result the framework promises. 

Compression and rank reduction. Is the Fisher rate exponent the rate-correct subspace for LoRA placement, structured pruning, and low-rank fine-tuning? The framework provides the analytic criterion (dead-direction definition 𝑢 ⊤ 𝐹𝑢 → 0) and a finer signal than rank-deficiency (the rate exponent). The compressibility-MDL line of Urdshals et al. (2025) pursues a related direction from the LLC side; whether the per-direction rate exponent gives a better subspace-selection criterion than the standard activation-variance or empirical-Fisher heuristics is an open empirical question. 

Continual-learning safe-direction constraints. Methods that constrain updates to directions leaving the current task’s loss unchanged to leading order (orthogonal-gradient, null-space) Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 105 

currently identify these directions empirically. Is the dead-direction definition, a unit vector along which 𝑢 ⊤ 𝐹𝑢 → 0, the analytic target these methods are approximating? Falsifying this requires comparing the empirically-selected safe directions to the framework’s predicted dead directions on a continual-learning benchmark. 

Equivariant optimizer design under gauge invariance. What does an equivariant precondi-tioner look like for each architectural gauge group? The quotient theorem (Corollary 78) speci-fies the design constraint; DDCAdam (Algorithm 2) constructs it for three classes (CE row-shift, ReLU rescale, LN scale). Extensions to attention-head permutation, RoPE phase symmetry, and other architectural gauges are open. DePavia et al. (2025) study how parameter-space rotations affect Adam’s implicit bias. 

Architectural primitives as rate-shaping choices. Each per-architecture lemma is a structural fact about how a designer’s choice of block class shapes the singular geometry. Residual DAGs preserve rate 0 on the residual stream. LayerNorm has an algebraic kernel direction 

> 𝛾 −1

/∥ 𝛾 −1 ∥ at the post-norm position; RMSNorm has no such universal kernel. Attention chains break composition additivity at depth ≥ 4. The open question for architecture search is whether the rate profile under canonical alignment is itself a useful objective: whether tuning a network’s design to make the rate ladder shallower or steeper changes generalisation in a predictable way. 

Mechanistic interpretability. Sparse-autoencoder feature directions and dead directions intersect: are features that are also dead the features the network has implicitly compressed or dropped, and does ablating them leave the loss unchanged to leading order? Superposition in transformer residual streams has a structural reason: Corollary 58 gives the geometric capacity that supports superposition feasibility. The LayerNorm kernel direction is a deterministic probe direction available in every LN-equipped model, with the algebraic guarantee cov (LN (𝑋 )) 𝑣 ∗ =

0 for any input distribution. Whether the per-K-FAC-block rate exponent and the LN kernel direction combine into a useful circuit-level diagnostic, one that flags which sub-circuit a feature lives in by its rate signature, is an open empirical question. The trajectory-rate readouts are complementary to other geometric probes in the recent SLT-DL literature: per-attention-head specialisation via the refined LLC (Wang et al., 2024), the loss-kernel probe of Adam et al. (2025), and the sequence-mode analysis of Chen and Murfet (2025); each operationalises a different aspect of singular structure, and the trajectory rate adds the deterministic, sampling-free per-layer reading to that roster. 

Trajectory-rate residuals as training diagnostics. The rate primitive of Theorem 2 is the foundational construct of the framework; the per-layer K-FAC ladder, residual-stream 𝜎 min ,LN kernel direction, and any further bridge-derived observable inherit a rate prediction under canonical alignment. Real networks at scale do not satisfy the conditions under which those predicted exponents are exactly realised. The framework’s role on such networks is a reference frame: the deviation between observed and predicted at any checkpoint is a per-layer, per-trajectory, rate-grounded observable, computable for any observable the bridge defines now or in the future. Whether the deviation profile across training predicts downstream quality (final benchmark scores, generalisation behaviour, pathology signatures) is an open empirical question the framework reduces to a well-defined measurement. The contribution here is the reference-frame structure itself: current and future bridge-derived observables inherit a definable deviation against an idealised target, independent of which observable is being read. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 106 

Diagnostic instrumentation for pretraining and fine-tuning. Are the activation-side 𝜎 min (𝑋 ℓ)

and gradient-side 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) readouts useful as structural-health indicators during large training runs? Both are consequences of the framework, computable from forward and backward passes at any checkpoint, and rate-grounded by Theorems 21 and Corollary 58. Whether the readings catch training pathologies (loss spikes, dead-channel emergence, gauge-mode drift under Adam) before downstream metrics do is open, a specific instance of the residual-as-diagnostic framing above. 

Algebraic-baseline vs trained singular structure. A concrete instance of the residual-as-observable framing. Lemma 18 pins down the leading constant of the activation-Gram Schur complement in the canonical setup, yielding the width-only invariant 𝑅 (ℎ) := 𝜆 min ( 𝐴 ℓ)/( 𝐴 ℓ)𝑢,𝑢 

at the framework’s predicted dead direction. Reading this on a LayerNorm-equipped pre-trained model at random initialisation, with 𝑢 = 𝛾 −1/∥ 𝛾 −1 ∥ from Proposition 63, gives 𝑅 ≈ 1

within finite-sample drift; on the trained checkpoint, 𝑅 (𝛾 −1) collapses, indicating that training opens additional dead directions below the algebraic baseline. Is the gap between random-init and trained 𝑅 (𝛾 −1) a useful per-checkpoint observable, separating architectural-algebraic null structure (fixed by the LN parameter alone) from training-induced SLT-singular structure (the dead directions the optimiser opens as it consolidates, in the compression regime of §12.1)? The observable is forward-pass-only, computable per block, with no gradient access required, and, being static, reads the same in either regime; its empirical utility across architectures is open. 

Closing. This paper makes Watanabe’s KL-order invariant trajectory-readable in original parameter coordinates, and shows that a single rate exponent organises the observable roster of a deep network: per-layer K-FAC factors, residual-stream 𝜎 min , the LayerNorm kernel direction, attention-chain composition, and the Fisher-Riemannian log det + volume identity. The same exponent carries Watanabe’s broader inventory (multi-component crossings, multi-plicity, singular fluctuation, prior shifts, and tempered posteriors; Section 13), and Section 14 sets out what remains genuinely open. Underneath all of it is one object: the dead direction, the unit vector that is at once Amari’s kernel-approaching direction of the Fisher metric and Watanabe’s tangent to the singular set, read by the same KL order. That shared invariant is the bridge, and it is why every result here is legible from both sides at once.

The appendices collect material deferred from the body: the architectural rate catalogue, holding the per-primitive instantiations that carry the canonical ladder without new phenom-ena, the non-canonical boundary, and the LayerNorm finite-𝑡 crossover (Appendix A); and the controlled parametric validations that anchor the rate predictions on small testbeds where every condition is independently controllable (Appendix B). # A Architectural rate catalogue 

This catalogue collects the architectural instantiations summarised in Table 3 but deferred from Section 9: the primitives that carry the canonical rate ladder through composition without introducing new behaviour, the non-canonical boundary where the ladder ceases to hold, and the finite-𝑡 crossover of the LayerNorm rate. Each is stated with the same structure used in the body, a per-block KL order feeding the composition theorem (Section 8) with its singular-geometry consequence, and each predicted rate is the one checked by the freeze-probe of Appendix B.2 and plotted in Figure 9. 

A.1 Rectangular widths 

Theorem 21 and its cross-entropy companion Theorem 36 assume square hidden dimensions 

ℎℓ = ℎ for all ℓ. Real networks have per-layer widths that differ: embeddings ( 𝑑 vocab → 𝑑 model ), MLP up/down projections ( 𝑑 → 4𝑑 → 𝑑 ), classifier heads ( 𝑑 → 𝐶 ). This subsection extends the bridge theorem to rectangular widths via a reduction to the square case restricted to a nested chain of narrow subspaces. The only new work is a rectangular variant of the matrix-product singular-value lemma; the backward-chain argument of Theorem 21 transfers directly. 

Setup. Let 𝑊 ℓ ∈ Rℎℓ ×ℎℓ−1 for ℓ = 1, . . . , 𝐿 , with the widths ℎ0, ℎ 1, . . . , ℎ 𝐿 arbitrary positive integers. Let ℎ∗ := min ℓ ℎℓ be the narrowest slot. Since the composed map 𝑀 = 𝑊 𝐿 · · · 𝑊 1 has rank at most ℎ∗, the natural “rank-deficient-by-one” singular configuration has rank (𝑀 ∗) =

ℎ∗ − 1. In this regime the dead direction lives in an ℎ∗-dimensional chain of subspaces that threads through the whole network. Concretely, an isometry chain is a sequence of linear isometries 𝜄 ℓ : Rℎ∗ ↩→ Rℎℓ for ℓ =

Assumption 100 rules out the pathological case where the orthogonal complement carries additional hidden rank deficiencies that would compete with the narrow-chain rate. It is a generic condition: a random linear map between complements of compatible dimensions satisfies it with probability one.

Corollary 104 (𝜆 min statement under complement genericity) . Under the additional hypothesis Assumption 100, the non-narrow eigenvalues of 𝐺 ℓ (𝜃 (𝑡 )) are Θ(1) in 𝑡 , so the smallest eigenvalue equals the dead-direction entry at leading order:

By Lemma 17 (Schur-form integral reduction: the dead-direction Schur complement at the dead row equals the dead-direction entry at leading order, since the non-dead block is ≻ 0 at Θ(1) and the cross-row entries do not exceed the Schur-cancellation order), the dead-direction eigenvalue of 𝐺 ℓ is Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ) in the isometry-adapted basis. The isometry-chain extension preserves the dead-row / non-dead-block structure of the parent lemma since the chain is a direct-sum extension of the canonical narrow block with a non-degenerate complement (the isometry projection Π acts as identity on the dead-direction coordinate and rotates the non-dead complement). Under Assumption 100, the complement block has all Θ(1) eigenvalues; combined with the narrow block’s ℎ∗ − 1 non-dead eigenvalues at 

Θ(1) and the dead-direction Schur-eigenvalue at Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ), the minimum is attained at the dead-direction eigenvalue for 𝑡 small enough, yielding the stated 𝜆 min equality. □Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 115 

Joint scope. The combination of rectangular widths with cross-entropy is closed (Re-mark 38); the formal joint statement covers MLP classifiers and Papyan-style neural-collapse setups without residuals. The residual extension (Theorem 43, §9.2) adds transformer FFN blocks, classifier heads attached through residuals, and ResNet-style CNNs to the in-scope list. 

Beyond Approach A. Theorem 103 uses Approach A: dead-direction rank-star ℎ∗ at most 

min ℓ ℎℓ, with an isometry chain threading through every layer along a shared narrow subspace of dimension ℎ∗. A more general treatment allowing the rank-star to exceed ℎ∗ at some layer (the dead direction maps to a lower-dimensional image at a bottleneck), or admitting different dead directions per layer (rather than a shared chain), is open. Approach A covers the architectural slice where every layer carries the same dead-direction signature; the open generalisations are needed when bottlenecks force the dead direction to compose with rank-1 projections inside the chain, or when the dead direction realigns layer by layer (the multi-direction chunk §A.2 handles the case of multiple parallel dead directions but assumes consistent indexing across layers, which is the same shared-chain structure). 

A.2 Multi-direction singularities and asymmetric approaches 

All previous bridge theorems assume a single dead direction perturbed at uniform rate 𝑡 

across all layers. Real singularities exhibit higher rank deficit (multiple dead directions) and the approach trajectory may scale different layers’ perturbations with different powers of the same scalar parameter 𝑡 (i.e., layer ℓ is perturbed by 𝑡 𝑝 ℓ for layer-dependent 𝑝 ℓ, not by separate time variables). The structural classification of multiple dead directions in MLP architectures via the neural-network rank of a parameter (the minimum hidden width needed to represent 𝑓 𝑤 ) is due to Farrugia-Roberts (2022, 2024); the present subsection extends that count-of-degenerate-directions framing to a rate per direction in the K-FAC G-factor. This subsection unifies three generalizations: 

• Arbitrary rank deficit : the singular configuration has rank (𝑊 ∗ 

> ℓ

) deficient by 𝑚 ≥ 1 rather than just 1, giving 𝑚 dead directions. 

• Non-symmetric approach : each weight is perturbed by 𝑡 𝑝 ℓ with possibly layer-varying exponent 𝑝 ℓ ≥ 1, not just 𝑡 1.

• Per-direction KL orders : the 𝑚 dead directions may have different per-layer exponent patterns 𝑝 (𝑖 ) 

> ℓ

, giving each direction its own effective KL order. These fold into a single multi-index bookkeeping. Each dead direction 𝑖 contributes its own eigenvalue to 𝐺 ℓ’s spectrum at its own rate 2 Íℓ′ >ℓ 𝑝 (𝑖 ) 

> ℓ′

> ℓ

The operational selection rule of Appendix 5.1 identifies rate-carrying eigenvalues by matching measured 𝛼 𝑖 to predicted values; under this theorem, multiple matches at a single component are expected when 𝑚 ≥ 2 and correspond to distinct dead directions, rather than being measurement noise. The ordering of which rate is "direction 1" vs "direction 2" is a labeling choice, not intrinsic. 

Remark 110 (Static-Fisher rate vs. trajectory observability for multiple directions) . Theo-rem 106 is a static-Fisher statement on the parametric trajectory. Multiple dead directions amplify the Adam non-equivariance documented in Remark 80: each direction independently couples to gauge-mode drift, so per-direction trajectory rate readout under standard Adam is doubly fragile. Validation of the per-direction rates accordingly uses a parametric freeze-probe under a theorem-compatible setup, not Adam-trajectory rate-fitting. The constructive closure for the gauge case (Corollary 86) lifts to multi-direction: under DDCAdam, the per-direction rates {2Π(𝑖 ) 

> ℓ

}𝑖 are recoverable from a real training trajectory in the same regime as under SGD, since the gauge-mode coupling that causes the doubly-fragile failure mode under Adam is removed by Proposition 83’s equivariance. 

Remark 111 (Joint scope with rectangular, cross-entropy, and biases) . The multi-direction extension touches only the dead-direction sub-block of 𝐺 ℓ (each direction independently contributing a Schur complement of the form analysed above), and is therefore disjoint from the rectangular-widths reduction (which acts on the non-dead block), the CE output-head base-case replacement (Lemma 33), and the bias-augmented Schur structure (each direction  

> 𝑖

carries its own perturbation 𝑏 (𝑖 ) 

> ℓ

(𝑡 ) = 𝑡 𝑞 (𝑖 )  

> ℓ𝑢 (𝑖 )
> ℓ

). The joint statements are sketched rather than separately proved; the disjoint-block structure of the multi-direction Schur reduction is the load-bearing reason each composition holds. 

A.3 Biases 

Previous bridge theorems state the forward pass as ℎℓ = 𝜙 (𝑊 ℓ ℎℓ−1), without biases. In practice every weight layer has an accompanying bias vector: ℎℓ = 𝜙 (𝑊 ℓ ℎℓ−1 + 𝑏 ℓ). This subsection extends the bridge theorem to cover biases, showing that under the natural singular-configuration convention 𝑏 ∗ 

> ℓ

= 0, biases do not alter the rate structure; their sole effect under smooth activations is a predictable finite-𝑡 Taylor correction. 

Setup. Let the singular configuration be 𝜃 ∗ = ({ 𝑊 ∗ 

> ℓ

}, {𝑏 ∗ 

> ℓ

= 0}) , with the 𝑊 ∗ 

> ℓ

structure inherited from the relevant base setup (single- or multi-direction, square or rectangular). The natural choice 𝑏 ∗ 

> ℓ

= 0 is justified by the rank-deficiency-of-the-Jacobian interpretation of “singular configuration”: biases do not affect the rank of the forward Jacobian, so their natural singular-configuration value is any value; setting them to 0 is the canonical choice (see Remark 113). For concreteness we state the single-direction symmetric case explicitly; the extension to multi-direction and non-symmetric approaches (Theorem 106) proceeds analogously Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 120 

by adding independent bias perturbations per dead direction. The dead-aligned approach perturbs both weights and biases in the dead direction:

Remark 113 (Natural singular-configuration choice for biases) . Setting 𝑏 ∗ 

> ℓ

= 0 at the singular configuration is a convention, not a restriction. At any non-zero 𝑏 ∗ 

> ℓ

that is not aligned with the dead direction, the analysis proceeds identically: only the dead-coordinate of the bias contributes to the rate calculation, and we can shift to 𝑏 ∗ 

> dead

= 0 by redefining the zero-point of the dead coordinate (which is WLOG under the canonical basis). Non-zero biases in non-dead coordinates are Θ(1) contributions that are absorbed into the Θ(1) non-dead block of 𝐺 ℓ and do not affect the dead-direction eigenvalue. 

Empirical validation. Direct validation at 𝐿 = 4, ℎ = 6, MSE, across three cases and three activations: 

• Case A (weight-only, 𝑏 ≡ 0): exact standard-theorem rates across linear, ReLU, GeLU, confirming biases-at-zero are inert. 

• Case B (bias-only, weights fixed at 𝑊 ∗, 𝑞 ∈ { 1, 2}): measured rate is 0 at every layer, every activation, confirming bias perturbations alone do not create a rate-carrying dead direction (part (c) of the theorem). 

• Case C (joint perturbation): 

– Linear, ReLU: exact match with the weight-only prediction (part (a) with 𝑟 bias  

> ℓ

= 0). 

– GeLU, 𝑞 ℓ = 1: measured 𝛼 1 = 7.64 , 𝛼 2 = 5.13 , 𝛼 3 = 2.57 (compared to the no-bias GeLU values 6.48 , 4.12 , 2.02 ). The additional slope-shift from the bias, on top of the standard smooth-activation 𝑟 𝐺 ℓ term, is approximately +1 per layer at our 

> 𝑡

-range [10 −1.2, 10 −0.3]. Scope and composition with prior extensions. Theorem 112 is stated and proved for the square-hidden, feedforward, single-direction, symmetric-approach setting. The bias column of ˜𝑊 ℓ = [𝑊 ℓ | 𝑏 ℓ] does not enter the backward chain at the dead direction (the bias column couples dead output to constant-1, not dead input), so the bias-augmented Schur reduction is disjoint from the rectangular non-dead-block reduction (Theorem 103, where the narrow chain does not include the 1-coordinate), the CE output-head replacement (Lemma 33, which leaves the backward chain unchanged), the residual-DAG path-distance (Theorem 43, where 𝐾 (ℓ) counts weight edges only), and the multi-direction per-direction Schur reduction (Theorem 106, where each direction 𝑖 carries its own perturbation 𝑏 (𝑖 )

the joint statements as natural follow-ups, not “mechanical applications,” because closing each requires verifying the disjointness formally, which we have done above only for the square-hidden single-direction MSE base case. 

A.4 SwiGLU 

This subsection states the per-block forward rate for the SwiGLU MLP variant used in modern decoder-only LLM families. The result is a direct instantiation of the bridge framework on the SwiGLU forward map, not an extension of the bridge theorem itself.

Remark 116 (Practical application to modern LLM architectures) . Modern decoder-only LLMs widely use SwiGLU MLPs in place of the standard fc1-activation-fc2 form. Use 𝑘 fwd SwiGLU = 3

(Proposition 114) in place of the standard 𝑘 fwd MLP = 2 when applying Theorem 30 to these architectures. 

A.5 Non-canonical alignment 

All prior bridge theorems assume the dead direction 𝑢 ℓ is aligned with a canonical basis vector at every layer. Trained networks rarely satisfy this exactly: dead directions emerge during training at rotations of the canonical frame, and we want to know what survives. The answer divides cleanly along the activation axis. Under linear activation, rotation is without loss of generality, and Theorem 21 transfers verbatim. Under nonlinear activation, canonical alignment is essential: arbitrary rotation collapses the rate to model-dependent values that have no clean closed form. We give the positive result for linear activation, the negative result for nonlinear, and a sketch of why nonlinearity breaks the rotation invariance. We restrict throughout to square hidden ( ℎℓ = ℎ for all ℓ); the rectangular extension is orthogonal and addressed below. Notation. The rotation parameter 𝛼 rot ∈ [ 0, 1] measures how far the dead direction is rotated away from the canonical basis: 𝛼 rot = 0 corresponds to canonical alignment ( 𝑢 ℓ = 𝑒 ℎ), and 𝛼 rot = 1 to a uniformly random rotation of the dead direction. 

Remark 117 (Genericity of non-canonical alignment) . The set of unit vectors 𝑢 ∈ Rℎ aligned with a coordinate basis vector has measure zero in the unit sphere 𝑆 ℎ−1, so canonical alignment is a measure-zero condition. For randomly-initialised networks under SGD with smooth or ReLU activations, dead directions emerging during training generically lie at non-zero rotation 

𝛼 rot from any coordinate basis. The negative result of Proposition 119 therefore applies generically, motivating an intermediate spectral-identification step (an operational selection rule that recovers the dead direction from the measured spectrum) at trained-network scale. 

Proposition 118 (Linear activation: rotation is without loss of generality) . Under activation class (P1) (linear, identity) and the square-hidden setup ( ℎℓ = ℎ for all ℓ), Theorem 21 holds for arbitrary dead-direction unit vectors 𝑢 with 𝑢 ℓ = 𝑈𝑢 0 for a fixed orthogonal 𝑈 ∈ 𝑂 (ℎ) applied consistently at every layer (here 𝑢 0 ∈ Rℎ denotes the input-space dead direction). The rate 

(𝐺 ℓ)𝑢𝑢 = Θ(𝑡 2(𝐿 −ℓ) ) is preserved exactly. Proof. Change of basis: let 𝑈 ∈ 𝑂 (ℎ) be an orthogonal matrix sending 𝑒 ℎ to 𝑢 0. Define rotated weights ˜𝑊 ℓ = 𝑈 ⊤𝑊 ℓ𝑈 (the same 𝑈 used at each layer’s input and output, valid because the square-hidden hypothesis means input and output ambient spaces both have dimension ℎ). Under this rotation, the singular configuration 𝑊 ∗ 

> ℓ

= 𝑈 · diag (1, . . . , 1, 0) · 𝑈 ⊤

(a symmetric matrix conjugate to diag (1, . . . , 1, 0) via 𝑈 on both sides, valid because the dead-direction perturbation 𝑡 · 𝑢𝑢 ⊤ is symmetric and the same orthogonal applies on the input and output sides) becomes ˜𝑊 ∗ 

> ℓ

= diag (1, . . . , 1, 0) (canonically aligned), and the perturbation 

𝑡 · 𝑢𝑢 ⊤ becomes 𝑡 · 𝑒 ℎ𝑒 ⊤ 

> ℎ

. Linearity preserves this equivalence: the forward pass ℎℓ = 𝑊 ℓ ℎℓ−1

transforms to ˜ℎℓ = ˜𝑊 ℓ ˜ℎℓ−1 with ˜ℎ = 𝑈 ⊤ℎ, and the backward chain transforms analogously. Theorem 21 applies to the rotated system, and the rate prediction transfers back. Validated Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 125 

on the parametric rotated-dead-direction sweep: linear activation matches the canonical rate exactly at arbitrary rotation amounts 𝛼 rot ∈ [ 0, 1]. □

Proposition 119 (Nonlinear activation: canonical alignment is essential, empirical demonstra-tion) . Under activation classes (P2) smooth (e.g., GeLU, tanh) and (P3) ReLU, the dead-direction rate is not preserved under arbitrary rotation. The canonical-basis rate exponent 2(𝐿 −ℓ) is not re-alised, and the measured exponent at finite 𝛼 rot takes activation-, rotation-, and depth-dependent values that may be substantially smaller than the canonical prediction (e.g., at 𝛼 rot = 0.1, 𝐿 = 4, 

> ℎ

= 6, the deepest-layer rates are < 1 vs the canonical 6). We present this as an empirical demonstration that canonical alignment is essential, not a fully analytical proof; the analytical mechanism is sketched below and a rigorous lemma stating a quantitative rate bound under controlled rotation is left as future work. Mechanism sketch. The rate prediction derives from the canonical-basis block structure of  

> 𝑊 ∗
> ℓ

, which makes the dead-direction backward propagation factor cleanly (Lemma 16). Elementwise activations 𝜙 commute with rotation only through the null rotation (identity). For a non-canonical dead direction 𝑢 = 𝑈𝑒 ℎ with 𝑈 non-diagonal, each backward Jacobian factor 𝜙 ′ (𝑎 ℓ) ⊙ · mixes the dead-direction-along-𝑢 with components of 𝑎 ℓ that have non-vanishing 𝜙 ′; these mixed contributions enter at Θ(1) and dominate the backward delta’s dead-direction projection. A rigorous bound on the resulting rate as a function of 𝛼 rot would require explicit accounting of how the elementwise nonlinearity couples canonical and rotated coordinates, which we have not done. □

Empirical demonstration. At 𝐿 = 4, ℎ = 6, with rotation amount 𝛼 rot ∈{0, 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 1.0}:

• Linear : rate preserved exactly at all 𝛼 rot (Proposition 118). 

• ReLU : at 𝛼 rot = 0, rate is (6, 4, 2, 0) (canonical); at 𝛼 rot = 0.1, rate collapses to 

(0.05 , 0.37 , 0.92 , 0); at 𝛼 rot = 1.0, rate is (0.18 , 0.29 , 0.18 , 0).

• GeLU : similar collapse, 𝛼 rot = 0.1 gives (0.19 , 0.37 , 1.44 , 0); 𝛼 rot = 1.0 gives 

(0.31 , 0.30 , 0.61 , 0).This demonstrates by counterexample that the theorem’s rate prediction does not hold under arbitrary rotation in the nonlinear cases. 

Corollary 120 (Near-canonical continuity, open) . Whether a quantitative rate bound is recoverable under small rotations (dead direction 𝑢 = 𝑈𝑒 ℎ with ∥𝑈 − 𝐼 ∥ ≤ 𝜖 ) for nonlinear activations remains open. The rotated-dead-direction sweep shows the rate degrades rapidly as  

> 𝛼 rot

→ 0+, suggesting any such bound would need to be highly 𝜖 -dependent and may not be polynomial in 𝑡 .

Practical takeaway. In a trained network the dead direction is not expected to align with any natural coordinate basis (Remark 117); the operational selection rule recovers it from the measured spectrum. Across the observable hierarchy: 𝜎 min -based identification is the cheapest tier (real-time cadence, no projection needed); 𝑢 ⊤𝐺𝑢 probing requires identifying 𝑢 

first (offline tier, via the selection rule); 𝜆 min (𝐺 ℓ) is intermediate (periodic cadence, 𝑛 /𝑑 ≥ 100 Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 126 

samples); the full Fisher spectrum is the most informative but offline-only at hidden widths 

ℎ ≳ 10 4. The non-canonical case here is one source of why direct dead-direction projection requires an intermediate identification step, not a categorical exclusion of any tier. 

Remark 121 (Scope: near-canonical continuity) . Proposition 119 establishes that canonical alignment is essential in the nonlinear cases. Corollary 120 flags the near-canonical continuity question as open: whether a quantitative rate bound is recoverable under small rotations 

∥𝑈 − 𝐼 ∥ ≤ 𝜖 for nonlinear activations. The orthogonal scope items (rectangular-widths approach boundary, attention-softmax extensions) are addressed elsewhere in the framework, not here. 

A.6 LayerNorm finite-𝑡 crossover 

The LayerNorm rate bound of Section 9.3 is an asymptotic ( 𝑡 → 0) statement; at the finite 𝑡 

where SGD trajectories live, the measured rate sits in a crossover region. The following gives its closed form on the LN-equipped MLP block. 

A.6.1 LayerNorm finite-𝑡 rate-shift: closed form for the MLP case

Conjecture 123 (LN finite-𝑡 rate shift, attention components) . For a softmax-attention block at canonical singular approach with Gaussian-isotropic input, post-LN at the block output, and the same noise-target setup as Theorem 122, the directional Fisher at component 𝑐 ∈{𝑊 𝑄 , 𝑊 𝐾 , 𝑊 𝑉 , 𝑊 𝑂 } has a finite-𝑡 expansion of the same three-regime form, with effective LN-crossing count 

𝐾 eff  

> 𝑐

= 𝐾 nominal  

> 𝑐

− 𝑐 softmax (𝑑, 𝑁 seq ) · 𝐾 softmax -rank (𝑐 ),

where 𝐾 softmax -rank (𝑐 ) ∈ { 0, 1} counts the number of times 𝑐 ’s backward gradient passes through softmax in the rank-1-averaging direction (specifically, 𝑊 𝑉 , whose gradient passes through 𝐴 ⊤ as a per-position averaging, has 𝐾 softmax -rank = 1; 𝑊 𝑂 , whose gradient passes through softmax in the orthogonal direction, has 𝐾 softmax -rank = 0), and 𝑐 softmax (𝑑, 𝑁 seq ) is a softmax-Jacobian-rank coefficient ∈ ( 0, 1) to be determined empirically per-architecture. 

Where Conjecture 123 is open. Closing the conjecture into a theorem requires: 1. exact composition of the channel-space projector 𝑃 (from LN) with the sequence-space projector 𝑃 seq (from softmax) acting on the dead direction 𝑒 ℎ ⊗ 1𝑁 seq : the projectors act on different factors of the 𝑑 × 𝑁 seq tensor, so their composition is mathematically straightforward but produces a Gaussian expectation in the joint space that has not been computed; 2. subleading 𝑡 -corrections to 𝐴 (𝑡 ) from 𝑄 -and-𝐾 -coupled deviations off canonical isotropy, contributing additional 𝑡 2-class terms whose coefficients depend on 𝑑 ℎ;3. a specific value of 𝑐 softmax (𝑑, 𝑁 seq ) derived from items (1)–(2), to compare against the empirical ≈ 0.4 at 𝑑 ≥ 8.These are tractable analytical computations of the same flavor as the MLP derivation; they are deferred to future analytical work and do not affect the structural prediction of the conjecture. 

Practical predictive use. On RMSNorm architectures (LLaMA, Qwen, Gemma), no analo-gous finite-𝑡 rate-shift mechanism exists: RMSNorm’s forward map has trivial null space (no mean-subtraction projector), so no equivalent of the 𝑂 (1) leak in (2) arises. The no-LN feed-forward predictions 𝛼 𝑐 = 2𝐾 (𝑐 ) apply in any fitting window. On LN architectures, predictions at scale 𝑡 depend on where 𝑡 sits relative to the crossover scales: 

• For static checkpoint observables (𝜎 min on the residual stream, the LN-kernel direc-tion 𝛾 −1/∥ 𝛾 −1 ∥): the rate-theorem trajectory is irrelevant. Predictions are protocol-independent and apply regardless of the fitting-window regime. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 130 

• For trajectory-rate observables (𝑢 ⊤𝐺𝑢 slope on a learned trajectory, parametric freeze-probe slope): the local log-slope at the trajectory’s terminal 𝑡 is given by Theorem 122’s rational-function form, and the integrated slope across a fit window is computable from the closed form. Predictions at new architectures should either measure the local slope at the target 𝑡 window or fall back to the architecturally-invariant 𝜎 min residual-stream observable. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 131 

# B Parametric validations 

This appendix collects the controlled parametric experiments that anchor the bridge theorem (Theorem 21) on small testbeds where every condition (canonical alignment, dead-direction perturbation amplitude, optimiser preconditioner, asymptotic versus transient regime) is independently controllable. Each subsection isolates one face of the rate prediction: the per-layer rate ladder on a two-layer autoencoder (§B.1), the per-primitive architectural freeze-probes that validate the lemmas of §9 (§B.2), the trajectory-rate readout under three preconditioner-knob ablations (§B.3), and the compatibility-boundary sweep across the architectural axis (§B.4). The TMS canonical configuration (§7.2) and the deep-linear reduced-rank regression illustration (§5.2), already presented in the body, complement the appendix-bound material below.

Probe protocol. Gaussian inputs 𝑥 ∼ N ( 0, 𝐼 𝑑 in ), identity reconstruction target 𝑦 true = 𝑥 .We compute 𝐺 hidden and 𝐺 input at the perturbed configuration 𝜃 (𝑡 ) via gradient capture, at a logarithmic grid of 𝑡 values in [10 −4, 3 × 10 −1] (asymptotic regime) or [10 −4, 2] (extended regime, used for the subleading-correction analysis). All computation is in fp64 for numerical precision near the singular point. 

Seeds and environment. 5 seeds per activation class: {42, 142, 242, 342, 442}. CPU-only execution, ∼30s per (seed, activation) configuration. Activation classes: linear, ReLU, GELU (smooth), tanh (smooth). The parametric freeze-probe is a deterministic-flow measurement at controlled 𝑡 -values: the only stochasticity is in the random initialisation seed, and the post-flow 𝜎 min readings cluster at 3-decimal precision in every cell of Table 7. Cross-seed std is reported in the table; for every entry the 5-seed std is at least an order of magnitude smaller than the gap to the nearest competing prediction (e.g., 𝜎 min slope 0 vs 1, or 𝑢 ⊤𝐺𝑢 

slope 0 vs 2), so the test is gap-not-noise-limited and the seed budget is sufficient. The TMS canonical configuration (§7.2) is a cross-architecture parametric extension at 30 seeds for the cleanest single-activation slope.

B.2 Architectural freeze-probe validations 

Each per-primitive lemma of §9 predicts a dead-direction rate that the architecture preserves, reshapes, or breaks. We check these predictions with a static parametric freeze-probe . At the canonical singular configuration 𝜃 ∗ we set each weight to 𝑊 ℓ (𝑡 ) = 𝑊 ∗

ℓ + 𝑡 𝑢 ℓ𝑢 ⊤

ℓ−1 along the symmetric approach, sweep 𝑡 over a fixed logarithmic grid, and read the dead-direction entry 𝑢 ⊤

ℓ 𝐺 ℓ (𝜃 (𝑡 )) 𝑢 ℓ by projecting onto the known dead direction 𝑢 ℓ at each 𝑡 . The measured rate is the log–log slope of that entry against 𝑡 in the asymptotic window, computed in fp64. The probe sits on the theorem’s own trajectory rather than a learned optimiser trajectory (Remark 23), so it isolates the architectural effect from optimiser dynamics. Rate-reshaping and rate-breaking primitives. Table 10 collects the primitives that change the rate, and each change matches the corresponding lemma. The rectangular widths leave the transversal rate at its square-case value across 64 width-by-depth configurations ( 4 width profiles × 𝐿 ∈ { 2, . . . , 5} × 4 activations, 672 per-layer measurements). A residual skip cuts the in-block weight-branch rate from the no-skip ladder (6, 4, 2, 0) to the shortest-weighted-path ladder (2, 0, 2, 0) (Corollary 48), while at the residual stream the dead-direction entry stops decaying altogether (rate 0), the depth-invariance of Corollary 58. A rotation off the canonical basis preserves the linear rate but collapses the nonlinear one (Proposition 119); LayerNorm reduces the integer ladder to the fractional bound-gap of Theorem 60. The cross-entropy estimator contrast. The cross-entropy probe makes the estimator scope of Remark 4 concrete. On the same static trajectory, the expected -Fisher G-factor recovers the 

(4, 2, 0) ladder to three decimals (Table 9), while the empirical -Fisher G-factor, measured at the observed labels, loses the rate signal once the configuration memorises the data: its dead-direction entry collapses toward the numerical floor and admits no clean slope. This is the freeze-probe realisation of Corollary 39, and it is why rate-fitting on trained classifiers uses the expected Fisher. 

Attention. The single-head attention component rates accompany the attention extension (Theorem 66, §9.4), whose standalone rate is the one open primitive; the component-isolation freeze-probes and the head-count sweep are reported there. 

B.3 Controlled parametric ablations across three optimizer designs 

This appendix presents controlled parametric ablations across three optimizer designs, isolat-ing each parametric knob on canonical (bridge testbed) and reach-of-singular-set (diagonal-LN) regimes. The three drivers, ParametricEpsFloor (interpolates SGD → Adam-like via 

> 𝜀

-floor), HomogeneityDial (interpolates SGD → sign-descent via update homogeneity 𝛽 ), and MomentumSign (interpolates pure-sign → sign-of-EMA via momentum 𝛽 1), each isolate a distinct optimizer-axis knob without confounds from the other two. Two findings emerge: (i) in the canonical regime, all three drivers reproduce the slope-2 bridge prediction to four-decimal accuracy, so the slope reads as a geometric property of the canonical-aligned configuration that the preconditioner choice leaves invariant; (ii) on the diagonal-LN reach observable, 𝜀 -floor reaches the singular set on a positive fraction of inactive coordinates while 

HomogeneityDial and MomentumSign do not, localising the reach mechanism to the 𝑣 ˆ-init transient. Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 136

Cell C: HomogeneityDial (Design A) sweep. The second driver HomogeneityDial applies the pointwise preconditioner update 𝑖 = −𝜂 · 𝑔 𝑖 · (| 𝑔 𝑖 | + 𝛿 )−𝛽 (with 𝛿 = 10 −12 a numerical floor only); 𝛽 = 0 is SGD, 𝛽 = 1 is sign descent, intermediate values are partial-sign updates. This isolates preconditioner homogeneity degree as a distinct knob from the 𝜀 -floor of cell A. On the canonical-aligned bridge testbed (single seed, exact mode), the late-third slope is 2.0000 across 𝛽 ∈ { 0, 0.25 , 0.5, 0.75 , 1.0} at R 2 ≥ 0.99 , with the 𝜎 min span growing from 

1.31 OOMs at SGD to 7.35 OOMs at sign descent: different traversal speeds along the same asymptotic curve. On the diagonal LN ( 3 seeds), the depth-of-reach into the singular set is 0% 

at every 𝛽 (the smallest 𝑟 min (inactive ) is 1.43 · 10 −2 at 𝛽 = 0 and 6.78 · 10 −2 at 𝛽 = 1, never reaching exact zero). Together with cell B’s 6.7–8.9% reach under ParametricEpsFloor at small 𝜀 , the singular-set-reach effect pins to the 𝑣 ˆ-init transient of 𝜀 -floor (where 𝑣 ˆ(0) = 0Dead Directions: Geometric Singular Learning 138 

produces an early-step 1/√𝜀 amplification of small gradients); pointwise nonlinearity of the preconditioner does not produce the same reach. 

Cell D: MomentumSign (Design C) sweep. The third driver applies update = −𝜂 · sign (𝛽 1𝑚 +(1 − 𝛽 1)𝑔 ), no second moment, no bias correction; 𝛽 1 = 0 is pure sign descent, 𝛽 1 = 0.99 is sign of long-window EMA. This isolates momentum-of-sign as a distinct knob. Canonical-aligned bridge testbed: at 𝛽 1 = 0.95 (the cleanest asymptotic in the step budget), the slope is 2.00 at R 2 = 1.0000 , confirming the canonical-regime geometry-lock under a third independent parametric family. On the diagonal LN ( 3 seeds), depth-of-reach is 0% at every 𝛽 1 ∈ { 0, 0.5, 0.9, 0.95 , 0.99 }, consistent with cell C and contrasted with cell B, further pinning the reach effect to the 𝑣 ˆ-init transient of ParametricEpsFloor .

Synthesis. Three independent parametric families confirm the canonical-regime slope-2

lock under deterministic gradient flow (cells A, C, D): the slope is a geometric property of the canonical-aligned configuration, invariant under the three preconditioner-family knobs swept here. On the depth-of-reach observable (cells B, C, D), only 𝜀 -floor reaches the singular set, localising the mechanism to the 𝑣 ˆ-init transient. The complementary axis-12 question, closed-form per-trajectory rate predictions for non-equivariant preconditioners on regimes where canonical alignment is not preserved, is the program’s open analytical target. 

B.4 Compatibility-boundary sweep 

The compatibility-boundary summary in the discussion of regime applicability (§12) is extracted from a 22-variation compatibility-boundary sweep covering: activation ∈ {linear, ReLU, GELU, tanh}; optimizer ∈ {SGD, SGD+momentum, Adam, AdamW, Shampoo, Muon, K-FAC}; normalisation ∈ {none, LN, RMSNorm}; loss ∈ {MSE, CE}; residual structure ∈ {none, per-layer, per-block}; depth 𝐿 ∈ {2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 20, 32}; init scale 𝜀 ∈ {5 · 10 −3, 5 · 10 −2,

5 · 10 −1}. Each variation uses 10 seeds at the canonical configuration (linear MLP, 𝐿 = 3,

= 4, 𝜎 = 0.1). Below we report a selected slice of the sweep that exhibits each regime named in §12; representative numbers are quoted, with per-cell standard deviations under
